the internal vertices of
P
traversed from
u
in order
such that each of them is a vertex of degree at least
3
in
G^{i,j}
.
For all
q\in[\lvert F\rvert+1]
,
we define the unique tree
T(q)

as the tree in
G^{i,j}-E(P)
containing
x_{q}
.
From
3.3
, we know that
\mathcal{C}

contains at most
|F|
distinct
G^{\prime}
-paths.
Hence, there exists some
q_{0}\in[\lvert F\rvert+1]

such that
T(q_{0})\setminus\{x_{q_{0}}\}
does not intersect
\mathcal{C}
.
Moreover, since
x_{q_{0}}
is a vertex of degree at least
3
in
G^{i,j}
,
tree
T(q_{0})
contains at least one vertex from
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
.

Now we distinguish two cases
depending on how many vertices from
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
appear in
T(q_{0})
.

One vertex from
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
appears in
T(q_{0})
.

Formally, we have that
|V(T(q_{0}))\cap(N_{i}\cup N_{j})|=1
.
Let
w
be this vertex, that is,
w
is the leaf of
T(q)
.

In this case
T(q_{0})
is a path in
G^{i,j}

and let
P^{\prime}
be the unique
(u,w)
-path in
G^{i,j}
(and thus in
G^{\prime}
).
By assumption about
u
and
v
,
we get that
\operatorname{dist}_{\geq 3}^{i,j}(u,w)\leq|F|+1

and
P^{\prime}
is a usable path.

In conclusion we get that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}\setminus P)\cup P^{\prime}

is also a cycle packing of size
\ell
in
G
.

Multiple vertices from
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
appear in
T(q_{0})
.

In this case we formally get that

|V(T(q_{0}))\cap(N_{i}\cup N_{j})|\geq 2
.
Let
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
be two distinct vertices in
T(q_{0})

such that they are also contained in
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
.
Moreover, we require that the unique path
P^{\prime}
from
u^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime}

does not contain any other vertex from
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
.
Since
T(q_{0})
is either a path or a tree with at least two leaves
and, by definition of
G^{i,j}
, all leaves belong to
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
,
we can choose
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
such that
\operatorname{dist}_{\geq 3}^{i,j}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leq 1
.

We get that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}\setminus P)\cup P^{\prime}
is also a cycle packing
of size
\ell
in
G

or
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
contains a cycle packing of size
\ell

(after removing the path from
i
to
j
)
in case
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in N_{i}
or
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in N_{j}

(note that a path starting from
i
and ending with
i
is also a cycle).

In both of the above cases,
we get a cycle packing of size
\ell

with strictly larger number of usable
G^{\prime}
-paths.
This contradicts the choice of
\mathcal{C}

and thus, completes the proof for the lemma.
∎

Rich Pairs and a Bound for the Number of Usable Paths.

For all
i,j\in F
, we bound the number of usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths
that are hit by a fixed vertex
v\in G^{\prime}
by some value
\Gamma

or prove that this pair is rich.
We say that a pair
(i,j)\in F\times F
is a
rich pair

if, for any cycle packing
\mathcal{C}
of
G
,
there are at least
k+1
vertex-disjoint
(N_{i}\cup N_{j},N_{i}\cup N_{j})
-paths
in
G^{\prime}
that are disjoint from
\mathcal{C}
.

We show that, if a pair
(i,j)
is not rich,
then
\Gamma
is the maximum number of usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths
that can be hit by any fixed vertex,
where we define
\Gamma
as

\Gamma\coloneqq\left(\Upsilon(2|F|+k)(|F|+3)\right)^{2}.

(3.2)

This bound is the key property to obtain a small depth of the branching tree
of the branching procedure that we describe later. See
Figure

3.1
for an illustration of
Lemma

3.8
.

v
i
j
i
i
i
i
j
i
j
j
i
j
i

Figure 3.1
:
An illustration from the proof of
Lemma

3.8
.
The four highlighted paths at the bottom
belong to the assumed maximal collection
\mathcal{P}
.
For convenience, we use
i
and
j
to denote the neighbors of vertex
i,j\in F
respectively.
The nodes with diamond shape belong to
A=\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}A_{P}
,
and after removing these nodes,
there is one component which has more than one leaf (appearing in the box).
Then, we can add the highlighted path in this box to
\mathcal{P}

and thus, strictly increase the size of
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
G
be a graph
of low degree with regard to
\Upsilon
.
For all
v\in V(G^{\prime})
and
i,j\in F
, either
(i,j)
is a rich pair,
or the number of usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths that are hit by
v
,
i.e., intersected by vertex
v
, is at most
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Fix some vertex
v\in V(G^{\prime})
, some
i,j\in F

and let
T_{v}
be the tree of
G^{i,j}
that contains
v
.
We define
T
as a maximal subtree of
T_{v}
containing
v

such that every
(N_{i},N_{j})
-path contained in
T
is usable
and every leaf of
T
is in
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
.
We root
T
at
v
.
Observe that the vertices of
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
appear only at the leaves of
T
,
as otherwise some
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths in
T
would not be minimal (and thus not usable).

From the definition of usable paths,
it follows that every usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-path in
G^{\prime}
that is hit by
v

has its endpoints in the set
(N_{i}\cup N_{j})\cap V(T)
,
which is precisely the set of leaves of
T
.
Hence, the number of usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths hit by
v

is at most
|(N_{i}\cup N_{j})\cap V(T)|^{2}
.

Based on this, our new goal is to prove that either
(i,j)
is a rich pair,
or the number of leaves of
T
is at most

\sqrt{\Gamma}=\Upsilon(2\lvert F\rvert+k)(\lvert F\rvert+3)
.

We say that two vertices of
T
are
incomparable
if neither vertex is a descendant of the other one in
T
.
For a path
P
in
T
, we define
\textnormal{{lca}}(P)
as the lowest common ancestor
of the two endpoints of
P
in
T
.
Consider a maximal collection
\mathcal{P}

of pairwise disjoint
((N_{i}\cup N_{j}),(N_{i}\cup N_{j}))
-paths in
T

such that the following two conditions hold:

•

For two distinct paths
P_{1},P_{2}\in\mathcal{P}
,

\textnormal{{lca}}(P_{1})
is incomparable with
\textnormal{{lca}}(P_{2})
.

•

For every path
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
there is no other
((N_{i}\cup N_{j}),(N_{i}\cup N_{j}))
-path
P^{\prime}
in
T

such that
\textnormal{{lca}}(P^{\prime})

is a descendant of
\textnormal{{lca}}(P)
.

Observe that any path in
G^{\prime}
can
intersect at most two paths in
\mathcal{P}
.
In particular,
since any cycle packing
\mathcal{C}
of
G
has at most
|F|
different
G^{\prime}
-paths,
at most
2|F|
paths in
\mathcal{P}
can intersect
\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore, if
|\mathcal{P}|\geq 2|F|+k+1
,
then
(i,j)
is a rich pair.
For the remainder of the proof we assume that
|\mathcal{P}|\leq 2|F|+k
.
Recall that we aim to bound the number of leaves of
T
.

For each
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
we additionally define the set
A_{P}

as the set of vertices on the unique path from
\textnormal{{lca}}(P)
to
v

that have degree at least three in
G^{i,j}
.
We directly get that
|A_{P}|\leq\operatorname{dist}_{\geq 3}^{i,j}(v,\textnormal{{lca}}(P))+2\leq|F|+3
,
since all
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths in
T
are usable.

We set
A=\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}A_{P}

and get
\lvert A\rvert\leq(2|F|+k)(|F|+3)
as
|\mathcal{P}|\leq 2|F|+k
.
As a next step we define the graph
T^{\prime}=T\setminus A
.
Since
T
is connected and
\deg_{G^{\prime}}(u)\leq\Upsilon
for every
u\in V(G^{\prime})
,
the graph
T^{\prime}
has at most
\Upsilon(2|F|+k)(|F|+3)
components.
In particular, every connected component of
T^{\prime}

contains at most one vertex of
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
.
Suppose for contradiction that one component of
T^{\prime}

has at least two vertices of
N_{i}\cup N_{j}
.
Then in this component we can find an
((N_{i}\cup N_{j}),(N_{i}\cup N_{j}))
-path
\widehat{P}
satisfying that (i)
\textnormal{{lca}}(\widehat{P})
does not belong to
A
;
(ii) there is no other
((N_{i}\cup N_{j}),(N_{i}\cup N_{j}))
-path
P^{\prime}
in
T

such that
\textnormal{{lca}}(P^{\prime})
is descendant of
\textnormal{{lca}}(\widehat{P})
.
Condition (i) implies that for any
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
\textnormal{{lca}}(P)
is incomparable with
\textnormal{{lca}}(\widehat{P})
.
Then we could strictly increase the size of
\mathcal{P}
,
contradicting the maximality of
\mathcal{P}
.

Therefore, the number of vertices of
(N_{i}\cup N_{j})\cap V(T)
,
i.e., the number of leaves of
T
, is at most
\Upsilon(2|F|+k)(|F|+3)
.
∎

Step 4: The Branching Procedure.

For a given instance

(G,U,F,k,\ell)
, we now design a branching procedure, which we call
Get-Candidates
. This procedure
generates
2^{\poly(|F|+k)}
candidate instances of a different (relatively easier) problem.
We design these instances such that
(i) it is enough to look for a solution for these candidate instances, and
(ii) each of these candidate instances can be solved in time
2^{k}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

We first present an informal description of the branching procedure.
First,
observe that if the input instance is a
Yes
-instance
and
\mathcal{C}
is a cycle-packing of size
\ell
,
then every solution, i.e., set of vertices that are deleted,
hits at least one of the
G^{\prime}
-paths of
\mathcal{C}
.
The branching procedure stores two complementary sets
\mathbf{avail}
and
\mathbf{hit}

of
G^{\prime}
-paths
such that it first finds a usable cycle packing
\mathcal{C}
of size
\ell

in
G
which only uses paths in
\mathbf{avail}
and then branches on

which
particular
G^{\prime}
-path of
\mathcal{C}
is hit by the solution,
that is, which path is added to
\mathbf{hit}
and removed from
\mathbf{avail}
.
Note that unlike typical branching algorithms,
this procedure does not branch on
the choice of a fixed vertex into the solution.
Instead, in one branch, it
considers a set of vertices (namely a
G^{\prime}
-path) that is guaranteed to be hit
by the solution.

We first describe the procedure to find a usable cycle packing
which uses paths from a specified set only.
For this we make use of the subroutine in
Section

3
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
H
be a forest
H
on
n
vertices
and
\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{f}
be
f
collections of paths of
H
.
In time
2^{\mathcal{O}(f)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
we can find
f
pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
P_{1},\dots,P_{f}

such that, for each
i\in[f]
,
it holds that
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}

whenever such paths exist or correctly answer
No
otherwise.

Note that the notation used in
Section

3

is independent of the notation of the algorithm that we have built so far.
As the proof of this lemma
uses standard bottom-up dynamic programming over rooted trees,
we defer the proof to
Section

3.1
.

With this subroutine,
we can design the first ingredient of the branching procedure.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
be an instance of
Cycle
-HitPack and let
\mathbf{avail}
be some set of
G^{\prime}
-paths.
In time
2^{\mathcal{O}(|F|\log|F|)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

we can find a usable cycle packing
\mathcal{C}
of size
\ell
in
G

whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are contained in
\mathbf{avail}
.

Proof.

Our goal is to use
Section

3
to find disjoint paths
in the graph
G^{\prime}
that can be extended
to a usable cycle packing of size
\ell
in
G
.
As input of the algorithm in
Section

3

we use
f
collections of
(N_{i_{1}},N_{j_{1}})
-paths,
…,
(N_{i_{f}},N_{j_{f}})
-paths
where
(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{f},j_{f})
are distinct pairs of
F\times F
.
However, these pairs cannot be chosen arbitrarily
as we must be able to extend the paths to a cycle packing.
To find these pairs, we start with some preprocessing.

Observe that a cycle packing
\mathcal{C}

naturally induces a collection of such pairs from
F\times F

based on the vertices from
F
that appear in each cycle.
But it can also happen that vertices in
F
appear in only one pair
because they are connected to some other vertex in
F
by an edge.
Based on these observations we show how to compute all possible sets
of pairs that have to be considered.

We first consider all possible permutations
\pi
of
F
.
Note that this does not provide any information about the cycles
and how the vertices in
F
are connected.
To resolve this, we introduce four special symbols

\to_{\mathsf{edge}}
,
\to_{\mathsf{path}}
,
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{edge}}
, and
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{path}}

and insert one of them after each position in the permutation
\pi
.
The permutation is then a string
\pi^{\prime}
of length
2\lvert F\rvert
.
The first two symbols, i.e.,
\to_{\mathsf{edge}}
and
\to_{\mathsf{path}}
,
indicate how two consecutive vertices in the permutation are connected,
either by an edge or by a path in
G^{\prime}
.
The other two symbols, i.e.,
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{edge}}
and
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{path}}
,
indicate that the cycle is closed by an edge or a path in
G^{\prime}

to the first vertex of the cycle,
the previous vertex in
\pi^{\prime}
following a symbol
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{edge}}
or
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{path}}
.

For example the string
1\to_{\mathsf{path}}3\to_{\mathsf{edge}}4\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{path}}5\to_{\mathsf{edge}}2\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{path}}

represents two cycles;
the first cycle starts at
1
and is then connected to
3
by a path in
G^{\prime}
,
vertex
3
has an edge to
4

and from
4
there is a path in
G^{\prime}
back to
1
;
the second cycle starts at
5
and has an edge to
2

and from there a path in
G^{\prime}
back to
5
.

By first choosing the permutation of
F
and then inserting the symbols,
we get that there are at most

\lvert F\rvert!\cdot 4^{\lvert F\rvert}\in 2^{\mathcal{O}(\lvert F\rvert\log\lvert F\rvert)}

possible strings to consider.
For each generated string,
we check for each occurrence of
\to_{\mathsf{edge}}
and
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{edge}}

whether the corresponding edge exists
(for ease of notation we assume in this step
that the vertices in
F
have self-loops).
For each occurrence of
\to_{\mathsf{path}}
and
\curvearrowleft_{\mathsf{path}}
,
we add the corresponding pair
(i,j)
to a list
\mathcal{L}_{\pi^{\prime}}
.
We additionally discard the string if it represents less than
\ell
cycles.

It remains to define the sets
\mathcal{P}_{i,j}
for all
(i,j)\in\mathcal{L}_{\pi^{\prime}}

that serve as input for the algorithm from
Section

3
.
For each
(i,j)\in\mathcal{L}_{\pi^{\prime}}
,
we pick the
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths in the set
\mathbf{avail}

which is given as input.

Now for each collection of sets of candidates
we invoke the algorithm from
Section

3
.
If the algorithm outputs a set of disjoint paths,
we complete it to a usable cycle packing
according to the above preprocessing.
If no set of disjoint paths was found,
we proceed to the next possible set of pairs,
i.e., string
\pi^{\prime}
, and repeat the procedure.

Eventually we find a usable cycle packing of size
\ell
for
G

whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are contained in
\mathbf{avail}

or can conclude that no such packing exists.
∎

Now we have everything ready to state the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
.
Recall that we are working with the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
, where
set
U
contains the undeletable vertices,
set
F
is a feedback vertex set of
G
,
and
F\subseteq U
.

Throughout the following branching procedure,
the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
is fixed
.
We can imagine that each node of the branching tree for
Get-Candidates
has a pair
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
associated with it,
where
\mathbf{avail}

and
\mathbf{hit}
are sub-collections of usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths for some
i,j\in F
.
The set
\mathbf{hit}
corresponds to the collection of
G^{\prime}
-paths
that have been guessed so far to be hit by the solution
and the set
\mathbf{avail}
contains all
G^{\prime}
-paths that can still be used
for a cycle packing.
Therefore we also get
\mathbf{avail}\cap\mathbf{hit}=\varnothing
.

The idea of the branching procedure is that at each node of the branching tree,
one finds a usable cycle packing of size
\ell

with a set of
r
different
G^{\prime}
-paths that are contained in
\mathbf{avail}
.
Then we branch into
r
directions (nodes)
where each of the
r
nodes includes one of the
r

G^{\prime}
-paths
into the set
\mathbf{hit}
in the associated pair
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
.

Now, we present the branching procedure formally.

Lemma 3.10

(
Get-Candidates
)
.

Let
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
be an instance of
Cycle
-HitPack of low degree.
In time
2^{\mathcal{O}((|F|^{2}k\Gamma)\log|F|)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

we can construct a list of candidates
\{\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}_{p}}\}
(for some
p\in 2^{\mathcal{O}((|F|^{2}k\Gamma)\log|F|)}
), where for each
q\in[p]
,
\mathcal{I}_{q}=(\mathbf{avail}_{q},\mathbf{hit}_{q})
,
such that the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
is a
Yes
-instance if and only if
there exists an index
q_{0}\in[p]
and a set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus U

of size at most
k
such that
X
hits all paths in
\mathbf{hit}_{q_{0}}
.

Proof.

We describe the branching procedure
by constructing the labeled branching tree.
For the root node of the branching tree
initialize
\mathbf{avail}
as the set of all usable
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths
in
G^{\prime}
for every
i,j\in F
, and set
\mathbf{hit}=\varnothing
.

At a node labelled
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
,
the
Get-Candidates
procedure performs the following
two steps:

Step 1.

Use
Lemma

3.9
to find a usable cycle packing
\mathcal{C}

of size
\ell
in
G
whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are contained in
\mathbf{avail}
.

Step 2.

Let
\mathcal{P}

be the
G^{\prime}
-paths of
\mathcal{C}
.
Branch into
r\coloneqq\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert
directions,
by creating new child nodes
associated with the label
(\mathbf{avail}\setminus\{P\},\mathbf{hit}\cup\{P\})

for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

First Stopping Criterion.

The branching procedure stops when
G
has no usable cycle packing of size
\ell
whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are totally contained in
\mathbf{avail}
.

Second Stopping Criterion.

The branching procedure also stops
when the depth of the current branch in the branching tree
is strictly more than
|F|^{2}\cdot k\Gamma
.

At the end of the branching procedure, let the
set of
candidates
be
\{\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}_{p}}\}
,
where for each
q\in[p]
,
\mathcal{I}_{q}=(\mathbf{avail}_{q},\mathbf{hit}_{q})
. Let

\mathcal{L}_{+}
be the list of
promising
candidates
, in which there are no usable cycle packing of size
\ell
whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are totally contained in
\mathbf{avail}
,
and let

\mathcal{L}_{-}
, called the
discarded
candidates, be the
remaining candidates.
The branching procedure returns
\mathcal{L}_{+}
as the output.

As a first step we analyze the running time of the branching procedure.

Claim 3.11

(Running time of
Get-Candidates
)
.

The running time of the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
is

2^{\mathcal{O}((|F|^{2}k\Gamma)\log|F|)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Proof of Claim .

First, observe that for any pair
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
that is associated with some node of the branching tree of
Get-Candidates
,
the size of the sets

\mathbf{avail}
and
\mathbf{hit}
is at most
n^{2}
, where
n
is the number of vertices of
the input graph
G
. This is because the sets
\mathbf{avail}
and
\mathbf{hit}
contain
paths of
G^{\prime}
, which is a forest. Since there is a unique path between any
two vertices in a tree, the total number of paths in
G^{\prime}
and hence in

\mathbf{avail}
and
\mathbf{hit}
is at most quadratic in the input size.

By
Lemma

3.9
,
Step 1 of
Get-Candidates
can be executed in time

2^{\mathcal{O}(|F|\log|F|)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Further from the second stopping condition of
Get-Candidates
,
the depth of the branching tree is
|F|^{2}k\Gamma
.
Moreover, the width is at most
\lvert F\rvert

as the algorithm branches into
\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert
directions in Step 2
and, by
3.3
, it holds that

\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert\leq\lvert F\rvert
.

We conclude that the number of nodes of the branching tree of
Get-Candidates
is
2^{\mathcal{O}(|F|^{2}k\Gamma\log|F|)}
.
The running time follows directly.
∎

As a next step we show that the output of the branching procedure
can be used to recover the solution for the original instance.

Claim 3.12
.

If there exists
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})\in\mathcal{L}_{+}

and a set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
of size at most
k

such that
X
hits all paths in
\mathbf{hit}
,
then the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
is a
Yes
-instance.

Proof of Claim .

By the design of the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
,
at least one path of the
G^{\prime}
-paths of every usable cycle packing
of size
\ell
are hit by
X

as otherwise
\mathbf{hit}
would not have been added to
\mathcal{L}_{+}

in the first stopping criterion.
Hence, in
G\setminus X
, there are no usable cycle packings of size
\ell
.
From
Lemma

3.7
,
we know that if
G
has a cycle packing of size
\ell
,
then
G
also has a
usable
cycle packing of size
\ell
.
It follows that in
G\setminus X
there are no cycle packings of size
\ell
.
Since
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
and
|X|\leq k
,
set
X
is a solution to
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
.
∎

It remains to show that a solution for the original instance
propagates to the new instances.

Claim 3.13
.

If the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
is a
Yes
-instance,
then there exists
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})\in\mathcal{L}_{+}

and a set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
of size at most
k

such that
X
hits all paths in
\mathbf{hit}
.

Proof of Claim .

Suppose that
X
is a solution to the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
,
that is, the vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
is of size at most
k
such that
X
hits every
cycle packing of size
\ell
in
G-X
.

To construct the set
\mathbf{hit}
, we analyze the branching procedure.
In every step of
Get-Candidates
where we can find a usable cycle packing
\mathcal{C}
of size
\ell
in
G
,
there is at least one path
P
of the
G^{\prime}
-paths that is hit by
X
.
Assume that this path is an
(N_{i},N_{j})
-path
for some
i,j\in F
.
We claim that we can choose
P
such that
the corresponding
(i,j)
pair is not rich.

Suppose for contradiction that all of the
G^{\prime}
-paths hit by
X

correspond to rich pairs of
F\times F
.
Then by the definition of rich pairs, there are for every cycle packing
at least
k+1
vertex-disjoint
(N_{i},N_{j})
-paths
that are disjoint from the cycle packing.
Hence, we can pick one of these paths that is not hit by
X

and replace the original path by it
to get a new cycle packing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
Thus
X
cannot hit all cycle packings of size
\ell
,
contradicting that
X
is a solution to the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
.
Therefore, our claim above holds and we can choose
P

such that the corresponding pair is not rich.

Then following the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
,
we can find a path in the branching tree
which leads to a leaf node
t
of the branching tree,
that is, we go to the child of the current node
where
P
is included in corresponding set
\mathbf{hit}
.
By our choice of the paths in the set
\mathbf{hit}
,
all paths in the set
\mathbf{hit}
associated with the leaf node
t

are hit by
X
by our claim above.

It remains to show that
t
is a promising candidate.
Suppose that the depth of
t
in the branching tree is less than

|F|^{2}\cdot k\Gamma
.
In this case it is true that we cannot find a usable cycle packing
\overline{\mathcal{C}}

at node
t
whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are contained in
\mathbf{avail}
.
Hence, candidate
t
is promising.

Suppose that the depth of
t
in the branching tree is equal to
|F|^{2}\cdot k\Gamma
and we can still find a usable cycle packing
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
at node
t
whose
G^{\prime}
-paths are contained in
\mathbf{avail}
.
This implies that
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is not hit by
X
.
By
Lemma

3.8
,

X
can hit at most
|F|^{2}\cdot k\Gamma
usable paths
(every path in
\mathbf{hit}
does not correspond to a rich pair by our claim).
This contradicts that
X
is a solution to the instance
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
.
Thus
t
corresponds to a promising candidate, say
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
.
By the way we branch among all children of a node in the branching tree, the paths of
\mathbf{hit}
are all hit by
X
.
This completes the proof for this claim. ∎

This concludes the proof of the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
.
∎

Step 5: Solving Instances with Promising Sets.

According to
Lemma

3.10
, to complete the algorithm,
it suffices to consider an instance together with a promising candidate
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})

and find a set of at most
k
vertices
that intersect all paths in
\mathbf{hit}
.
Recall that the set
\mathbf{hit}
contains paths of the forest
G^{\prime}
.

If we do not have any undeletable vertices, i.e., if
U=\varnothing
, then this
problem is equivalent to
Vertex Multicut on Trees
, which is
polynomial-time solvable
[
17
, Folklore]
.
If there are undeletable vertices,
i.e., when
U\neq\varnothing
, then this problem resembles

Edge Multicut on Trees
which can be solved in
\mathcal{O}(2^{k}\cdot n)

time
[
35
]
.
We give this algorithm dealing with promising sets
in
Lemma

3.14
.
If one can find a solution for any of the promising sets, then the
algorithm reports that
(G,U,F,k,\ell)
is a
Yes
-instance; otherwise,
it reports a
No
.

Lemma 3.14

(Path-Hitting on Forests)
.

Given a forest
G^{\prime}
, a set
U\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of undeletable vertices,
a collection of paths
\mathcal{P}
of
this forest and an integer
k
.
In time
2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
one can find a set
X\subseteq V(G^{\prime})\setminus U
of size at most
k
, if it exists, such that
X
hits all paths in
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
G^{\prime}
is a tree
that is rooted at some arbitrary vertex
as otherwise we consider the trees of the forest sequentially
and root each tree arbitrarily.

Let
P_{u,v}
be a path from
u
to
v
in
\mathcal{P}

such that the
lowest common ancestor (lca) of two endpoints of any other path in
\mathcal{P}
is not a descendant of the lca of
u
and
v
in
G^{\prime}
.
Let the lca of
u
and
v
be denoted as
\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v)
.footnoteObserve that
\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v)\in\{u,v\}
is possible.
We define two distinguished vertices on the path
P_{u,v}
as follows.
Let
u^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}
be the first vertices on the path
from
\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v)
to
u
,
v
such that
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\notin U
, respectively.
We claim that there exists a minimum-sized set
X
disjoint from
U
that hits
all paths in
\mathcal{P}
such that
X
contains either
u^{\prime}
or
v^{\prime}
.
Once the claim is
proven, it yields a straightforward branching algorithm
with running time
2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

We now show that our claim is true. Let
\overline{X}
be
a minimum-sized set that is disjoint from
U
and intersects all paths
in
\mathcal{P}
. In particular,
\overline{X}
intersects the
(u,\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v))
-path or
the
(\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v),v)
-path of
P_{u,v}
. Without loss of
generality, assume that
\overline{X}
intersects the
(u,\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v))
-path of

P_{u,v}
(the other case is symmetric)
and let
\overline{u}\in\overline{X}
be a vertex on
the
(u,\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v))
-path. We claim that

X=\overline{X}\setminus\{\overline{u}\}\cup\{u^{\prime}\}
is also a set
that hits all paths in
\mathcal{P}
of minimum size.
Suppose not, then there exists another path
P_{1}
in
\mathcal{P}
,
say with endpoints
u_{1}
and
v_{1}
,
which is not hit by
X
and goes through
\overline{u}
.
By the choice of
P_{u,v}
as the path
where the lca of the two endpoints is lowest in the tree,
the path
P_{1}
also goes through
\textnormal{{lca}}(u,v)
, and hence
u^{\prime}
.
Thus,
P_{1}
is hit by
X
which proves the claim.
This completes the proof for the lemma.
∎

Using
Lemma

3.14
, for each promising candidate
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
,
a set of size at most
k
that hits all paths in

\mathbf{hit}
can be found in
2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time, if it exists. We
are now ready to complete the proof of
3.1

which we restate here for convenience.

See
3.1

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(G,U,F,k,\ell)
be the input instance of
Cycle
-HitPack
.
The overall algorithm works as follows.

1.

For each subset
D\subseteq F
where
\lvert D\rvert\leq k
,
we construct a new instance

\mathcal{I}_{D}=(G-D,U\cup(F\setminus D),F\setminus D,k-\lvert D\rvert,\ell)
,
that is, we guess the subset of the feedback vertex set that is deleted.
where the feedback vertex set is undeletable.

2.

For each instance
\mathcal{I}_{1}
from the first step
we use the algorithm from
Lemma

3.4

to create a new instance
\mathcal{I}_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{1})
of low degree with regard to
\Upsilon
.

3.

For each instance
\mathcal{I}_{2}
from the second step,
we use the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
from
Lemma

3.10

to generate a list
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\mathcal{I}_{2})
of promising candidates.

4.

For each instance
\mathcal{I}_{2}
from the second step
and each candidate
(\mathbf{avail},\mathbf{hit})
in the corresponding list
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\mathcal{I}_{2})

from the third step,
we check if there is a set
X
of deletable vertices
that hits every path in
\mathbf{hit}
by using
Lemma

3.14
.
If this is the case, then output
Yes
.
Otherwise we proceed with the next set in
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\mathcal{I}_{2})

or with the next instance from the first step.

5.

If no branch outputs
Yes
, then the algorithm outputs
No
.

We first prove the correctness of the algorithm.
The correctness of the branching in the first step follows from

Lemma

3.2
.
For the second step the correctness follows directly from the
properties of the algorithm in
Lemma

3.4
.
The correctness of the remaining steps follows
from the design of the procedure
Get-Candidates
in
Lemma

3.10

and by
Lemma

3.14
.

It remains to analyze the running time of the entire algorithm.
The first step takes time
2^{\lvert F\rvert}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
By
Lemma

3.4
,
the second step can be applied in time polynomial in the size of the instance.
According to
Lemma

3.10
,
the branching procedure
Get-Candidates
runs in time

2^{\mathcal{O}((|F|^{2}k\Gamma)\log|F|)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
and the number of promising sets
it generates is
2^{\mathcal{O}((|F|^{2}k\Gamma)\log|F|)}
.
The last step takes time
2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
As by assumption we have
\Gamma=\poly(|F|+k)
,
the entire procedure runs in time

2^{\lvert F\rvert}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}((|F|^{2}k\Gamma)\log|F|)}\cdot 2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=2^{\poly(\lvert F\rvert+k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

which concludes the proof.
∎

3.1
Proof of
Lemma
3
:
Finding Vertex-Disjoint Paths

We now prove

Section

3
,
which we restate here for convenience.

See
3

Proof.

We first show that it suffices to consider the case when
H
is a tree.

The Graph
H
is a Forest.

Assume that
H
is a forest
and let
H_{1},\dots,H_{r}
be the
r
different trees of
H
.
For a subforest
H^{\prime}
of
H
and a set of indices
I\subseteq[f]
,
we define
\mathsf{CoverPath}(H^{\prime},I)
to be
\mathsf{true}
if and only if

H^{\prime}
contains a pairwise vertex-disjoint collection of paths

\{P_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\mid i\in I\}
.

Suppose that we can answer
\mathsf{CoverPath}(H^{\prime},I)

for any
I\subseteq[f]
when
H^{\prime}
is tree.
For ease of notation, for all
j\in[r]
,
we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{j}
the union of the first
j
trees of
H
,
formally, we define
\mathcal{H}_{j}\coloneqq H_{1}\uplus\dots\uplus H_{j}
.
It vacuously follows that
\mathsf{CoverPath}(H^{\prime},\varnothing)=\mathsf{true}

for any forest
H^{\prime}
.
With these assumptions,
for all
j\in[r]
and all
I\subseteq[f]
,
we can use the following dynamic program
to compute
\mathsf{CoverPath}(H_{j},I)
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CoverPath}(\mathcal{H}_{j},I)\coloneqq\bigvee_{I^{\prime}\subseteq I}\mathsf{CoverPath}(H_{j},I^{\prime})\land\mathsf{CoverPath}(\mathcal{H}_{j-1},I\setminus I^{\prime}).

Then the final output of the algorithm is
\mathsf{CoverPath}(H,[f])
.
The correctness of this step follows because all
H_{j}
’s are disjoint trees
and
H=\mathcal{H}_{r}
.

If we assume that we can compute
\mathsf{CoverPath}(H_{j},I)

for each
j\in[r]
and
I\subseteq[f]

in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(f)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
then the above dynamic program solves the problem on forests in time

2^{\mathcal{O}(f)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
As a next step we design the claimed algorithm for trees.

The Graph
H
is a Tree.

Based on the above reasoning,
we now focus on the case when
H
is a tree.
We prove this lemma using bottom-up dynamic programming on
H
.
To begin, we arbitrarily root the tree
H
. For each
v\in V(H)
, let
H_{v}
be
the subtree rooted at
v
in
H
.
For any vertex set
W\subseteq V(H)

(of vertices that are incomparable,
i.e., no vertex in
W
is a descendant of any other vertex in
W
),
let
H_{W}
be the disjoint union of the subtrees
rooted at each vertex
v\in W
.
For a vertex
v
in
H
,
we denote by
\mathsf{child}(v)
be the set of children of
v
in
H
.

Our goal is to compute three tables
T
,
T_{\mathsf{child}}
, and
T_{\mathsf{path}}

such that, for all
I\subseteq[f]
, vertices
v\in V(H)
,
and paths
P
in collection with
v\in V(P)
,

\displaystyle T[v,I]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{true}\iff\text{there are vertex-disjoint paths }\{P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\text{ in }H_{v},

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{true}\iff\text{there are vertex-disjoint paths }\{P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\text{ in }H_{\mathsf{child}(v)},\text{ and}

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{true}\iff\text{there are vertex-disjoint paths }\{P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\text{ in }H_{v}\setminus V(P).

We compute the table entries
T[v,I],T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]
and
T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I]

using a bottom-up dynamic programming on the rooted tree
H
.

We first we define the base cases.
If
I=\varnothing
, then we set
T[v,\varnothing]=\mathsf{true}

for every
v\in V(H)
.
Similarly, if
v
is a leaf and
I=\{i\}
,
then we set
\mathsf{true}
if and only if
\{v\}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}

(otherwise we define the table entry to be
\mathsf{false}
).
Finally, if
v
is a leaf and
|I|\geq 2
,
then we can directly set
T[v,I]
to
\mathsf{false}
.

For the remaining case, when
v\in V(H)
is not a leaf and
\lvert I\rvert\geq 2
,
then we compute the table entry by setting

\displaystyle T[v,I]\coloneqq T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]\vee\bigvee_{i\in I}\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{P}_{i},\\
v\in V(P)\end{subarray}}T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I\setminus\{i\}].

Observe that
T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]
and
T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I]
can be computed with the
analogous dynamic programming to
\mathsf{CoverPath}
. Note that in
T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I]
table we again end in the
disjoint case. To address that we can add a dummy-vertex adjacent to every root
of the trees in the forest.

Next we prove the correctness of the table entries.

Claim 3.15
.

For all vertices
v
of
H
,
all sets
I\subseteq[f]
,
all paths
P
in collection with
v\in V(P)
,
the table entries
T[v,I]
,
T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]
, and
T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I]

are computed correctly.

Proof of Claim .

We give a proof by induction on the depth of
v
in
H
.
We focus on the proof of correctness for the table
T
.
The correctness proofs for
T_{\mathsf{child}}
and
T_{\mathsf{path}}
follow analogously.

Suppose there exists a collection of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
\{P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}
in
H_{v}
.
If
v
does not appear in any of these paths,
i.e.,
v\not\in\bigcup_{i\in I}V(P_{i})
,
then the paths in
\{P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}
are also
contained in
H_{\mathsf{child}(v)}
and by induction
T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]=\mathsf{true}
.
Moreover, if there is an index
i\in I
such that
path
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
contains
v
, i.e.,
v\in V(P_{i})
,
then
\{P_{i^{\prime}}\mid i^{\prime}\in I\setminus\{i\}\}
are paths in

H_{v}-V(P_{i})
.
By induction this implies
T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I\setminus\{i\}]=\mathsf{true}
,
which finishes this direction of the proof.

For the other direction, suppose that
T[v,I]=\mathsf{true}
.
We show that in this case there exists a collection
of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
\{P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}

in
H_{v}
.
It is straightforward to verify the bases cases.
Therefore, we focus on the case when
v
is not a leaf and
|I|\geq 2
.

From the definition of the table entry it follows that if
T[v,I]=\mathsf{true}
,
then (1) table entry
T_{\mathsf{child}}[v,I]
is
\mathsf{true}
or
3
3

3

Case (1) and (2) are not necessarily disjoint.

(2) there is some index
\widehat{i}\in I

and some path
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{i}}
with
v\in V(P)

such that
T_{\mathsf{path}}[v,P,I\setminus\{\widehat{i}\}]
is
\mathsf{true}
.
In case (1), by induction,

H_{\mathsf{child}(v)}
contains the desired collection of paths.
For case (2),
the induction hypothesis implies that
H_{v}-V(P)

contains paths
\{P_{i}\mid i\in I\setminus\{\widehat{i}\}\}

that are pairwise vertex-disjoint.
From the fact that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{i}}
,
it directly follows that

\{P_{i}\mid i\in I\setminus\{\widehat{i}\}\}\cup\{P\}

is the desired collection of paths in
H_{v}
.
∎

It remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm.
Observe that the number of states in the tables
T
,
T_{\mathsf{child}}
, and

T_{\mathsf{path}}
is
2^{\mathcal{O}(f)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Moreover, to compute a single
table entry
2^{\mathcal{O}(f)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
operations are required.
∎

4
Edge
-HitPack
Parameterized by
k+\ell
: Single-Exponential Algorithm

In this section, we prove
1.11
.

See
1.11

Proof.

For a graph
G
, we denote by
\nu(G)
the matching number of
H
,
that is, the size of the maximum matching in
H
.
It is well-known that a maximum matching (and thus the matching number)
for a graph
H
can be computed in polynomial time
using the algorithm by Edmonds
[
22
]

or by Micali and Vazirani
[
62
]
for example.

Let
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
be the given instance of
Edge
-HitPack
.
The algorithm starts by some preprocessing of the instance
to identify trivial
Yes
and
No
instances.

•

Compute the matching number
\nu(G)
of
G
.
If
\nu(G)
is smaller than
\ell
, then directly answer
Yes
.

•

Otherwise, compute the matching number
\nu(G[U])
of
G[U]
,
that is, the subgraph induced by the undeletable vertices.
If
\nu(G[U])
is at least
\ell
, then directly output
No
.

•

Additionally, the algorithm outputs
No
if
k<0
.

After this preprocessing, the algorithm proceeds as follows.

1.

Compute a maximum matching
M
of
G[U]
.

2.

Invoke Edmonds’ blossom algorithm
[
22
]

to compute an augmenting path
P
for
M
in
G
, that is,
a path that starts and ends with vertices not covered by
M

and alternates between edges not in
M
and edges in
M
.

If no such augmenting path exists, then output
Yes
.

3.

Let
u
and
v
be the two endpoints of
P
.
Note that a single edge with both endpoints not in
M

is also an augmenting path
and that at least one of
u
and
v
is deletable
as otherwise
M
would not be the largest matching in
G[U]
.

4.

Construct three instances

I_{1}=(G\setminus\{u\},U,k-1,\ell)
if
u\notin U
,

I_{2}=(G\setminus\{v\},U,k-1,\ell)
if
v\notin U
,
and
(G,U\cup\{u,v\},k,\ell)
,
corresponding to the case that
u
is deleted, that
v
is deleted
and that
u
and
v
are made undeletable.
Solve these three instances recursively
and output
Yes
if the algorithm return
Yes
for at least one instance.

To analyze the running time of the algorithm,
we define a branching measure
\mu
with
\mu(I)\coloneqq k+\ell-1-\nu(G[U])
.
By the preprocessing,
\mu(I)
is always non-negative
and from the definition of
\mu
we get
\mu(I)\leq k+\ell
.

If the algorithm deletes
u
or
v
,
then the measure
\mu(I_{1})
and
\mu(I_{2})
decreases by one
as
k
decreases by one.
If the algorithm puts
\{u,v\}
into
U
,
then the measure
\mu(I_{3})
decreases by one as
\nu(G[U])
increases by one
because
u
and
v
are the endpoints of the augmenting path
for the previously largest matching.

Observe that all steps of the algorithm can be computed in polynomial time.
Since
\mu(I)
decreases in each step
and the algorithm branches into three cases,
the bound on the runtime follows.

The correctness of the algorithm follows from the definition of our problem
and the well-known Berge’s theorem
[
5
]
,
which says that a matching
M
in a graph
G
is maximum
if and only if there is no augmenting path with
M
.
∎

5
q\text{-Clique}
-HitPack
Parameterized by
Treewidth:

Double-Exponential Algorithm

In this
Section
we consider the
H
-HitPack
problem
when parameterizing by treewidth and provide an algorithmic upper bound when
H
is a
complete graph. The algorithm in this section serves two purposes. First, it is
a smooth introduction to the more involved algorithm for
H
-HitPack
for
general
H
in
Section

7
with the additional advantage
that the algorithm in this section has a slightly better dependence on
tw
.
The second purpose is that it can
be used as a black-box in
Section

6
to get a single-exponential
dependence on
tw
in the case when
H=K_{2}
.

Formally, in this section we prove the following theorem.
See
1.7

Although the running time is double-exponential in treewidth, we provide a
standard dynamic program based on the tree decomposition of the graph. Note that
the claimed runtime allows us to omit the requirement that such a tree
decomposition has to be given with the input graph because we can compute an
optimal tree decomposition as the first step of the algorithm.

Intuition.

For each bag, we consider the different ways in which a partial solution
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus U
could interact with the bag
X_{t}
. We describe this
interaction by a triple
(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})
with the following interpretation.

•

The set
D
contains exactly
k_{0}
vertices not in
X_{t}
. Moreover,
inside a bag
X_{t}
the set
D
deletes all the vertices in
D_{0}
.

•

For every set
A\subseteq X_{t}\setminus D_{0}

the function
f_{0}(A)
stores the following integer.
We interpret
A
as the set of vertices we want to avoid,
and
f(A)
is the size of the largest packing for
G_{t}-D

where additionally no vertex from
A
is covered.

The runtime is essentially bounded by the number of functions
f_{0}
we have to
consider. As there are
2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
vertex subsets and for each such subset we
want to store a value between
0
and
n
, the number of functions is at most

n^{2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}}
. We later show that we can get a significantly better bound on
the number of functions, which then leads to the claimed running time.

To formally exploit these properties, we have to design a dynamic program whose
style deviates from the classical approaches for such algorithms. In most cases,
we first aggregate the information from the appropriate states for the child
nodes and then compute the entry for the parent node. However, we proceed in a
different direction. When considering a state for a child node, we check for
which states of the parent node this information is relevant. By this approach,
it is not necessary to consider all possible table entries but only those where
we store a non-zero entry (i.e., an entry corresponding to a valid partial
solution).

Classes.

Before we state the algorithm, we first introduce some notation.
For any graph
G^{\prime}
we let
\nu_{K_{q}}(G^{\prime})
be the
K_{q}
-packing number of
G^{\prime}

(i.e., the number of vertex-disjoint complete copies of
K_{q}
).
Consider a node
t_{0}
of the tree decomposition,
an integer
k_{0}\in[0..k]
,
a subset
D_{0}\subseteq X_{t}
of vertices,
and a function
f_{0}\colon 2^{X_{t}\setminus D_{0}}\to[0..n]
.
We say that a set
D\subseteq V_{t_{0}}\setminus U

is of class
(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

\lvert D\setminus D_{0}\rvert=k_{0}
and
D\cap X_{t_{0}}=D_{0}
.

2.

for all
A\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{0}

we have
f_{0}(A)=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))
.

For each node
t_{0}
of the tree decomposition,
we compute a list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
of classes for
t_{0}

such that
(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})

if and only if
there is a set
D\subseteq V_{t_{0}}\setminus U
that is of class
(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})
.

Before we state the algorithm,
we first define five procedures to extend the classes for a child of node
to a class for the parent node.
The first two procedure correspond to the introduce node,
the third and fourth to the forget node,
and the last one to the join node.

Lemma 5.1

(Introduce 1)
.

Let
t_{0}
be an introduce node with the unique child
t_{1}

and let
v\notin U
be the vertex introduced.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Intro}_{1}

that, for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{0}
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{\textup{{tw}}})

a class
c_{0}=(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D\cup\{v\}
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

We set
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1}\cup\{v\},f_{1})
as the class for
t_{0}
.
Since
v
not contained in
G_{t_{1}}

but contained in
D_{0}=D_{1}\cup\{v\}
,
the two graph
G_{t_{1}}-D_{1}
and
G_{t_{0}}-(D_{1}\cup\{v\})
are identical.
Hence, it trivially follows that
D\cup\{v\}
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.
∎

Next we consider the case when the vertex introduced is not deleted.

Lemma 5.2

(Introduce 2)
.

Let
t_{0}
be an introduce node with the unique child
t_{1}

and let
v
be the vertex introduced.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Intro}_{2}

that, for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{\textup{{tw}}})

a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

We define the function

f_{0}\colon 2^{X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{1}}\to[0..n]

where, for all
A\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{1}
, we set

\displaystyle f_{0}(A)\coloneqq\begin{cases}f_{1}(A\setminus\{v\})&\text{if }v\in A,\\
\max\left(f_{1}(A),1+\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\subseteq X_{t}\setminus(D_{0}\cup A)\\
C\text{ is }K_{q}\text{ containing }v\end{subarray}}f_{1}(A\cup C\setminus\{v\})\right)&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

(5.1)

Then the new class is defined as
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{0})
.
Consider some set
A\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{1}
.
If
v\in A
,
then
G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A\setminus\{v\})
is identical to
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)

since
v
is introduced at
t_{0}
.
Thus,
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A\setminus\{v\}))=f_{1}(A\setminus\{v\})=f_{0}(A)
.

For the case when
v\notin A
we split the proof into two parts.

f_{0}(A)\leq\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))
.

First, consider the case when
f_{0}(A)=f_{1}(A)
.
Since every packing for
G_{t-1}-(D\cup A)

is also a packing for
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)
,
we get
f_{1}(A)=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A))\leq\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))
.

Let
C\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus(D_{0}\cup A)

be a
q
-clique containing
v
.
Every packing for
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A\cup C)
can be extended
to a packing for
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)
by covering the vertices in
C

with the same copy of
K_{q}
.
Hence,
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))\geq\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A\cup C))+1
.
Since
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A\cup C)
does not contain
v
,
the graphs
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A\cup C)
and

G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A\cup C\setminus\{v\})
are identical,
which implies that the packing number is the same.
Hence,
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A\cup C))\geq f_{1}(A\cup C\setminus\{v\})

follows
by the assumption about
f_{1}
.

f_{0}(A)\geq\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))
.

Let
P
be the packing for
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)

containing exactly
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))
cliques.
If
v
is not covered by
P
,
then
P
is also a packing for
G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A)

and contains at most
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A))
cliques,
which is exactly
f_{1}(A)
by assumption.
Hence,
P
contains at most
f_{0}(A)
cliques by the definition of
f_{0}
.

In the case when
v
is covered,
there must be a clique
C\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus(D_{0}\cup A)

containing
v
such that
C
is covered by the same copy of
K_{q}
.
Hence, the number of cliques in
P

is at most
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A\cup C))+1
.
Since
v
must be avoided in this case,
P
contains at most

\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A\cup C\setminus\{v\}))+1
cliques
and this is equal to
f_{1}(A\cup C\setminus\{v\})+1

which is bounded by
f_{0}(A)
by the definition of
f_{1}
.

This concludes the proof as the function
f_{0}

can be constructed in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{\lvert X_{t}\setminus D_{1}\rvert})
.
∎

The following two cases correspond to the forget nodes.
The first procedure deals with the setting
when the forgotten vertex is deleted.

Lemma 5.3

(Forget 1)
.

Let
t_{1}
be a forget node with the unique child
t_{0}

and let
v
be the vertex forgotten.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Forget}_{1}

that, for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{\textup{{tw}}})

a class
c_{0}=(k_{1}+1,D_{1}\setminus\{v\},f_{1})

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
with
v\in D
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

From
\lvert D\setminus D_{1}\rvert=k_{1}

and the assumption that
v
is forgotten at
t_{0}
,
we get that

\lvert D\setminus(D_{1}\setminus\{v\})\rvert=\lvert D\setminus D_{1}\rvert+1=k_{1}+1
.
Since
v
is deleted, we get that
G_{t_{0}}-D=G_{t_{1}}-D
.
Thus, the bound on the packing number follows trivially,
as we consider the same graph in both settings.
∎

In contrast to the previous case,
we now assume that the vertex forgotten is not deleted.

Lemma 5.4

(Forget 2)
.

Let
t_{1}
be a forget node with the unique child
t_{0}

and let
v
be the vertex forgotten.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Forget}_{2}

that, for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{\textup{{tw}}})

a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})

such that for following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
with
v\notin D
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{1}
.
Observe that every packing for
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)

is also a packing for
G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A)
as both graphs are identical.
As the packing number for
G_{t_{1}}-D-A
is equal to
f_{1}(A)
,
this is also the packing number for
G_{t_{0}}-D-A
.
∎

The last procedure covers the case of the join node.

Lemma 5.5

(Join Node)
.

Let
t_{0}
be a join node
and let
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
be the two children of
t_{0}
.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Join}
,
that, for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}

and a given class
c_{2}=(k_{2},D_{1},f_{2})
for
t_{2}
,
computes in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}})}

a class
c_{0}=(k_{1}+k_{2},D_{1},f_{0})

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D^{\prime}\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U

and for all sets
D^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V_{t_{2}}\setminus U
,
if
D^{\prime}
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}

and
D^{\prime\prime}
is of class
c_{2}
for
t_{2}
,
then
D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}
is of class
c_{0}
.

Proof.

We define a function
f_{0}\colon 2^{X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{1}}\to[0..n]

where, for all
A\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{0}
, we set

\displaystyle f_{0}(A)\coloneqq\max_{A_{1}\uplus A_{2}=X_{t_{0}}\setminus(D_{0}\cup A)}f_{1}(A\cup A_{1})+f_{2}(A\cup A_{2}).

(5.2)

To simplify notation, we set
D=D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}
in the following.
By the classes of
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
we get
D^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime\prime}=D_{1}
,
which implies that
\lvert D\setminus D_{1}\rvert=\lvert D^{\prime}\setminus D_{1}\rvert+\lvert D^{\prime\prime}\setminus D_{1}\rvert=k_{1}+k_{2}

Consider some set
A\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus D_{1}

and let
P
be a maximum packing for
G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)
.
By the definition of a tree decomposition,
the packing
P
cannot contain a clique that
covers vertices in
G_{t_{1}}-X_{t_{1}}
and
G_{t_{2}}-X_{t_{2}}
simultaneously.
Hence, there exists partition
\widetilde{A}_{1}\uplus\widetilde{A}_{2}=X_{t_{0}}\setminus(D_{1}\cup A)

such that
\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A))=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A\cup\widetilde{A}_{1}))+\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{2}}-(D\cup A\cup\widetilde{A}_{2}))
.

From the definition of
f_{0}
and the properties of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
,
it follows that

\displaystyle f_{0}(A)

\displaystyle\geq f_{1}(A\cup\widetilde{A}_{1})+f_{2}(A\cup\widetilde{A}_{2})

\displaystyle=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D^{\prime}\cup A\cup\widetilde{A}_{1}))+\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{2}}-(D^{\prime\prime}\cup A\cup\widetilde{A}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A)).

Now let
\overline{A}_{1}
and
\overline{A}_{2}
be the sets
maximizing the sum in (
5.2
).
Then it directly follows that

\displaystyle f_{0}(A)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(A\cup\overline{A}_{1})+f_{2}(A\cup\overline{A}_{2})

\displaystyle=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D\cup A\cup\overline{A}_{1}))+\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{2}}-(D\cup A\cup\overline{A}_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{1}}-(D^{\prime}\cup A\cup\widetilde{A}_{1}))+\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{2}}-(D^{\prime\prime}\cup A\cup\widetilde{A}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\nu_{K_{q}}(G_{t_{0}}-(D\cup A).

This concludes the proof that (
5.2
) is a correct definition.
To construct the function
f
for each
A
,
we just need to iterate over at most
2^{\textup{{tw}}}
possible subsets.
Hence, the output can be computed in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}})}
.
∎

Now we have everything ready to state the algorithm solving

q\text{-Clique}
-HitPack
.

Proof of
1.7
.

Given the instance
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
,
we first compute an optimal tree decomposition of
G

and then transform the decomposition into a nice tree decomposition
where the root and leaf nodes have an empty bag.
For all leaf nodes
t_{0}
of the tree decomposition,
we set
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})=\{(0,\varnothing,\varnothing\mapsto 0)\}
.

We traverse the nodes of the tree decomposition in post-order
and for each node
t_{0}
with at least one child
we perform the following actions (depending on the type of node
t_{0}
).

Introduce Node.

Let
t_{1}
be the unique child of
t_{0}

and let
v
be the vertex introduced at
t_{0}
,
that is,
X_{t_{0}}=X_{t_{1}}\cup\{v\}
.
Repeat the following for all classes
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
:
If
v\in V(G)\setminus U
, then use
\mathsf{Intro}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Intro}_{2}
on
c_{1}

to compute two classes
c_{0}
and
c_{0}^{\prime}

and add both to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.
Otherwise, we have
v\notin V(G)\setminus U
and apply
\mathsf{Intro}_{2}
on
c_{1}
to compute the class
c_{0}^{\prime\prime}
and add it to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Forget Node.

Let
t_{1}
be the unique child of
t_{0}

and let
v
be the vertex forgotten,
that is,
X_{t_{0}}=X_{t_{1}}\setminus\{v\}
.
Repeat the following for all classes
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
:
If
v\in D_{1}
, then use
\mathsf{Forget}_{1}
on
c_{1}
to compute the class
c_{0}
and add it to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.
Otherwise, we have
v\notin D_{1}
and use
\mathsf{Forget}_{2}
on
c_{1}
to compute the class
c_{0}^{\prime}
and add it to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Join Node.

Let
t_{0}
be the unique parent of the nodes
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Repeat the following for all pairs of classes

(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})\times\mathcal{L}(t_{2})

where
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},f_{1})
and
c_{2}=(k_{2},D_{2},f_{2})
:
Check that
D_{1}=D_{2}
and if so apply
\mathsf{Join}
on
(c_{1},c_{2})

to get a class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}

and add it to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

It remains to define the output of the procedure.
For this let
r
be the root of the tree decomposition.
Then, the algorithm outputs
Yes
if there is a class
(k_{0},\varnothing,\varnothing\mapsto\ell_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(r)

for some
k_{0}\in[0..k]
and
\ell_{0}\in[0..\ell-1]
.

Correctness.

Next we prove that this dynamic program is correct.

Claim 5.6

(Correctness)
.

For all nodes
t_{0}
,
integers
0\leq k_{0}\leq k
,
vertex sets
D_{0}\subseteq X_{t}\setminus U
,
and
functions
f_{0}\colon 2^{X_{t}\setminus D_{0}}\to[0..n]
,
the following two statements are equivalent:

•

There is a set
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus U

of class
(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})
for
t_{0}
.

•

(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Proof of Claim .

By the definition of the dynamic program, it suffices to show that the first
statement implies the second statement. We prove the correctness inductively
based on the type of the node in the tree decomposition.

Leaf Node.

Since the bags of the leaf nodes do not contain any vertices,
no vertices can be deleted.
By assumption, the leaf nodes do not have children
and hence, there is only the empty packing
which contains no copy of
K_{q}
.

Introduce Node.

If the set
D
is of class
c_{0}
and
v\in D
,
then
D\setminus\{v\}
is of
some
class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
.
By induction, we get
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
.
From the algorithm and the properties of
\mathsf{Intro}_{1}
,
we obtain a class
c
such that
D
is of class
c
for
t_{0}
.
Moreover, the algorithm adds
c
to
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.
Since each set
D
has exactly one class for each node,
we have
c_{0}=c
and thus,
c_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Note, that if
v\notin D
, then we similarly get that
c_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
by using
\mathsf{Intro}_{2}
.

Forget Node.

The result follows analogously to the introduce node.

Join Node.

Assume that
D
is of class
c_{0}
.
If we consider
D^{\prime}=D\cap V_{t_{1}}
and
D^{\prime\prime}=D\cap V_{t_{2}}
,
we have that
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
are of classes
c_{1}
and
c_{2}

for
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, respectively.
By the induction hypothesis it follows that

c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
and
c_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{2})
.
From the definition of the algorithm and the properties of
\mathsf{Join}
,
it follows that
D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}=D
is of some class
c
for
t_{0}
.
By the uniqueness of classes, we conclude
c=c_{0}

and therefore,
c_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.
∎

As the last step we prove the running time of the algorithm.

Claim 5.7
.

Let
L
denote the maximum length of a list
\mathcal{L}(t)
for all nodes
t
.
Then, the algorithm terminates in time

L^{2}\cdot 2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\cdot\poly(n)
.

Proof of Claim .

Computing a nice tree decomposition is possible in time

2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\cdot\poly(n)

[
17
, Chapter 7]
.
Observe that handling the join nodes dominates the running time
as we have to consider up to
L^{2}
different pairs of classes.
Because each new class can be computed in time
2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}
,
the claim follows.
∎

We conclude the proof by bounding the number of classes
(k_{0},D_{0},f_{0})
that
can appear for each node
t_{0}
. There are
k
choices for
k_{0}
and at most

2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
choices for
D_{0}
. By a naive bound, the number of choices of
function
f_{0}
is
n^{2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}}
. We claim that the number of functions
appearing in the algorithm is actually
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}})}}
. This would conclude
the proof, as it means that the running time of the algorithm is
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}})}}\poly(n)
.

Recall, that
f_{0}(\varnothing)
is the maximum integer
in the image of
f_{0}
. Moreover, the smallest integer is at most
\textup{{tw}}+1

smaller because avoiding single additional vertex can decrease the packing
number by at most one. Therefore, the maximum and the minimum in the image differ
by at most
\textup{{tw}}+1
. In conclusion, the number of functions is bounded by
n\cdot(\textup{{tw}}+1)^{2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}}
as there are
n
choices for the maximum (obtained at

f_{0}(\varnothing)
) and
\textup{{tw}}+1
choices for the values of the remaining

2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
subsets.
∎

6
Edge
-HitPack
Parameterized by Treewidth:

Single-Exponential Algorithm

In this section, we show that for
q=2
,
the algorithm of
Section

5
for
q\text{-Clique}
-HitPack
runs in time
2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

without
any changes whatsoever
to the algorithm.

See
1.12

In light of
5.7
,
it is sufficient to give an upper bound on the number of different classes
that subsets
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus D
can have
at each node
t
of the tree decomposition.
As
|D\setminus D_{0}|
can take at most
n
different values
and
D\cap X_{t}
can take at most
2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
different values,
it boils down to bounding the number of different functions

f(A)=\nu(G_{t}-(D\cup A))
that can arise for a fixed graph
G_{t}

and different sets
D

(note that function
\nu_{K_{2}}=\nu
denotes the size of the maximum matching).
The main combinatorial result of this section is precisely such a bound.
This immediately shows that the algorithm of
1.7

for
q=2
runs in time
2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
proving
1.12
.

See
2.1

Before, we prove
Section

2.1

we restate
Section

2.1
.
Note, that we have already proven
Section

2.1

in
Section

2
.

See
2.1

The following lemma proves a stronger statement, giving a bound on the number of possibilities for a more expressive function that describes how that size of the maximum matching changes when removing a set
S
. We prove this generalization using a simple purely graph-theoretical construction. Let
\nu(G)
be the size of the maximum matching in
G
. Observe that removing a vertex cannot increase this value and can decrease it only by at most 1. Thus
\nu(G)-\nu(G-S)
is always between 0 and
|S|
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
G
be a graph over a vertex set
V\supseteq[k]
for some integer
k
. Let

g_{G,k}\colon 2^{[k]}\to\{0,1,\dots,k\}
be the function defined by

g_{G,k}(S)=\nu(G)-\nu(G-S)
. For each
k
, there are
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3})}
functions
g_{G,k}
that can arise this way.

Proof.

Let
d=\nu(G)
. Let us construct the graph
G^{*}
from
G
by introducing

n-2d+2k
independent vertices that are adjacent to every original vertex of

G
. Graph
G^{*}
has as set
V^{*}
of
n^{*}=2n-2d+2k
vertices. Let

t=\min\{3k,n-2d+2k\}\leq 3k
. For notational convenience, let us rename the
vertices of
G^{*}
such that
t
of the newly introduced vertices form the set

\{k+1,\dots,k+t\}
. Let
k_{0}:=k+t
, we have
[k_{0}]\subseteq V^{*}
. Let

h_{G^{*},k_{0}}\colon 2^{[k_{0}]}\to\{0,1\}
be the function defined for
G^{*}
and
k_{0}
as in

Section

2.1
. We claim that
g_{G,k}(S)
for any
S\subseteq[k]

can be deduced from the function
h_{G^{*},k_{0}}
. As
Section

2.1

asserts that there are at most
2^{\mathcal{O}(k_{0}^{3})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3})}
possible functions
h_{G^{*},k_{0}}
, the same bound also holds for the number of possible functions
g_{G,k}
.

Let
S\subseteq[k]
be an arbitrary subset and let
s=|S|
.
Then
G-S
has a matching of size at least
d-s
, which leaves at most

n-s-2(d-s)=n+s-2d
vertices uncovered. As every matching in
G-S
has size at
most
d
, every matching leaves at least
n-s-2d
vertices uncovered. Let us
show how the existence of a matching in
G-S
that leaves at most a certain number of vertices uncovered can be deduced from the function
f
. Let
x\in\{0,1,\dots,2k\}
such that
n-s-2d+x\geq 0
(hence
s-x\leq n-2d
) and let
c=2k+s-x
. Note that
s\leq k
implies that
c\leq 3k
and

s-x\leq n-2d
implies
c\leq n-2d+2k
. Thus
c\leq t
and let

S^{*}=S\cup\{k+1,\dots,k+c\}
. We claim that
G-S
has a matching leaving at
most
n-s-2d+x
vertices uncovered if and only if
G^{*}-S^{*}
has a perfect
matching. Consider a matching
M
of
G-S
that leaves
n-s-2d+x
vertices
uncovered. In
G^{*}-S^{*}
, the number of newly introduced vertices is exactly

n-2d+2k-c=n-2d+x-s
, thus
M
can be completed to a perfect matching
M^{*}
of

G^{*}-S^{*}
. Similarly, if
G-S
has a perfect matching, then
n-2d+2k-c=n+x-s-2d

newly introduced vertices are covered in this matching. If we remove the edges
of
M^{*}
incident to these edges, then we obtain a matching of
G-S
avoiding
exactly this number
n+x-s-2d
of vertices of
G-S
. Thus
f_{G,k}(S)\leq i
for any

S\subseteq[k]
and any
0\leq i\leq k
can be deduced given the function
h_{G^{*},k_{0}}
.
∎

Now the proof of
Section

2.1
follows immediately.

Proof of
Section

2.1
.

In the
n
vertex graph
G
,
there are
n
possibilities for the value of
\nu(G)
.
Let us consider those functions
f_{G,k}
that arise from some
n
-vertex graph
G

with
\nu(G)=d
for some fixed integer
d
.
Then by
Lemma

6.1
,
there are
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3})}
possibilities for the function
g_{G,k}=d-\nu(G-S)
,
which implies that there are only that many possibilities for the function
f_{G,k}
.
Considering every possible
d
,
this proves the bound of
n\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3})}
.
∎

7
H
-HitPack
Parameterized by Treewidth:

Double-Exponential Algorithm

In
Section

5
we have seen the algorithm for
H
-HitPack

parameterized by treewidth when
H
is a clique.
In the following we consider the general case
when
H
is an arbitrary connected graph with at least three vertices.
Note that if
H
has only two vertices,
the problem is precisely
K_{2}
-HitPack
.
In this case the result follows by
1.7

or rather by
1.12
proving the improved running time.
Formally, we prove
1.6
.

See
1.6

Recall that for the case when
H
is a clique
we heavily exploited that, for each clique appearing in the final packing,
there is one bag containing all vertices covered by this clique.
By this property we could describe the states for each node
by a function based on the subsets of the bag.

When now considering the more general case
we do not have this assumption anymore.
Instead, a copy of the graph
H
in the packing
might cover vertices from many different bags
(think of a long path for example).
Hence, the algorithm does not “see” all vertices of the copy at the same time
and therefore, also has to deal with
partial packings
.
Such a partial packing is some variant of a packing where we also allow
that
subgraphs
of
H
appear in the packing (in a controlled way).
For each such partial packing we define a
type

which describes how the packing interacts with the bag.
See
Figure

7.1
for an illustration of these concepts.

Then the idea of the algorithm is as follows.
For each node
t
we consider all possible types
that a packing could have with respect to this node.
For each such type
T
,
we store a bound on the maximum number of copies of
H

that can appear in any partial packing of type
T
.

We start by introducing the notation and concepts needed
to formally state the dynamic program.
The algorithm is then presented in
Section

7.1
,
its correctness is proven in
Section

7.2
,
and the runtime is analyzed in
Section

7.3
.

(a)

The graph
H
.

(b)

An example for a (partial)
H
-packing for
G_{t}
.
Note that in the upper left only a subgraph of
H
is packed.

(c)

A graphical representation of the type

(\prtn,H_{1},h_{1},H_{2},h_{2})

for the partial packing.

The three left-most vertices are not covered and form part
0
,
the highlighted five-vertex graph in the middle forms
H_{1}
,
and the highlighted four-vertex graph on the right corresponds to
H_{2}
.
The functions
h_{1}
and
h_{2}

map the vertices in
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
as depicted.

Figure 7.1
:

An illustration for a node
t

of how a partial packing and its type relate to each other.

The black vertices and edges correspond to the edges and vertices of
G
.
The deleted vertices are indicated by hollow dots.
The highlighted vertices and edges show how the copies of
H

are packed to the vertices of
G
.
A highlighted vertex or edge with a white filling,
indicates that we do not know
to which vertices and edges of
G
the ones of
H
correspond.

Partial
H
-packings.

Let
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
be an instance of
H
-HitPack

and let
tw
be the treewidth of
G
.
As a first step the algorithm computes an optimal tree decomposition of
G

Now consider a node
t
of the computed tree decomposition of width
tw
.
It is well-known that this can be done in time
2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\poly(n)

[
45
,
8
]
.
We additionally introduce some notation when working with tree decompositions.
When
t
denotes some node of a tree decomposition,
then we define
X_{t}
as the associated bag.
We denote by
V_{t}
the vertex set of the subtree of the tree decomposition
that is rooted in
t
, and define
G_{t}=G[V_{t}]
as the corresponding graph.

For some integers
p,q\geq 0
,
a tuple

P=(h_{1},\dots,h_{p},\overline{H}_{1},\overline{h}_{1},\overline{H}_{2},\overline{h}_{2},\dots,\overline{H}_{q},\overline{h}_{q})

is a
partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}
(or just
t
for short)
if
the following holds:

•

Each
h_{i}
is an injective homomorphism from
H
to
G_{t}\setminus X_{t}
. We refer to these as the
complete
copies
of
H
(in
P
).

•

Each
\overline{H}_{j}
is an induced subgraph of
H
, and
each
\overline{h}_{j}
is an injective homomorphism from
\overline{H}_{j}
to
G_{t}
whose image has a non-empty intersection with
X_{t}
.
We refer to these as the
partial copies
of
H
(in
P
).
4
4

4

Note that
\overline{H}_{j}=H
is possible and this counts as a partial copy as long as the image intersects the bag
X_{T}
.

•

The images of all
h_{i},\overline{h}_{j}
are pairwise vertex disjoint.

•

For each
j\in[q]
, we refer to a vertex of
\overline{H}_{j}

that has a neighbor in
H
outside of
\overline{H}_{j}

as a
border vertex
of
\overline{H}_{j}
.
All such border vertices are mapped to a vertex from
X_{t}
by
\overline{h}_{j}
.

Types.

Next, we define so-called
types
for
t
.
Intuitively, such a type describes how a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}

interacts with the vertices from the bag
X_{t}
.
For this we first consider a function
\prtn
, which partitions the vertices in the bag
X_{t}
. Here, we also introduce a part
0
, which we interpret to include those vertices that are not covered by any copy of
H
.
All other parts of the partition are associated with some induced subgraph
H_{i}
of
H
together with a function
h_{i}

that specifies which vertex from the bag belongs to which vertex of
H_{i}
,
that is,
h_{i}
points out
H_{i}
as a (not necessarily induced) subgraph
on the vertices in
X_{t}
.
Formally, we represent these (labeled) subgraphs
by injective homomorphisms from
H_{i}
to
G[X_{t}]
.
In addition,
h_{i}
includes information about the vertices of
H

that are not in
H_{i}
.
These vertices are labeled using two special symbols

\uparrow
and
\downarrow
to indicate
that they lie above
X_{t}
, i.e., not in
V_{t}
,
or below
X_{t}
, i.e., in
V_{t}\setminus X_{t}
, respectively.

Definition 7.1

(Type for
t
,
D
-avoiding)
.

For some
0\leq w\leq\lvert X_{t}\rvert
,
a
type

T

for
t

is defined as a tuple
T=(\prtn,H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})

such that

•

\prtn
is a function
X_{t}\to[0..w]

and, for each
i\in[w]
, we set
X_{t}(i)\coloneqq\prtn^{-1}(i)
,

•

for all
i\in[w]
,

H_{i}
is a non-empty induced subgraph of
H
with
|X_{t}(i)|
vertices,
and

•

for all
i\in[w]
,

h_{i}
is a function from
V(H)

to
X_{t}(i)\cup\{\downarrow,\uparrow\}

such that

h_{i}|_{V(H_{i})}
is an injective homomorphism from
H_{i}
to
G[X_{t}(i)]
.

If for some set
D\subseteq X_{t}

it holds that
\prtn(v)=0
for all
v\in D
,
then we say that
T
is
D
-avoiding
.

In the following definition we define the type of a partial
H
-packing.

Definition 7.2

(Type of a partial
H
-packing)
.

Let
P=(h_{1},\dots,h_{p},\overline{H}_{1},\overline{h}_{1},\dots,\overline{H}_{q},\overline{h}_{q})

be a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}
with regard to
X_{t}
.

•

Let
\prtn\colon X_{t}\to[0..q]
be the function
with
\prtn(v)=j\in[q]

if
v\in X_{t}
is in the image of
\overline{h}_{j}
;
and
\prtn(v)=0
otherwise.

•

For all
j\in[q]
,
we set
\widehat{H}_{j}\coloneqq\overline{H}_{j}\left[\overline{h}_{j}^{-1}(X_{t})\right]
.

•

For all
j\in[q]
,
we define
\widehat{h}_{j}\colon V(H)\to X_{t}\cup\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}

with

\widehat{h}_{j}(a)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\uparrow,&a\notin\overline{h}_{j}^{-1}(V_{t}),\\
\overline{h}_{i}(a),&a\in\overline{h}_{j}^{-1}(X_{t}),\\
\downarrow,&a\in\overline{h}_{j}^{-1}(V_{t}\setminus X_{t}).\end{cases}

Then
T=(\prtn,\widehat{H}_{1},\widehat{h}_{1},\dots,\widehat{H}_{q},\widehat{h}_{q})
is the
type of
P
.

Remark 7.3
.

It is straightforward to check that the type of a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}
with regard to
X_{t}
is a type for
t
with
w=q
.

With the definition of partial
H
-packings of a certain type,
we define the
T
-completion number.

Definition 7.4

(
T
-completion number)
.

Let
T
be a type for
t
. Consider the partial
H
-packings for
G_{t}
with respect to
X_{t}
that have type
T
. The
T
-completion number
of
G_{t}
(with respect to
X_{t}
) is the maximum number of complete copies of
H
obtained for such a partial
H
-packing of type
T
.

We prove a bound on the number of types
in terms of
tw
and the size of
H
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
H
be a fixed graph.
For all nodes
t
and all sets
D\subseteq X_{t}
,
let
\mathcal{T}_{t}(D)
denote the set of all possible
D
-avoiding types for
t
.

Then we get that

\lvert\mathcal{T}_{t}(D)\rvert\leq\textup{{tw}}^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\cdot\poly(\lvert H\rvert))}
.

Proof.

As
w\leq|X_{t}|\leq\textup{{tw}}+1
there are at most
(\textup{{tw}}+2)^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
choices for the function
\prtn
.
For each
H_{i}
there are at most
2^{\lvert V(H)\rvert}
choices,
and for each
h_{i}
there are at most
(\textup{{tw}}+3)^{\lvert V(H)\rvert}
choices.
Hence, the total number of types is bounded by

(\textup{{tw}}+2)^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\cdot\left(2^{\lvert V(H)\rvert}\cdot(\textup{{tw}}+3)^{\lvert V(H)\rvert}\right)^{\!\textup{{tw}}+1}

which is upper bounded by
\textup{{tw}}^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\cdot\poly(\lvert H\rvert))}

and thus proves the claim.
∎

As a next step we define some notation
which we use to modify types. We will use the following notation for neighborhoods:
For a vertex
v
of
G
, we use
N_{G}(v)
to denote the neighborhood of
v
in
G
, that is, the set of all vertices that share an edge with
v
. For a subset
U
of the vertices of
G
, we use
N_{U}(v)
to denote the set of neighbors of
v
in
U
.

Extending Types by a Vertex.

Let
T=(\prtn,H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})
be some type for
t

and let
v
be a vertex not in
G_{t}
and thus, also not in
X_{t}
.
For all
i\in[0..w]
,
we define
\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto i)
as the set of all possible types for
t^{\prime}
with
X_{t^{\prime}}=X_{t}\cup\{v\}
that extend
T

in the sense that now the vertex
v
is contained in the
i
th part
(which might be a new one).
These extensions also consider all possible ways in which
v
could appear in the corresponding (partial) copy of
H
.

Formally, we define
\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto i)
as follows:

•

If
v
is not covered by a (partial) copy of
H
then it is included in part
0
:

\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0)\coloneqq\{(\prtn|_{v\mapsto 0},H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})\}.

(7.1)

•

Suppose
v
is covered by a vertex
a
of a copy of
H

for which the corresponding induced subgraph
H_{i}
of
H

already covers other vertices of
X_{t}

(this implies that
a
is not in
H_{i}
),
i.e., the corresponding (partial) copy of
H

is already considered as part of the type
T
.
Then
v
is included in part
i
,
let
\prtn^{\prime}
be the corresponding extension of
\prtn
.
Moreover,
H_{i}
is adjusted to include the vertex
a
,
so let
H_{i,a}\coloneqq H[V(H_{i})\cup\{a\}]
.
Finally,
h_{i,a}
is a mapping from
V(H)
to

X_{t^{\prime}}\cup\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}
that is identical to
h_{i}
,
with the exception that
h_{i,a}(a)=v
.
Ultimately, as we care about subgraph copies of
H
in
G
,
edges of
H
should be preserved,
and so we need only consider the set
\mathcal{A}

containing those vertices
a
of
H
with
N_{H_{i}}(a)\in N_{X_{t}}(v)
.
So, for each
i\in[w]
, we define

\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto i)\coloneqq\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\{(\prtn^{\prime},H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{i,a},h_{i,a},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})\}.

(7.2)

•

Finally, suppose that
v
is covered by some copy of
H

that has not been considered yet.
Then a new part
w+1
is introduced, and
v
is mapped to
w+1
by
\prtn^{\prime\prime}
,
the corresponding extension of
\prtn
.
The corresponding partial copy of
H
then only holds the single vertex
a
,
and a corresponding mapping
h_{a}
maps every vertex of
H
to
\uparrow
,
except for
a
, which is mapped to
v
.
We set

\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto w+1)\coloneqq\bigcup_{a\in V(H)}\{(\prtn^{\prime\prime},H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w},H[\{a\}],h_{a})\}.

(7.3)

For the case when
i=0
, we know that there is only one type
T^{\prime}\in\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0)
.
Hence, we abuse notation and directly write
\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0)

whenever we refer to
T^{\prime}
.

Removing Vertices from Types.

Now we define the converse of the previous operation,
that is, the modification of the types when we remove a vertex from a bag
X_{t}
.
Let
T=(\prtn,H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})
be some type for
t
,
and let
v
be a vertex of the considered bag. Let
X_{t^{\prime}}=X_{t}\setminus\{t\}
.
We define a set of types
\textsf{remove}(T,v)
for
t^{\prime}
as follows:

•

If
v
was not covered by any copy of
H
, i.e., if
\prtn(v)=0
, then we can simply remove it by restricting
\prtn
.
Formally we set

\textsf{remove}(T,v)=\{(\prtn|_{X_{t^{\prime}}},H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})\}.

(7.4)

•

Suppose
v
is the last vertex in its part of
X_{t}
,
that is, there is an
i\in[w]
with
\prtn(v)=i
and
|V(H_{i})|=1
.
Then remove this part from the type.
It turns out that in this situation we can assume that the corresponding copy of
H
is completely below
t^{\prime}
,
i.e., if
h_{i}
maps every vertex of
H
to
\downarrow
,
except for
v
.
The reason for this is that otherwise some vertices
covered by the respective copy of
H
would supposedly be not in
G_{t^{\prime}}

(that is, “above”
t^{\prime}
), and some vertices covered by
H

would be in
G_{t^{\prime}}
but none of them would be in
X_{t^{\prime}}
,
which is a contradiction to the fact that
H
is connected
and
X_{t^{\prime}}
a separator.
In this case we set

\textsf{remove}(T,v)=\{(\prtn|_{X_{t^{\prime}}},H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{i-1},h_{i-1},H_{i+1},h_{i+1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})\}.

(7.5)

•

Finally, suppose that
\prtn(v)=i
for some
i\in[w]
,
and that
|V(H_{i})|>1
.
In this case
v
is removed from
\prtn
,
and its preimage
h_{i}^{-1}(v)
is removed from
H_{i}
,
while the updated homomorphism
h^{\prime}_{i}
is identical to
h_{i}
,
with the exception that
h_{i}^{-1}(v)
is now mapped to
\downarrow

(since it covers a vertex “below” the bag
X_{t^{\prime}}
).
By the same argument as in the previous case,
we can assume that none of the vertices in
N_{H}(h_{i}(v))

are mapped to
\uparrow
by
h_{i}
.
We set

\textsf{remove}(t,v)=\{(\prtn|_{X_{t^{\prime}}},H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{i}-h_{i}^{-1}(v),h^{\prime}_{i},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})\}.

(7.6)

Combining Types.

The last operation is used to combine two types.
We use this later for the join nodes.
Let
T_{1}=(\prtn,H_{1},h_{1},\dots,H_{w},h_{w})

and
T_{2}=(\prtn^{\prime},H_{1}^{\prime},h_{1}^{\prime},\dots,H_{w}^{\prime},h_{w}^{\prime})
be two types
for the same node
t
.
We define the addition of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
as
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}

if the following requirements are met (otherwise,
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}
is undefined):

•

\prtn=\prtn^{\prime}
,

•

H_{i}=H_{i}^{\prime}
(for each
i\in[w]
),

•

h_{i}|_{H_{i}}=h^{\prime}_{i}|_{H^{\prime}_{i}}
(for each
i\in[w]
),
and

•

\{h_{i}(a),h_{i}^{\prime}(a)\}\neq\{\downarrow\}
for all
a\in V(H)
.

Then we define, for all
i\in[w]
,
the combined function
(h_{i}\oplus h_{i}^{\prime})
with

\displaystyle(h_{i}\oplus h_{i}^{\prime})(a)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\uparrow,&h_{i}(a)=h_{i}^{\prime}(a)=\uparrow,\\
v,&h_{i}(a)=h_{i}^{\prime}(a)=v,\\
\downarrow,&(h_{i}(a),h_{i}^{\prime}(a))\in\{(\uparrow,\downarrow),(\downarrow,\uparrow)\},\end{cases}\qquad\forall a\in V(H).

(7.7)

Here the idea is that the way the vertices in
X_{t}
are covered does not change.
If for one type some vertex appears below and for the other above,
then this vertex appears below for the combined type.
If both types require that the vertex appears above,
then this also holds for the combined type.

Formally, we define the final type as

\displaystyle T_{1}\oplus T_{2}\coloneqq(\prtn,H_{1},h_{1}\oplus h_{1}^{\prime},\dots,H_{w},h_{w}\oplus h_{w}^{\prime})

(7.8)

Before we give the algorithm we prove one structural result that allows us to get an improved running time.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
G
be a graph and
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.
Moreover, consider
D\subseteq V_{t}
and
D_{0}=D\cap X_{t}
.
Let
L
be the maximum
T
-completion number taken over all types
T
of some partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
with respect to
X_{t}\setminus D
.
Then, for all
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
,
if there is a partial
H
-packing of type
T
,
then there is a partial
H
-packing of type
T
with at least

\max\{L-\lvert X_{t}\rvert\cdot\lvert V(H)\rvert,0\}
complete copies of
H
.

Proof.

Let
P
be some partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
with respect to
X_{t}\setminus D

containing
L
complete copies of
H
.

Fix some arbitrary type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})

and some partial
H
-packing
Q
of type
T
for
G_{t}-D
. Note that the complete copies of
H
contained in some partial packing do not affect its type. So, by removing all complete copies of
H
in
Q
,
we obtain another partial
H
-packing
Q^{\prime}
of type
T
.
Since each vertex in
X_{t}
can be covered
