by at most one vertex from a copy of
H
in
Q^{\prime}
,
the partial packing
Q^{\prime}
contains (partial) copies of
H
with a total of at most

R\coloneqq\lvert X_{t}\rvert\cdot\lvert V(H)\rvert
vertices.

Now let
P^{\prime}
be the packing obtained from
P

by removing all (partial) copies of
H
that cover some vertex of
G_{t}
that is also covered by a partial copy in
Q^{\prime}
.
Since there are at most
R
vertices covered by partial copies of
Q^{\prime}
,
the partial packing
P^{\prime}
contains at least
L-R
complete copies of
H
.
By the construction of
P^{\prime}
, the packings
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
are vertex-disjoint
and therefore,
P^{\prime}\cup Q^{\prime}
is a partial
H
-packing of type
T

which contains at least
L-R
complete copies of
H
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Now we are ready to state the algorithm for
H
-HitPack

parameterized by treewidth.

7.1
Dynamic Program

Now, we present an algorithm behind the proof of
1.6
.
The algorithm is a dynamic program
based on the tree decomposition of the input graph
and fills a table entry
A[t_{0},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0}]
for all

•

nodes
t_{0}
, integers
k_{0}\in[0..k]
and
\ell_{0}\in[0..\ell]
,

•

subsets
D_{0}\subseteq X_{t_{0}}\setminus U
,

•

functions
f_{0}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})\to[0..(\textup{{tw}}+1)\cdot\lvert V(H)\rvert]\cup\{\bot\}
.

Now, we define featured sets
which are precisely the sets the algorithm counts.

Definition 7.7

(Featured set)
.

We say that a vertex set
D\subseteq V_{t_{0}}\setminus U
with
D_{0}\subseteq D
and
\lvert D\setminus D_{0}\rvert=k_{0}

is
featured in
(t_{0},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0})
if the following two conditions hold:

•

For every type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
with
f_{0}(T)\neq\bot
, the
T
-completion number for
G_{t_{0}}-D

(i.e., the maximum number of complete copies of
H
in a partial

H
-packing of type
T
) is exactly
\ell_{0}-f_{0}(T)
.

•

For every type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
with

f_{0}(T)=\bot
,
there is no partial
H
-packing for
G_{t_{0}}-D
of type
T
.

Henceforth, we define
A[t_{0},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0}]
to be the number of sets
that are
featured
in
(t_{0},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0})
.
This concludes the definition of the dynamic programming table. Note that here
we solve the counting version.
For the decision version, it would suffice if table

A
stores Boolean values.

Let
r
be the root node of the tree decomposition such that the corresponding
bag
X_{r}
does not contain any vertices. Note that the only valid type for
r

is the empty type, which we denote by
(\varnothing)
. Then the algorithm
returns

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{0}=0}^{k}\sum_{\ell_{0}=0}^{\ell-1}A[r,k_{0},\varnothing,\ell_{0},(\varnothing)\mapsto 0],

as the number of solutions for the given instance of
H
-HitPack
.

As the program deals with each table entry separately,
fix some node
t
,
some integer
k_{0}\in[0..k]
,
some set
D_{0}\subseteq X_{t}
,
some integer
\ell_{0}\in[0..\ell]
,
and some function
f\colon\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})\to[0..(\textup{{tw}}+1)\cdot\lvert V(H)\rvert]\cup\{\bot\}
.
For ease of notation we define
R\coloneqq(\textup{{tw}}+1)\cdot\lvert V(H)\rvert

for the range of the integers in the image of
f
.

Leaf Node.

If
t
is a leaf node we set
A[t,0,\varnothing,0,(\varnothing)\mapsto 0]\coloneqq 1
.
For all other combinations we set the table entry to be
0
.

Introduce Node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the child of
t
and let
v
be the vertex introduced at
t
,
that is,
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}\cup\{v\}
.

Case 1:
v\in D_{0}
.

Then, we first define
the function

f^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}\setminus\{v\})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}
such as

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=f(\textsf{extend}(T^{\prime},v\mapsto 0)).

(7.9)

Using this we set the table entry as

\displaystyle A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]

\displaystyle\coloneqq A[t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0}\setminus\{v\},\ell_{0},f^{\prime}].

(7.10)

Case 2:
v\notin D_{0}
.

We first perform two checks for the function
f
.

First, we iterate over all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
. Let
i=\prtn(v)

be the partition of
v
in
T
. If
i=0
, then the check succeeds directly
for this type. Otherwise, we let
a\in V(H_{i})
be the vertex such that

h_{i}(a)=v
in
T
. For all
b\in N_{H_{i}}(v)
, we check if
h_{i}(b)\in N_{G}(v)
. If this condition is true, we proceed to the next type. Otherwise,
we check whether
f(T)=\bot
. If this test fails for any type, we
define the table entry of
A
to be
0
.

As a second step, for all
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})
, we check if there
exists
c_{T^{\prime}}\in[0..R]
such that, for all
T\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\textsf{extend}(T^{\prime},v\mapsto i)
with
f(T)\neq\bot
, it holds that
f(T)=c_{T^{\prime}}
. If this condition is satisfied, we define the function

f^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}
with

f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})=c_{T^{\prime}}
and set

\displaystyle A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]\coloneqq A[t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f^{\prime}]

(7.11)

Otherwise, the table entry is set to
0
.

Forget Node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the unique child of
t
and let
v
be the vertex forgotten,
that is,
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}\setminus\{v\}
.
First, we define function
f_{1}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}\cup\{v\})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}
as

\displaystyle f_{1}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)).

(7.12)

Next, we define function
f_{2}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})\to[0..R+1]\cup\{\bot\}
as

\displaystyle f_{2}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\bot,&\text{if }f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))=\bot,\\
f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))+1,&\text{if }\prtn(v)\neq 0\text{ and }\lvert H_{\prtn(v)}\rvert=1,\\
f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)),&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(7.13)

We define

\displaystyle r_{\min}=\min_{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}):f_{2}(T^{\prime})\neq\bot}f_{2}(T^{\prime})

and

\displaystyle r_{\max}=\max_{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}):f_{2}(T^{\prime})\neq\bot}f_{2}(T^{\prime})

as the smallest and largest value in the image of
f_{2}
respectively.
Now, we have two cases. If
v\in U
, then we can assume that

r_{\max}-r_{\min}\leq R

(otherwise the table entry can be set to
0
directly)
and we fill out the table

\displaystyle A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]

\displaystyle\coloneqq A[t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0}-r_{\min},f_{2}-r_{\min}].

(7.14)

Otherwise, we know that
v\notin U
. If
r_{\max}-r_{\min}\leq R
, then we fill the table entry as

\displaystyle A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]

\displaystyle\coloneqq A[t^{\prime},k_{0}-1,D_{0}\cup\{v\},\ell_{0},f_{1}]+A[t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0}-r_{\min},f_{2}-r_{\min}].

(7.15)

Otherwise we ignore the second part of the definition and set

\displaystyle A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]

\displaystyle\coloneqq A[t^{\prime},k_{0}-1,D_{0}\cup\{v\},\ell_{0},f_{1}].

(7.16)

Join Node.

Now, we describe the join operation.
Let
t_{1},t_{2}
be the two children of
t
with the same bag as
t
.

We need to guarantee that
\bot
propagates correctly. Therefore, we
introduce the following definition.

Definition 7.8

(Compatible Tuples)
.

We say that three integers

\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in[0..\ell]
and
three functions

f,f_{1},f_{2}
from types to
[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}

are
compatible

if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

exist
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
with
f_{1}(T_{1})=f_{2}(T_{2})=0
, and

•

f(T_{1}\oplus T_{2})=\bot
if and only if
f_{1}(T_{1})=\bot
or
f_{2}(T_{2})=\bot
for any types
T_{1},T_{2}
, and

•

\ell_{0}-f(T_{1}\oplus T_{2})=\ell_{1}-f_{1}(T_{1})+\ell_{2}-f_{2}(T_{2})
for all types
T_{1},T_{2}
(if none of the terms is
\bot
).

Now, we let
A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]
be

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in[0..k]:\\
k_{0}=k_{1}+k_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2},f_{1},f_{2}\\
\text{compatible with }\ell_{0},f_{0}\end{subarray}}A[t_{1},k_{1},D_{0},\ell_{1},f_{1}]\cdot A[t_{2},k_{2},D_{0},\ell_{2},f_{2}].

(7.17)

This concludes the description of the dynamic programming recursion.

7.2
Correctness

It remains to prove the correctness of this dynamic program.

Lemma 7.9

(Correctness)
.

For all nodes
t
,
integers
k_{0}\in[0..k]
,
vertex sets
D_{0}\subseteq X_{t}
,
integers
\ell_{0}\in[0..\ell]
,
functions
f\colon\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}
,
and integers
c\geq 0
,
the following two statements are equivalent:

(P.1)

There are exactly
c
pairwise different vertex sets
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus U

with
D_{0}\subseteq D
and
\lvert D\setminus D_{0}\rvert=k_{0}

such that, for all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
,
the
T
-completion number for
G_{t}-D

is exactly
\ell_{0}-f(T)
if
f(T)\neq\bot

or there is no partial
H
-packing of type
T
.

(P.2)

A[t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f]=c
.

Proof.

We prove the statement inductively by handling each possible type
of a node
t
of the tree decomposition individually.

Leaf Node.

There is only one way to select no vertices from the empty set
and hence, the statement is trivially true.

Introduce Node.

We distinguish two subcases based on whether

v
is in
D_{0}
.

Case 1:
v\in D_{0}
.

We first consider the case when
v\in D_{0}
.
Recall that
v
is not part of any (partial) covering in this case.
Recall the definition of function
f^{\prime}

from
Equation

7.9
:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}\setminus\{v\})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}\text{ where }f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})=f(\textsf{extend}(T^{\prime},v\mapsto 0)).

Our goal is to show that
(P.1)
for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f,c)
is equivalent to

(P.1)
for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0}\setminus\{v\},\ell_{0},f^{\prime},c)
. Then, by the induction hypothesis, this is equivalent to

(P.2)
for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0}\setminus\{v\},\ell_{0},f^{\prime},c)
and, by the definition of the table entry from

Equation

7.10
, this is equivalent to

(P.2)
for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f,c)
.

To prove the above equivalence, we define a bijection
\psi
from the
partial solutions for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f)
to the partial solutions for

(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0}\setminus\{v\},\ell_{0},f^{\prime})
. For a partial solution
D
for
t

with the claimed properties from
(P.1)
,

\psi
maps
D
to
D^{\prime}\coloneqq D\setminus\{v\}
as the corresponding partial
solution for
t^{\prime}
.

We first show that
\psi
is well-defined. For some partial solution
D

for
t
and for some type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}\setminus\{v\})
, let
P

be a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-(D\setminus\{v\})

of type
T^{\prime}
with regard to

X_{t^{\prime}}
with the maximum number of complete copies of
H
. Observe that

G_{t^{\prime}}-(D\setminus\{v\})=G_{t}-D
as vertex
v
is introduced at
t
.
Moreover, the type of
P
with regard to
X_{t}
is
\textsf{extend}(T^{\prime},v\mapsto 0)
.
Hence, there are exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{extend}(T^{\prime},v\mapsto 0))
copies of
H
in

P
, which is equal to
\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})
by the definition of
f^{\prime}
.

As injectivity follows from the definition of
\psi
, it remains to show
that
\psi
is surjective. Consider some partial solution
D^{\prime}
for
t^{\prime}

with the properties from
(P.1)
. Fix some
type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
and a partial
H
-packing
P
for

G_{t}-(D^{\prime}\cup\{v\})
of type
T

with the maximum number of complete copies of
H
. Since
P

does not contain
v
, packing
P
has type
\textsf{remove}(T,v)
with regard to

X_{t^{\prime}}
. Hence, it contains at most
\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(\textsf{remove}(T,v))=\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{extend}(\textsf{remove}(T,v),v\mapsto 0))
copies of
H
. Moreover, as it is a
packing with the maximum number of complete copies of
H
, it contains exactly that
many copies of
H
. From

Equations

7.1
and
7.4
, we get
that
\textsf{extend}(\textsf{remove}(T,v),v\mapsto 0)=T
because we assumed that
v\in D_{0}
.
Hence, the right-hand side is equal to
\ell_{0}-f(T)
, and thus, finishes
the proof of the surjectivity.

Case 2:
v\notin D_{0}
.

First, consider the case where for
some type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})
, there are two types
T_{1}\neq T_{2}\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto i)

with
f_{1}(T_{1}),f_{2}(T_{2})\neq\bot
such
that
f(T_{1})\neq f(T_{2})
. We claim that in this case, there is no
solution for
t
with the claimed properties from

(P.1)
. Assume otherwise and let the
solution be
\widetilde{D}
. Without loss of generality, it suffices to
consider the case when
f(T_{1})>f(T_{2})
. Let
P_{2}
be a partial

H
-packing for
G_{t}-\widetilde{D}
of type
T_{2}

with the maximum
T
-completion number.
After removing
v
from
P_{2}
, we get a packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-\widetilde{D}
.
This packing
P_{2}-v
can be extended to a partial
H
-packing for

G_{t}-\widetilde{D}
of type
T_{1}

by choosing the packing for
v
appropriately. Let
P_{2}^{\prime}

be this new packing. Since
P_{2}^{\prime}
and
P_{2}
contain the same number of
complete copies of
H
,
packing
P_{2}^{\prime}
contains
\ell_{0}-f(T_{2})>\ell_{0}-f(T_{1})
copies
of
H
. But this contradicts the assumption that the
T_{1}
-completion number for
G_{t}-\widetilde{D}
is exactly
\ell_{0}-f(T_{1})
.
Thus, in this case, we have correctly set the value of the table entry to

0
.

Hence, for each type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})
,
there is a constant
c_{T^{\prime}}
such that,
the function
f^{\prime}
is defined as

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}\text{ where }f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})=c_{T^{\prime}}.

As for the first case, our goal is to show that

(P.1)
for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f,c)

is equivalent to
(P.1)
for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f^{\prime},c)
. Then, the induction hypothesis implies that the
latter result is equivalent to
(P.2)

for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f^{\prime},c)
, and by the definition of the table
entry in
Equation

7.11
, we get that this is
equivalent to
(P.2)
for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f,c)
.

In the following, we show that every partial solution
D
for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f)
is also a partial solution for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f^{\prime})
and vice versa, that is, we prove a bijection between the two sets of
solutions.

Let
D
be a partial solution for
t
. Consider some type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})
.
Fix some arbitrary type
\widetilde{T}\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\textsf{extend}(T^{\prime},v\mapsto i)\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
.
We can extend all partial
H
-packings for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D
of type
T^{\prime}

to a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
\widetilde{T}

by extending the packing for
v
according to
\widetilde{T}
because

v
is not yet covered by the packing. Hence, the
T^{\prime}
-completion
number for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D
is at most the
\widetilde{T}
-completion number
for
G_{t}-D
, which is precisely
\ell_{0}-f(\widetilde{T})=\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})
.

Now, let
\widetilde{P}
be the partial
H
-packing of type
\widetilde{T}
containing the
largest number of complete copies of
H
. By our assumptions about
f
, the packing

\widetilde{P}
contains exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\widetilde{T})
copies of
H
. Removing
the vertex
v
from
\widetilde{P}
,
we get a new partial
H
-packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

of type
\textsf{remove}(\widetilde{T},v)
with regard to
X_{t^{\prime}}
. By our
choice of
\widetilde{T}
, we get
T^{\prime}=\textsf{remove}(\widetilde{T},v)
, and thus, the
packing also have type
T^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the
T^{\prime}
-completion number is exactly
\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})
.

Next, we prove the reverse direction. Consider some partial solution
D^{\prime}

for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f^{\prime})
.
Fix some type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})

and let
P
be a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D^{\prime}
of type
T

with the maximum number of complete copies.
Observe that the number of complete copies of
H
does not change
if we remove
v
from
P
.
Moreover,
P-v
has type
\textsf{remove}(T,v)
with regard to
X_{t^{\prime}}
.
Hence, the number of complete copies of
H
in
P

is at most
\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(\textsf{remove}(T,v))
.
From
Equations

7.1
,
7.2
,
7.3
,
7.4
and
7.5
,
we get that this is at most
\ell_{0}-f(T)
.
We conclude that
D^{\prime}
is a partial solution for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f)
.

Let
P^{\prime}
be some partial
H
-packing of type
\textsf{remove}(T,v)
containing the
largest number of complete copies of
H
. By our assumptions about
f^{\prime}
, the
packing
P^{\prime}
contains exactly
\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(T^{\prime})
copies of
H
. Since
v

is not covered in
P^{\prime}
, we can extend
P^{\prime}
to a new partial

H
-packing
P
of type
T
by choosing the packing for
v
according to
T
. We see
that
P
and
P^{\prime}
contain the same number of complete copies of
H
, which is equal
to
\ell_{0}-f^{\prime}(\textsf{remove}(T,v))
, and by the definition of
f^{\prime}
it is also
equal to
\ell_{0}-f(T)
.

Forget Node.

Assume out of the
c
partial solutions from

(P.1)

c_{1}
additionally satisfy
v\in D
and
the remaining
c_{2}\coloneqq c-c_{1}
satisfy
v\notin D
.
Recall,
from
Equations

7.12
and
7.13
,
that the function
f_{1}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}\cup\{v\})\to[0..R]\cup\{\bot\}
satisfies

\displaystyle f_{1}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)),

and the function
f_{2}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})\to[0..R+1]\cup\{\bot\}
satisfies

\displaystyle f_{2}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\bot,&\text{ if }f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))=\bot,\\
f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))+1,&\text{ if }\prtn(v)\neq 0\text{ and }\lvert H_{\prtn(v)}\rvert=1,\\
f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)),&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Also recall that

r_{\min}
is the smallest integer from the image of
f_{2}

and
r_{\max}
is the largest integer in the image.

We claim that if
r_{\max}-r_{\min}>R
, then
c_{2}=0
.
Assume otherwise and let
D
be such a solution
satisfying the constraints in
(P.1)
.
Let
T_{\min}\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
be a type
such that
f(T_{\min})=r_{\min}

and
T_{\max}\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
analogously for
r_{\max}
.
Since there is a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
T_{\max}

and a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
T_{\min}

with
\ell_{0}-f(T_{\min})
copies of
H
,
by
Lemma

7.6
,
the
T_{\max}
-completion number is at least

\ell_{0}-f(T_{\min})-R>\ell_{0}-r_{\max}
.
But this contradicts the assumption that the
T_{\max}
-completion number is exactly
\ell_{0}-f(T_{\max})=\ell_{0}-r_{\max}
.
Hence, we can safely assume in the following that

r_{\max}-r_{\min}\leq R
.
Moreover, observe that subtracting
r_{\min}
from
\ell_{0}
and
f_{2}

is only needed to adjust the range of the function
f_{2}

to be in
[0..R]
.
Hence, we omit this from the proof in the following
to keep notation simple.

Assume that
v\notin U
first (the case when
v\in U
is analogous).
Our goal is to show that
(P.1)

for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f,c)

is equivalent to the two combined properties

(P.1)

for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{1},c_{1})

and
(P.1)

for
(t^{\prime},k_{0}-1,D_{0}+v,\ell_{0},f_{2},c_{2})
together.
Then we can use the induction hypothesis
to get the equivalence to
(P.2)

for both cases
and then by the definition of the dynamic programming table
A
this is equivalent to

(P.2)

for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f,c)
.

For both settings we show that the partial solutions for
t

precisely coincide with the partial solutions for
t^{\prime}
,
that is, there is a bijection between these partial solutions.
Nevertheless, we handle both cases separately.

Case 1:
v
is deleted.

Fix some partial solution
D
with
v\in D

and the properties from
(P.1)
.
Consider some type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0}\cup\{v\})
.
Observe that
G_{t^{\prime}}-D=G_{t}-D
and hence,
every partial
H
-packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D
of type
T^{\prime}

is also a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)

with regard to
X_{t}
.
Therefore, the
T^{\prime}
-completion number for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

is at most the
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)
-completion number for
G_{t}-D

which is by assumption
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))

and by definition of
f_{1}
this is equal to
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T^{\prime})
.

Moreover,
let
P
be the partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)

with the maximum number of complete copies of
H
.
Since
P
is also a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D
of type
T^{\prime}
,
the
T^{\prime}
-completion number of
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

is at least
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))=\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T^{\prime})
.

Now consider some partial solution
D^{\prime}
for

(t^{\prime},k_{0}-1,D_{0}\cup\{v\},\ell_{0},f_{1})
.
Pick some arbitrary type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
.
Let
P
be some partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D^{\prime}
of type
T
.
Then, since
v\in D^{\prime}
, we get that
P
is a partial

H
-packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D^{\prime}
of type
\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0)
.
By assumption the number of complete copies of
H
in
P

is at most
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0))

which is, by the definition of
f_{1}
,
equal to
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0),v))

and by

Equations

7.1
and
7.4

equal to
\ell_{0}-f(T)
.

Let
P^{\prime}
be some partial
H
-packing for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D^{\prime}

of type
\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0)

with the maximum number of complete copies of
H
.
Since
v
is not contained in the packing,
this is also a partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D^{\prime}
of type
T

containing
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto 0))
copies of
H
.
Hence, by the definition of
f_{1}
,
the
T
-completion number is exactly
\ell_{0}-f(T)
.

Case 2:
v
is not deleted.

Consider some partial solution
D
with
v\notin D

for
(t,k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f)
.
We show that
D
is also a partial solution for

(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{2})
.
Consider some type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})

with
\prtn(v)\neq 0
and
\lvert H_{\prtn(v)}\rvert=1
.
Pick some partial
H
-packing
P^{\prime}
for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D
of type
T^{\prime}
.
By our assumption we know that
v
is covered by a copy of
H

that is entirely contained in
P^{\prime}
.
Hence, with respect to
X_{t}
there is one more copy of
H
in
P^{\prime}

then with respect to
X_{t^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, the packing
P^{\prime}
has type
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)

with regard to
X_{t}
.
Hence, the
T^{\prime}
-completion number for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

is at most the
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)
-completion number
for
G_{t}-D
minus
1
;
by assumption this number is equal to
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))-1

which is equal to
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})
by the definition of
f_{2}
.

On the other side,
let
P
be the partial
H
-packing of type
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)

containing exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))
copies of
H
.
We directly get that
P
is a partial
H
-packing of type
T^{\prime}

containing exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))-1
copies of
H
.
Hence, by the definition of
f_{2}

from
Equation

7.13
,
the
T^{\prime}
-completion number of
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

is exactly
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})
.

Now consider some type
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t^{\prime}}(D_{0})
,
with
\prtn(T^{\prime})=0
or
\lvert H_{\prtn(v)}\rvert>1
.
Again pick some partial
H
-packing
P^{\prime}
for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D
of type
T^{\prime}
.
Since
v
is either not covered by any (partial) copy of
H

or part of a (partial) copy covering at least one more vertex from
X_{t^{\prime}}
,
the number of complete copies of
H
in
P^{\prime}
with respect to
X_{t^{\prime}}

is equal to the number of complete copies of
H
in
P^{\prime}
with respect to
X_{t}
.
Moreover, observe that
P^{\prime}
has type
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)

with regard to
X_{t}
.
Hence, the
T^{\prime}
-completion number for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

is at most the
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)
-completion number
for
G_{t}-D
which is, by assumption, exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))

and this is equal to
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})
by the definition of
f_{2}
.

On the other side,
let
P
be the partial
H
-packing of type
\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v)

containing exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))
copies of
H
.
Since
v
is either not covered
or part of a partial packing containing some other vertex from
X_{t}
,
the packing
P
is a partial
H
-packing of type
T^{\prime}

containing exactly
\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))-1
copies of
H
.
Hence, by the definition of
f_{2}

from
Equation

7.13
,
the
T^{\prime}
-completion number of
G_{t^{\prime}}-D

is exactly
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})
.

For the reverse direction consider some partial solution
D^{\prime}

for
(t^{\prime},k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{2})
.
Let
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
be some type
and
P
some partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D^{\prime}
of type
T
.
There is a unique type
T^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\textsf{extend}(T,v\mapsto i)

such that
P
is a partial
H
-covering for
G_{t^{\prime}}-D^{\prime}
of type
T^{\prime}
.
The number of complete copies of
H
in
P
with respect to
X_{t^{\prime}}

is at most
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})
.
Observe that when
v
is not covered
or covered by a copy of
H
also covering other vertices from
X_{t^{\prime}}
,
then
P
contains as many copies of
H
with respect to
X_{t^{\prime}}

as with respect to
X_{t}
.
By the definition of
f_{2}
,
we get
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})=\ell_{0}-f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))
.
Otherwise, we know that
P
with respect to
X_{t}

contains one copy of
H
more than
P
with respect to
X_{t^{\prime}}
.
By assumption the latter is at most
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})-1

which is equal to
\ell_{0}-(f(\textsf{remove}(T^{\prime},v))+1)-1
.
Using
Equations

7.2
and
7.5

we conclude that in both cases
the
T
-completion number is at most
\ell_{0}-f(T)
.

On the other side,
let
P^{\prime}
be a partial
H
-packing of type
T^{\prime}

with
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T^{\prime})
complete copies of
H
.
By an argument similar to the ones before,
we get that
P^{\prime}
contains
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T)
complete copies of
H

with respect to
X_{t}
.
Hence, the
T
-completion number is exactly
\ell_{0}-f_{2}(T)
.

Join Node

Let
D
be one of the
c
different partial solutions. We define two new
sets
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
as
D_{1}\coloneqq D\cap V_{t_{1}}
and
D_{2}\coloneqq D\cap V_{t_{2}}
.
By the structure of the tree decomposition, we have
D_{1}\cap D_{2}=D_{0}
.
We choose
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
as
k_{1}=\lvert D_{1}\setminus D_{0}\rvert
and
k_{2}=\lvert D_{2}\setminus D_{0}\rvert
.

Our first goal is to construct two functions,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, and define
two integers
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
in
[0..\ell]
such that

\ell_{1},\ell_{2},f_{1},f_{2}
are compatible with
\ell_{0},f_{0}
.
Moreover, we define them
such that
D_{1}
is one of the
c_{1}
partial solutions
for
(t_{1},k_{1},D_{0},\ell_{1},f_{1})

from
(P.1)

and
D_{2}
is one of the
c_{2}
partial solutions
for
(t_{2},k_{2},D_{0},\ell_{2},f_{2})

from
(P.1)
.

For all
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
,
let
\mathcal{P}(T)
be the set of all possible partial
H
-packings
for
G_{t}-D
of type
T
.
For all types
T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{0})
and
T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{2}}(D_{0})
,
we define the following two sets of partial packings:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(T\to T_{1})\coloneqq\{P^{\prime}=P\cap V_{t_{1}}\mid P\in\mathcal{P}(T),P^{\prime}\text{ has type $T_{1}$ w.r.t.\ }X_{t_{1}}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(T\to T_{2})\coloneqq\{P^{\prime}=P\cap V_{t_{2}}\mid P\in\mathcal{P}(T),P^{\prime}\text{ has type $T_{2}$ w.r.t.\ }X_{t_{2}}\}

Based on this definition,
for all
T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{0})
,
we consider all packings in
\mathcal{P}(T\to T_{1})

and define
\lambda(T\to T_{1})
as the maximum number of complete copies of
H

in these packings.
Likewise, we define the value
\lambda(T\to T_{2})

based on
\mathcal{P}(T\to T_{2})
for all
T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{2}}(D_{0})
.
We set

\ell_{1}\coloneqq\max_{T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{0})}\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})}\lambda(T\to T_{1})\quad\text{ and }\quad\ell_{2}\coloneqq\max_{T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{2}}(D_{0})}\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})}\lambda(T\to T_{2}).

We define the two functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
as

\displaystyle f_{1}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{0})\to[0..\ell]

\displaystyle\text{ with }f_{1}(T_{1})=\ell_{1}-\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})}\lambda(T\to T_{1})\text{
and}

\displaystyle f_{2}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t_{2}}(D_{0})\to[0..\ell]

\displaystyle\text{ with }f_{2}(T_{2})=\ell_{2}-\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})}\lambda(T\to T_{2}).

We first argue that the image of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
is actually contained in

[0..R]
as otherwise, there would be no solution. Assuming there is a
solution, we directly deduce from

Lemma

7.6
that the
T
-completion numbers for different types
T
differ by at most
R
. Hence, if one type
violated this property, it would lead to a contradiction.

Next, we prove that
D_{1}
is a solution for
(t_{1},k_{1},D_{0},\ell_{1},f_{1})

and
D_{2}
is a solution for
(t_{2},k_{2},D_{0},\ell_{2},f_{2})
. Since the
proofs for both cases are almost identical, we will focus only on the
proof for
t_{1}
.

Let
T_{1}
be some type in
\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{0})
, and let
P_{1}
be a
partial
H
-packing for
G_{t_{1}}-D_{1}
of type
T_{1}
.
Choose
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})

such that
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T_{1})=\ell_{1}-\lambda(T\to T_{1})
, and let
P

be the corresponding partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
T
.
Now, observe
that
P^{\prime}\coloneqq(P\cap V_{t_{2}})\cup P_{1}
is also a partial packing for

G_{t}-D
and has type
T_{1}
with regard to
X_{t_{1}}
. Therefore,
P^{\prime}\cap V_{t_{1}}
is a packing of type
T_{1}
and thus is contained in
\mathcal{P}(T\to T_{1})
. Moreover, this packing contains at most
\ell_{1}-f_{1}(T_{1})
copies
of
H
according to the definition of
f_{1}
.
Since

P^{\prime}\cap V_{t_{1}}=((P\cap V_{t_{2}})\cup P_{1})\cap V_{t_{1}}=P_{1},

this implies that the
T_{1}
-completion number is at most
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T_{1})
.

By the definition of the function
f_{1}
, it directly follows that there is
a partial
H
-packing of type
T_{1}

containing
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T_{1})
complete copies
of
H
. Hence, combined with the previous result, the
T_{1}
-completion number is exactly
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T_{1})
.

For the reverse direction, fix some
k_{1},k_{2}
with
k_{1}+k_{2}=k_{0}
,

\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
, and
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
such that, for all
T_{1},T_{2}
with
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}=T
,
it holds that
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},f_{1},f_{2}
are compatible with
\ell_{0},f_{0}
.
We show that if there is a partial solution
D_{1}
for
(t_{1},k_{1},D_{0},\ell_{1},f_{1})
and a partial solution
D_{2}
for
(t_{2},k_{2},D_{0},\ell_{2},f_{2})
, then the set
D_{1}\cup D_{2}
is a partial solution for
(t,k_{1}+k_{2},D_{0},\ell_{0},f)
.

For ease of notation, we set
D=D_{1}\cup D_{2}
. Now consider some type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})

and some partial
H
-packing for
G_{t}-D
of type
T
.
As a
first step, we observe that
P\cap V_{t_{1}}
is a partial packing for

G_{t_{1}}-D=G_{t_{1}}-D_{1}
. Since the type of
P\cap V_{t_{1}}
is
T_{1}

for some
T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{0})
, the number of complete copies of
H
in
P\cap V_{t_{1}}
is at most
\ell_{1}-f_{1}(T_{1})
. Similarly, the number of
complete copies in
P\cap V_{t_{2}}
is at most
\ell_{2}-f_{2}(T_{2})
, where
T_{2}
is
such that
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}=T
. Hence, the number of complete copies
of
H
in
P
is at most
\ell_{1}-f_{1}(T_{1})+\ell_{2}-f_{2}(T_{2})=\ell_{0}-f(T)
.

Next, we show that the bound for the
T
-completion number is
tight. Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be types such that
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}=T
.
Choose a partial
H
-packing
P_{1}
of type
T_{1}

containing
\ell_{1}-f_{1}(T_{1})
copies of
H
.
Likewise, let
P_{2}
be a partial
H
-packing of type
T_{2}
with

\ell_{2}-f_{2}(T_{2})
complete copies of
H
. It follows that
P_{1}\cup P_{2}

is a partial
H
-packing of type
T

with
\ell_{0}-f_{1}(T_{1})+\ell_{2}-f_{2}(T_{2})

complete copies of
H
. Hence, the
T
-completion number for

G_{t}-D
is exactly
\ell_{0}-f(T)
.

Now, for all tuples
(t_{1},k_{1},D_{0},f_{1})
and
(t_{2},k_{2},D_{0},f_{2})
, we
can use the induction hypothesis to conclude that

(P.1)
is equivalent to

(P.2)
. By the definition of the table
entry from
Equation

7.17
, this is equivalent to

(P.2)
. This concludes the
proof of correctness of the dynamic program.
∎

7.3
Running Time

As the last step we prove the bound on the running time
of the dynamic program.

Claim 7.10
.

For a fixed graph
H
, the above algorithm runs in time

2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}}\cdot\poly(n)

to fill the table
A
.

Proof of Claim .

By
Lemma

7.5
,
for each node and each subset of the bag there are at most

\tau\coloneqq\textup{{tw}}^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\poly\lvert H\rvert)}

different types.
Hence, the size of the table
A
is bounded by

\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\textup{{tw}})\cdot k\cdot 2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\cdot(\ell+1)\cdot(R+1)^{\tau}\leq 2^{\tau\cdot\mathcal{O}(\log(R+1))}\cdot\poly(n).

Now we analyze the time it takes to compute a single table entry.
Observe, that is suffices to check the join nodes,
as this running time dominates the running time for the other node types.
Using a brute-force approach to compute the entry
and a naive bound for the running time yields

k^{2}\cdot((R+1)^{\tau})^{2}\cdot\tau^{2}\leq 2^{3\cdot\tau\cdot\log(R+1)}\cdot\poly(n).

Combined with the first observation from above,
we get that the algorithm has the following runtime:

\displaystyle\left(2^{3\cdot\tau\cdot\log(R+1)}\cdot\poly(n)\right)\cdot\left(2^{\tau\cdot\log(R+1)}\cdot\poly(n)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{4\cdot\tau\cdot\log(R+1)}\cdot\poly(n)

Note, that this is at most

2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\cdot\log\textup{{tw}}\cdot\poly\lvert H\rvert)}}\cdot\poly(n)
.
The claimed running time follows, since
H
is a fixed graph.
∎

The proof of
1.6

now follows from
Lemmas

7.9
and
7.10
.

8
Cycle
-HitPack
Parameterized by Treewidth:

Double-Exponential Algorithm

In this section we extend the result from
Section

7

to the case where we want to pack cycles of arbitrary length
(at least length
3
).
Formally, we prove
1.2
.

See
1.2

The underlying idea for this algorithm is the same as for the other algorithms
for cliques and finite graphs. However, as we are not packing a fixed, single
finite graph
H
but rather an infinitely sized family of graphs, we need a
different way to represent the intersection of a partial packing with the
vertices and edges from a bag. For this description, we heavily exploit that we
are packing cycles and not some other family of graphs that could be
unstructured or significantly more complex to describe, for example, the family
of planar graphs.

The crucial observation is that every partial cycle-packing consists of a
collection of cycles and paths. Hence, the vertices in a bag (and actually all
other vertices) can be grouped into three groups, which can intuitively be
described as follows.

1.

The group of vertices that are not part of any cycle or path
(this includes the deleted vertices).

2.

The group of vertices that are the endpoints of a path
that is later extended to a cycle.

3.

The group of vertices that are contained in a cycle
or contained in a path but not as their endpoints.

As a next step, we first formalize this idea by introducing some notation. Since
the final algorithm operates on a tree decomposition, we define the notation
only in this context.

Definition 8.1

(Partial Cycle-Packing)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.

A
partial cycle-packing
for
G_{t}
(or just
t
for short)
is a set
\mathcal{P}

of vertex disjoint paths and cycles in
G_{t}

such that for every path
P
in
\mathcal{P}
,
the endpoints of
P
are contained in the bag
X_{t}
.

Depending on how a partial cycle-packing interacts with the vertices in a bag,
we define different types to classify the different cycle-packings.
See
Figure

8.1
for an illustration
of an example for a type.

Definition 8.2

(Types and
D
-avoidance)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.
A
type

T
for
t
is defined as a tuple
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)

where
Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2}
form a partition of
X_{t}

and
M
is a perfect matching for the vertices in
Y_{1}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a partial cycle-packing for
G_{t}
.
We set

•

Y_{0}\coloneqq\{v\in V(\mathcal{P})\cap X_{t}\mid\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(v)=0\}

as the set of all vertices in
X_{t}

that are not part of any path or cycle in
\mathcal{P}
,

•

Y_{1}\coloneqq\{v\in V(\mathcal{P})\cap X_{t}\mid\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(v)=1\}

as the set of all vertices in
X_{t}

that are endpoints of a path in
\mathcal{P}
,

•

Y_{2}\coloneqq\{v\in V(\mathcal{P})\cap X_{t}\mid\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(v)=2\}

as the set of all vertices in
X_{t}

that are either in a path (but not as endpoints) or in a cycle,
and

•

M\coloneqq\{\{u,v\}\in Y_{1}\times Y_{1}\mid u\neq v,\exists P\in\mathcal{P}:u,v\in P\}

as the perfect matching on
Y_{1}

describing which endpoints appear in the same path of the partial packing.

Then we say that
\mathcal{P}
is of type
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)
for
t
.
If for some set
D\subseteq X_{t}
it holds that
D\subseteq Y_{0}
,
then we say that
type
T
is
D
-avoiding
.
We denote the set of all
D
-avoiding types for
t
by
\mathcal{T}_{t}(D)
.

When considering partial cycle-packings of a certain type,
we want to formally describe the number of cycles that potentially could be packed
in the remaining graph while preserving this type.
We formalize this by the
T
-completion number.

Definition 8.3

(
T
-completion number)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.

For a type
T
for
t
,
we define the
T
-completion number of
G_{t}
,
denoted by
\nu^{\circ}(T)
,
as the maximum number of
complete
cycles in
\mathcal{P}

over all possible cycle-packings
\mathcal{P}
of type
T
for
G_{t}
.
The number of partial cycles, i.e., paths, is not included in this count.

Intuitively the completion number of different types can vary
and for some types its “low” while for others its “high”.
Our next step is to define what we mean by a “low” completion number
which then transfers to the corresponding type.

Definition 8.4

(Low Types)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.
Consider two
D
-avoiding types
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D)
for
t

such that (1) type
\widehat{T}
maximizes the
\widehat{T}
-completion number
and (2) type
T
is such that
\nu^{\circ}(T)<\nu^{\circ}(\widehat{T})-2(\textup{{tw}}+1)
.
Then we say that
T
is low (for
t
)
.

We first prove that low types are not relevant
for cycle-packings of maximum size.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.
Consider a type
T
for
t
that is low.
Then, there is no
maximum
cycle-packing
\mathcal{P}
for
G

such that
\mathcal{P}
has type
T
restricted to
G_{t}
.

Proof.

Consider a maximum cycle-packing
\mathcal{P}
for the entire graph
G
.
Assume for contradiction’s sake
that for some node
t
of the tree decomposition
the type of
\mathcal{P}
restricted to
G_{t}
is
T

and
T
is low for
t
.
We claim that
\nu^{\circ}(T)<\nu^{\circ}(T_{\varnothing})-(\textup{{tw}}+1)

where
T_{\varnothing}=(X_{t},\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing)

is the type for
t

where no vertex of the bag is contained in the packing.

First, let
\widehat{T}
be a type for
t
proving that
T
is low,
that is, type
\widehat{T}
maximizes the
\widehat{T}
-completion number
and let
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
be a corresponding cycle-packing.
Removing all paths and cycles from
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
that intersect
X_{t}
,
yields a (partial) cycle-packing of type
T_{\varnothing}
.
Because of the removed paths and cycles
we get
\nu^{\circ}(T_{\varnothing})\geq\nu^{\circ}(\widehat{T})-(\textup{{tw}}+1)
.
Hence, from

\nu^{\circ}(T)<\nu^{\circ}(\widehat{T})-2(\textup{{tw}}+1)\leq\nu^{\circ}(T_{\varnothing})-(\textup{{tw}}+1)

the claim follows.

Now we move back to proving the statement.
We define
\mathcal{P}_{0}
as the restriction of
\mathcal{P}
to the graph
G_{t}

and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
as the restriction of
\mathcal{P}
to the graph
G-G_{t}

where we additionally remove all paths.
For convenience, we denote the type of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
for
G-G_{t}
by
\overline{T}
.
Additionally,
consider a (partial) cycle-packing
\mathcal{P}_{\varnothing}
of type
T_{\varnothing}

containing
\nu^{\circ}(T_{\varnothing})
complete cycles.

With these definitions at hand,
we define a new packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
as the union
of
\mathcal{P}_{\varnothing}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.
Clearly
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a cycle-packing for the graph
G

as the packing
\mathcal{P}_{\varnothing}
does not cover vertices from the bag.
Next we analyze the relation between the size of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
From the above constructions and assumptions we get

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\nu^{\circ}(T_{0})+\lvert\overline{\mathcal{P}}\rvert+(\textup{{tw}}+1)

\displaystyle<\nu^{\circ}(T_{\varnothing})-(\textup{{tw}}+1)+\nu^{\circ}(\overline{T})+(\textup{{tw}}+1)

\displaystyle=\nu^{\circ}(T_{\varnothing})+\nu^{\circ}(\overline{T})

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\rvert

which proves that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
contains more cycles than
\mathcal{P}
.
This contradicts our assumption that
\mathcal{P}
is a maximum cycle-packing,
no node of the decomposition has a type that is low.
∎

Since we are actually solving the
Cycle
-HitPack
problem,
we are not interested in just packing cycles
but rather in deleting vertices such that
we cannot pack to many cycles in the remaining graph.
Therefore, it is necessary to argue about all partial cycle-packings
for some graph
G_{t}
at the same time.
This idea is formalized by the concept of classes.

Definition 8.6

(Classes)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition.

Moreover, let
k_{0}\in[0..\lvert V(G)\rvert]
be a non-negative integer,

D_{0}
a subset of vertices from
X_{t}
,

\ell_{0}\in[0..\lvert V(G)\rvert]
be a non-negative integer,
and
f_{0}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})\to[0..2\textup{{tw}}+2]\cup\{\bot,{\curlyveeuparrow}\}
.

We say that
a set
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus U

is of class
c_{0}=(k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0})
for
t

if

•

\lvert D\setminus D_{0}\rvert=k_{0}
and
D\cap X_{t}=D_{0}
,

•

there is a type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
with
f_{0}(T)=0
,
and

•

for all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})
,

–

if there is no partial cycle-packing of type
T
for
t
,
then
f_{0}(T)=\bot
,

–

if
T
is low for
t

or, for every partial packing
\mathcal{P}
of type
T
for
t
,
there is a descendant
t^{\prime}
of
t

such that the type of
\mathcal{P}
for
t^{\prime}
is low,
then
f_{0}(T)={\curlyveeuparrow}
,
or

–

otherwise if
the
T
-completion number for
G_{t}
is exactly
\ell_{0}-a

for some
a\in[0..2\textup{{tw}}+2]
,
then
f_{0}(T)=a
.

To simplify notation we assume that
\bot
and
{\curlyveeuparrow}

are invariant under arithmetic operations
where
\bot
takes precedence over
{\curlyveeuparrow}
,
that is,
{\curlyveeuparrow}+\bot=\bot
.

(a)

An example for a partial cycle-packing for
G_{t}
.

The bold edges correspond to the edges in the partial packing.

(b)

A graphical representation of the type

(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)

of the depicted partial packing.

The small group of the three round vertices at the top middle corresponds to
Y_{0}
,
the vertices at the bottom represent
Y_{2}
,
and the remaining vertices form
Y_{1}
.
The matching
M
between the vertices in
Y_{1}

is represented by the dashed edges.

Figure 8.1
:

An illustration for a node
t

of how a partial cycle-packing and its type relate to each other.

The black vertices and edges correspond to the edges and vertices of
G
.
The deleted vertices are indicated by hollow dots.

Before stating the algorithm, we provide a bound for the number of
D
-avoiding types and classes for a fixed node
t
.
These bounds follow directly from the definition of the two concepts.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
G
be a graph
and let
t
be a node of its tree decomposition of width
tw
.

For a set
D\subseteq X_{t}
, the number of
D
-avoiding types for
t
,
i.e., the size of
\mathcal{T}_{t}(D)
, is at most
\tau\in 2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}
.

The number of classes for
t
is at most

2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}}\cdot n^{2}
.

Proof.

We start by bounding the number of
D
-avoiding types for a fixed set
D

and then use this result to bound the number of classes.

The number of partitions of
X_{t}
into the three sets
Y_{0}
,
Y_{1}
, and
Y_{1}

is bounded by
3^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
.
The choices for the set
M
which contains a perfect matching for
Y_{1}
,
is bounded by the number of permutations on
\lvert Y_{1}\rvert
elements.
Hence, the number of types is at most

\tau\coloneqq 3^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\cdot(\textup{{tw}}+1)^{\textup{{tw}}+1}=2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}
.

To bound the number of classes
(k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0})
for
t
,
observe that there are at most
n+1
choices for
k_{0}
,
exactly
2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}
choices for the set
D_{0}
,
at most
n+1
choices for the integer
\ell_{0}
,
and at most
(2(\textup{{tw}}+1)+3)^{\tau}
choices for the function
f_{0}
.
Combining this yields that there are at most

n\cdot 2^{\textup{{tw}}+1}\cdot n\cdot(2\textup{{tw}}+5)^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}}=2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}}\cdot n^{2}

different classes for a node
t
.
∎

8.1
Auxiliary procedures

The algorithm for
Cycle
-HitPack
is a dynamic program
based on a given tree decomposition.
We state the program in a bottom-up manner.
For this we define five different procedures
each of which compute the value for the parent node
based on the values for the child nodes.
To simplify the procedures we make use of introduce edge nodes
and assume that each edge is introduced exactly once.

\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{del}}
.

The introduced vertex is deleted and
thus, included in the part of the partition
corresponding to uncovered vertices.
See
Lemma

8.8
.

\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
.

The introduced vertex is not deleted.
Since we are using introduce edge nodes, the vertex is isolated and
must be contained in the set corresponding to uncovered vertices
for all types.
See
Lemma

8.9
.

\mathsf{IntroEdge}_{\mathsf{del}}
.

The endpoints of the introduced edge are deleted
and thus, the edge cannot be included in any packing.
See
Lemma

8.10
.

\mathsf{IntroEdge}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
.

Both endpoints of the introduced edge are not deleted.
Thus, this edge can be included in the possible packings
but it can also not be included in a packing.
See
Lemma

8.11
.

\mathsf{Forget}_{\mathsf{del}}
.

The vertex forgotten at the node is deleted.
By forgetting this vertex we have to change the budget
for the number of vertices being deleted.
See
Lemma

8.12
.

\mathsf{Forget}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
.

The vertex forgotten at the node is not deleted.
As the new class forgets whether the vertex was used in a packing
or remained uncovered, two types for the child
combine into one type for the parent.
See
Lemma

8.13
.

\mathsf{Join}
.

The two classes for the two child nodes are combined into a new class
for the parent where the existing paths might be joined to paths or cycles.
See
Lemma

8.16
.

We start with the introduce vertex nodes where we have two different cases.
The first case corresponds to the choice that
v
is deleted.

Lemma 8.8

(Introduce Vertex Node With a Deleted Vertex)
.

Let
t_{0}
be an introduce vertex node with child
t_{1}

and let
v\in V_{t_{0}}\setminus U
be the vertex introduced.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{del}}
that,
for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau)

a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1}\cup\{v\},\ell_{1},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D\cup\{v\}
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

We define the function
f_{0}
as follows.
For all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{1})

with
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)

we set

f_{0}(Y_{0}\cup\{v\},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)\coloneqq f_{1}(T).

Since vertex
v
does not appear in the graph
G_{t_{1}}
,
and is deleted from
G_{t_{0}}
by considering
D_{0}=D_{1}\cup\{v\}
,
the graphs
G_{t_{1}}-D_{1}
and
G_{t_{0}}-D_{0}
are identical.
Hence, if the set
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D\cup\{v\}
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.
∎

The second case for the introduce vertex node corresponds to the case
when the vertex is not deleted.

Lemma 8.9

(Introduce Vertex Node With a Usable Vertex)
.

Let
t_{0}
be an introduce vertex node with child
t_{1}

and let
v\in V_{t_{0}}
be the vertex introduced.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
that,
for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

Observe that
v
is an isolated vertex in
G_{t_{0}}
.
Hence, it especially does not have any neighbors
in
G_{t_{0}}-X_{t_{0}}
.
This implies that all partial solutions for
t_{0}

cannot include
v
in a partial cycle-packing.
Thus, all types for
t_{0}
can be described by all types for
t_{1}

where we add
v
to the set of uncovered vertices.

Observe that the intuitive idea agrees with the setting
from the previous case, where the vertex is deleted.
However, since in the current case the introduced vertex
might receive some edge at some point in the future,
the sets of types are strictly speaking not identical
and thus, both cases need to be handled separately.

Based on this observation, we define the new function
f_{0}
as follows.
For all
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{1})

with
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)
,
we set

f_{0}(Y_{0}\cup\{v\},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)\coloneqq f_{1}(T).

With this definition it follows directly
that every set
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
with class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}

is also of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.
∎

Next we consider the introduce edge nodes
where we also provide two different procedures.
The first one corresponds to the case where the edge cannot be selected
in a packing because at least one of its endpoints is deleted.

Lemma 8.10

(Introduce Edge Node With a Deleted Endpoint)
.

Let
t_{0}
be an introduce edge node with child
t_{1}

and let
uv
be the edge introduced.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{IntroEdge}_{\mathsf{del}}
that,
for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U

with
D\cap\{u,v\}\neq\varnothing
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

If (at least) one of the endpoints of the introduced edge is deleted,
i.e., contained in the set
D_{1}
and thus,
D
,
the edge cannot be part of any (partial) cycle-packing.
Hence, the class of any such set
D
does not change.
∎

The next procedure handles the case when both endpoints of the introduced edge
are not deleted, meaning that the edge could be included in a packing.

Lemma 8.11

(Introduce Edge Node With Usable Endpoints)
.

Let
t_{0}
be an introduce edge node with child
t_{1}

and let
uv
be the edge introduced.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{IntroEdge}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
that,
for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}

computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{0},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U

with
D\cap\{u,v\}=\varnothing
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

It remains to define
f_{0}
.
For this we first define a function
f
which we derive
f_{0}
later from.
In the definition of
f
we consider six possible cases
where two of them are symmetric.
For all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{1})
with
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)
,
we define

Case 0:

\displaystyle~f(Y_{0},

\displaystyle Y_{1},

\displaystyle Y_{2},

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle)\coloneqq

\displaystyle f_{1}(T),

Case 1:

\displaystyle\ \text{if }u\in Y_{0}\land v\in Y_{0}

\displaystyle~f(Y_{0}\setminus\{u,v\},

\displaystyle Y_{1}\cup\{u,v\},

\displaystyle Y_{2},

\displaystyle M\cup\{uv\}

\displaystyle)\coloneqq

\displaystyle f_{1}(T),

Case 2a:

\displaystyle\ \text{if }u\in Y_{0}\land yv\in M

\displaystyle~f(Y_{0}\setminus\{u\},

\displaystyle Y_{1}\setminus\{v\}\cup\{u\},

\displaystyle Y_{2}\cup\{v\},

\displaystyle M\setminus\{vy\}\cup\{uy\}

\displaystyle)\coloneqq

\displaystyle f_{1}(T),

Case 2b:

\displaystyle\ \text{if }xu\in M\land v\in Y_{0}

\displaystyle~f(Y_{0}\setminus\{v\},

\displaystyle Y_{1}\setminus\{u\}\cup\{v\},

\displaystyle Y_{2}\cup\{u\},

\displaystyle M\setminus\{xu\}\cup\{xv\}

\displaystyle)\coloneqq

\displaystyle f_{1}(T),

Case 3:

\displaystyle\ \text{if }xu\in M\land yv\in M

\displaystyle~f(Y_{0},

\displaystyle Y_{1}\setminus\{u,v\},

\displaystyle Y_{2}\cup\{u,v\},

\displaystyle M\setminus\{xu,yv\}\cup\{xy\}

\displaystyle)\coloneqq

\displaystyle f_{1}(T),\text{ and}

Case 4:

\displaystyle\ \text{if }uv\in M

\displaystyle~f(Y_{0},

\displaystyle Y_{1}\setminus\{u,v\},

\displaystyle Y_{2}\cup\{u,v\},

\displaystyle M\cup\{uv\}

\displaystyle)\coloneqq

\displaystyle f_{1}(T)-1

where
x,y\in Y_{1}
are such that
u,v,x,y
are all distinct.
Recall, that
\bot
and
{\curlyveeuparrow}
are invariant
under arithmetic operations,
this especially implies
\bot-1=\bot

and
{\curlyveeuparrow}-1={\curlyveeuparrow}
.

Observe that
f
is a function from
\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{0})

to
[-1..2\textup{{tw}}+2]\cup\{\bot,{\curlyveeuparrow}\}
.
If there is no type
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{0})
such that
f(T)=-1
,
then we define
f_{0}=f
.
Otherwise, we define
f_{0}
based on
f
where,
for all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{0})
,

f_{0}(T)\coloneqq\begin{cases}f(T),&\text{if }f(T)\in\{{\curlyveeuparrow},\bot\},\\
{\curlyveeuparrow},&\text{if }f(T)=2(\textup{{tw}}+1),\\
f(T)+1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

As a last step it remains to define
\ell_{0}
.
If
f_{0}=f
, then we set
\ell_{0}=\ell_{1}

and otherwise we set
\ell_{0}=\ell_{1}+1

which is complementing the definition of
f_{0}
.

Now consider a set
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U=V_{t_{0}}\setminus U

with
D\cap\{u,v\}=\varnothing

that is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
.
We claim that
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.
To prove this, fix some type
T_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{1})
.

If a packing of type
T_{0}
does not contain the edge
uv
,
then this packing is also of type
T_{0}
for
t_{1}
.
This is Case 0 in the definition above.

If the edge is selected,
then there is a unique type
T_{1}
for
t_{1}
resulting from deleting the edge.
Depending on how the inclusion of edge
uv
changed the type,
we consider four different cases:

Case 1:

A new path is created.

Case 2:

A path is extended by this edge.

Case 3:

Two paths are connected to each other.

Case 4:

A cycle is closed.

For the first three cases, the number of cycles does not change.
Only for Case 4, we obtain one more cycle in the type for
t_{0}

which was not present in the type for
t_{1}
.
∎

As a next step we consider forget nodes.
Depending on whether the forgotten vertex is deleted or not,
we consider two disjoint cases.

Lemma 8.12

(Forget Node With a Deleted Vertex)
.

Let
t_{0}
be a forget node with child
t_{1}

and let
v\notin U
be the vertex forgotten.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Forget}_{\mathsf{del}}
that,
for a given class
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}
with
v\in D_{1}
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau)

a class
c_{0}=(k_{1}+1,D_{1}\setminus\{v\},\ell_{1},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
then
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

The function
f_{0}
basically agrees with
f_{1}

except that every type for
t_{0}
does not include
v
.
As every type for
t_{1}
includes
v
,
there is a simple one-to-one correspondence
between the types for
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
.

Formally, we define the function

f_{0}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{1}\setminus\{v\})\to[0..2\textup{{tw}}+2]\cup\{\bot,{\curlyveeuparrow}\}

as follows.
For all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{1})

with
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)
,
we set

f_{0}(Y_{0}\setminus\{v\},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)\coloneqq f_{1}(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)=f_{1}(T).

When considering a set
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U

of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
,
we immediately get, by our choice of
f_{0}
,
that
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.
∎

The next procedure covers the second case for a forget node
where the vertex forgotten is not deleted
and thus, could potentially appear in cycle-packings.

Lemma 8.13

(Forget Node With a Usable Vertex)
.

Let
t_{0}
be a forget node with child
t_{1}

and let
u
be the vertex forgotten.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Forget}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
that,
for a given class
T_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})

computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
a class
c_{0}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{0})

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U

with
v\notin D
,
if
D
is of class
c_{1}
, then
D
is of class
c_{0}
.

Proof.

We define
f_{0}
as follows.
For all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{1})

with
T=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)
,
we set

f_{0}(T)\coloneqq\ell_{1}-\max\bigl(\ell_{1}-f_{1}(Y_{0}\cup\{v\},Y_{1},Y_{2},M),\ell_{1}-f_{1}(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2}\cup\{v\},M)\bigr)

where we assume that
a>{\curlyveeuparrow}>\bot
for all
a\in\mathbb{N}
.

Observe that when the vertex
v
is not deleted,
it can either be covered or uncovered.
In both cases we can get the same type for
t_{0}
,
although the packing has different types for
t_{1}
.
∎

The last procedure handles the case for join nodes.
Here we have to combine two partial cycle-packings to form a new one.
In order to make this combination formal,
we define a function
\mathsf{reduce}

which operates on the union of two matchings and combines them
such that partial packings are joined together whenever possible
and cycles are closed as soon as two partial cycle-packings
form together a cycle.

Definition 8.14
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t
be a join node of its tree decomposition.
We define the function

\mathsf{reduce}\colon 2^{X_{t}\times X_{t}}\times\mathbb{N}\to 2^{X_{t}\times X_{t}}\times\mathbb{N}

as

\mathsf{reduce}(M,\lambda)\coloneqq(M,\lambda)
if, for all distinct
u,v,w\in X
,
the set
M
satisfies
\lvert M\cap\{uv,vw\}\rvert\leq 1
.
Otherwise, we define

\mathsf{reduce}(M,\lambda)\coloneqq\begin{cases}(M\setminus\{uv,vw\}\cup\{uw\},\lambda),&\text{if }uv,vw\in M\land uw\notin M,\\
(M\setminus\{uv,vw\},\lambda+1),&\text{if }uv,vw\in M\land uw\in M.\end{cases}

With this function
\mathsf{reduce}
in mind,
we now formally define the combination of two types.

Definition 8.15

(Combination of Types)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
t_{0}
be a join node of its tree decomposition
with children
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Let
T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{1})
be a type for
t_{1}

with
T_{1}=(Y^{\prime}_{0},Y^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{2},M^{\prime})

and
T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{2}}(D_{1})
be a type for
t_{2}

with
T_{2}=(Y^{\prime\prime}_{0},Y^{\prime\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime\prime}_{2},M^{\prime\prime})
.

The combination of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
is undefined
whenever
Y^{\prime}_{2}\not\subseteq Y^{\prime\prime}_{0}
or
Y^{\prime\prime}_{2}\not\subseteq Y^{\prime}_{0}
,
that is, when a vertex would be incident to at least three selected edges
in the combined packing.
Otherwise, we define a new type
T_{0}=(Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},M)

with

\displaystyle Y_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq Y^{\prime}_{0}\cap Y^{\prime\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle Y_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(Y^{\prime}_{1}\cap Y^{\prime\prime}_{0})\cup(Y^{\prime}_{0}\cap Y^{\prime\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle Y_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq Y^{\prime}_{2}\cup Y^{\prime\prime}_{2}\cup(Y^{\prime}_{1}\cap Y^{\prime\prime}_{1})

where the set
M
is defined as
the set such that
(M,\lambda)
is the fix point of the function

\mathsf{reduce}

on input
((M^{\prime}\setminus M^{\prime\prime})\cup(M^{\prime\prime}\setminus M^{\prime}),\lvert M^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime\prime}\rvert)
.

We say that
T_{1}
and
t_{2}
can be combined to type
T_{0}

by creating
\lambda
cycles
and denote this by
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}\overset{+\lambda}{=}T_{0}
.

With this definition we can now state the procedure for the join node.

Lemma 8.16

(Join)
.

Let
t_{0}
be a join node with children
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.

There is a procedure
\mathsf{Join}
that,
for given classes
c_{1}=(k_{1},D_{1},\ell_{1},f_{1})
for
t_{1}

and
c_{2}=(k_{2},D_{2},\ell_{2},f_{2})
for
t_{2}

with
D_{1}=D_{2}
,
computes in time
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{3})
a class

c_{0}=(k_{1}+k_{2},D_{1},\ell_{0},f_{0})
for
t_{0}

such that the following holds:
For all sets
D^{\prime}\subseteq V_{t_{1}}\setminus U

and all sets
D^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V_{t_{2}}\setminus U

if
D^{\prime}
is of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}

and
D^{\prime\prime}
is of class
c_{2}
for
t_{2}
,
then
D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.

Proof.

First observe that we assumed that each edge is introduced exactly once
in the underlying tree decomposition.
Hence, the edge cannot appear in the partial packing for the left subtree

and
the partial packing for the right subtree.
Moreover, every type for
t_{0}
can be split into a component
coming from
t_{1}
and a part coming from
t_{2}

depending on the edges that have been introduced so far.

We first define a function
f

which serves as the basis for defining
f_{0}
.
For all types
T_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{1})
, we set

f(T)\coloneqq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{1}}(D_{1})\\
T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{2}}(D_{1})\\
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}\overset{+\lambda}{=}T_{0}\end{subarray}}\begin{cases}\ell_{1}-f_{1}(T_{1})+\ell_{2}-f_{2}(T_{2})+\lambda,&\text{if }f_{1}(T_{1}),f_{2}(T_{2})\in\mathbb{N},\\
{\curlyveeuparrow},&\text{if }{\curlyveeuparrow}\in\{f_{1}(T_{1}),f_{2}(T_{2})\}\not\ni\bot\\
\bot,&\text{if }\bot\in\{f_{1}(T_{1}),f_{2}(T_{2})\},\end{cases}

where we assume that
a>{\curlyveeuparrow}>\bot
for all
a\in\mathbb{N}
.

Based on this we now define
f_{0}
and
\ell_{0}
.
For some type
\widehat{T}
maximizing
f(\widehat{T})
, we set
\ell_{0}\coloneqq f(\widehat{T})
.
We construct
f_{0}
based on
f
where,
for all types
T\in\mathcal{T}_{t_{0}}(D_{0})
, we set

f_{0}(T)\coloneqq\begin{cases}f(T),&\text{if }f(T)\in\{{\curlyveeuparrow},\bot\},\\
{\curlyveeuparrow},&\text{if }f(T)<\ell_{0}-2(\textup{{tw}}+1),\\
\ell_{0}-f(T),&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

With this definition it follows directly
that for
two given sets
D^{\prime}\subseteq V_{t_{1}}
of class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}

and
D^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V_{t_{2}}
of class
c_{2}
for
t_{2}
,
the set
D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
.
∎

8.2
Dynamic program

Now we have everything ready to state the algorithm solving
Cycle
-HitPack
,
that is, prove
1.2
which we restate for convenience.

See
1.2

Proof of
1.2
.

Given an instance
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
of
Cycle
-HitPack
,
we first compute an optimal tree decomposition of
G

and then transform this decomposition into a nice tree decomposition
with introduce edge nodes where each edge is introduced once
and the additional requirement that
the root and leaf nodes have empty bags.

The algorithm computes,
for each node
t
of the tree decomposition,
a list
\mathcal{L}(t)
of classes
c
for this node
such that there is a set
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus U
that is of class
c
.

The dynamic program traverses the tree decomposition in post-order
and for each node
t_{0}
we perform the following actions
(depending on the type of node
t_{0}
).

Leaf Node.

For each leaf node
t_{0}
of the tree decomposition,
we define the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
explicitly by setting

\mathcal{L}(t_{0})\coloneqq\{(0,\varnothing,0,(\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing)\mapsto 0)\}
.

Introduce Vertex Node.

Let
t_{1}
be the unique child of
t_{0}

and let
v
be the vertex introduced at
t_{0}
,
that is,
X_{t_{0}}=X_{t_{1}}\cup\{v\}
.
Repeat the following two steps for all classes
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
:

•

Apply
\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
from
Lemma

8.9

on
c_{1}
to compute the class
c_{0}

and add
c_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

•

If
v\in V(G)\setminus U
, i.e., the vertex
v
can be deleted,
then use
\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{del}}
from
Lemma

8.8

to compute a class
c^{\prime}_{0}

and add
c^{\prime}_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
as well.

Introduce Edge Node.

Let
t_{1}
be the unique child of
t_{0}

and let
uv
be the edge introduced.
Repeat the following for all classes
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
:

•

If
u\in D_{1}
or
v\in D_{1}
, then use
\mathsf{IntroEdge}_{\mathsf{del}}
from
Lemma

8.10
on
c_{1}

to compute the class
c_{0}
and add
c_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

•

Otherwise we get
u,v\notin D_{1}
.
Then, we use
\mathsf{IntroEdge}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
from

Lemma

8.11
on
c_{1}

to compute the class
c_{0}^{\prime}
and add
c^{\prime}_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Forget Node.

Let
t_{1}
be the unique child of
t_{0}

and let
v
be the vertex forgotten,
that is,
X_{t_{0}}=X_{t_{1}}\setminus\{v\}
.
Repeat the following for all classes
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
:

•

If
v\in D_{1}
,
then use
\mathsf{Forget}_{\mathsf{del}}
from
Lemma

8.12
on
c_{1}

to compute the class
c_{0}
and add
c_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

•

If
v\notin D_{1}
, then use
\mathsf{Forget}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
from
Lemma

8.13
on
c_{1}

to compute the class
c_{0}^{\prime}
and add
c^{\prime}_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Join Node.

Let
t_{0}
be the unique parent of the nodes
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Repeat the following for all pairs of classes

(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})\times\mathcal{L}(t_{2})
:
Check that
D_{1}=D_{2}
and if so apply the procedure
\mathsf{Join}
from
Lemma

8.16
on
(c_{1},c_{2})

to get a class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}

and add
c_{0}
to the list
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

It remains to define the output of the algorithm.
For this let
r
be the root of the tree decomposition.
Then, the algorithm outputs
Yes
if the list
\mathcal{L}(r)
contains a class

(k_{0},\varnothing,\ell_{0},(\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing)\mapsto 0)

for some
k_{0}\in[0..k]
and
\ell_{0}\in[0..\ell-1]
.

Correctness.

It remains to prove the correctness and the running time of this algorithm.
We first show correctness by proving the following claim.

Claim 8.17

(Correctness)
.

For all nodes
t_{0}
,
integers
0\leq k_{0}\leq k
,
vertex sets
D_{0}\subseteq X_{t}\setminus U
,
integers
0\leq\ell_{0}<\ell
,
and
functions
f_{0}\colon\mathcal{T}_{t}(D_{0})\to[0..2\textup{{tw}}+2]\cup\{\bot,{\curlyveeuparrow}\}
,
the following two statements are equivalent:

•

There is a set
D\subseteq V_{t}\setminus U

of class
(k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0})
for
t_{0}
.

•

(k_{0},D_{0},\ell_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Proof of Claim .

By the definition of the procedures from

Lemmas

8.8
,
8.9
,
8.10
,
8.11
,
8.12
,
8.13
and
8.16

and the definition of the list for the leaf nodes,
it directly follows that the second statement implies the first one.

Therefore, we only have to prove
that the first statement implies the second statement.
We prove this inductively
based on the type of the node
t_{0}
in the tree decomposition.

Leaf Node.

Since the bags of the leaf nodes do not contain any vertices,
no vertices can be deleted.
By assumption, the leaf nodes do not have children
and hence, the only possible class is the empty class,
which we included in the list.

Introduce Vertex Node.

If the set
D
is of class
c_{0}
for
t_{0}
and
v\in D
,
then
D\setminus\{v\}
is of
some
class
c_{1}
for
t_{1}
.
By induction we get
c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
.
From the algorithm and the properties of
\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{del}}
,
we obtain a class
c
for
t_{0}
such that
D
is of class
c
for
t_{0}
.
Moreover, the algorithm adds
c_{0}
to
\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.
Since each set
D
has exactly one class for each node,
we have
c_{0}=c
and thus,
c_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.

Note, that if
v\notin D
,
then we similarly get that
c_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})

by using
\mathsf{IntroVtx}_{\mathsf{not{\text{-}}del}}
.

Introduce Edge Node.

The correctness follows analogously to the introduce vertex node
by distinguishing the two cases
whether an endpoint of the introduced edge was deleted
or both endpoints are not deleted.

Forget Node.

The result follows analogously to the introduce vertex node
by splitting into the two cases of
v
being deleted or not.

Join Node.

Assume that
D
is of class
c_{0}
.
If we consider
D^{\prime}=D\cap V_{t_{1}}
and
D^{\prime\prime}=D\cap V_{t_{2}}
,
we have that
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
are of some classes
c_{1}
and
c_{2}

for
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, respectively.
By the induction hypothesis it follows that

c_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{1})
and
c_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{2})
.

From the definition of the algorithm
and the properties of the procedure
\mathsf{Join}
,
it follows that
D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}=D
is of some class
c
for
t_{0}
.
By the uniqueness of classes, we conclude that
c=c_{0}

and therefore,
c_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(t_{0})
.
∎

As the last step we prove the running time of the algorithm
depending on the size of the lists.

Claim 8.18
.

Let
L
denote the maximum length of a list
\mathcal{L}(t)
for all nodes
t
.
Then, the algorithm terminates in time

L^{2}\cdot 2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\cdot\poly(n)
.

Proof of Claim .

Computing a nice tree decomposition is possible in time

2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}\cdot\poly(n)

[
17
, Chapter 7.6]
.
Observe that handling the join nodes dominates the running time
as we have to consider up to
L^{2}
different pairs of classes.
Since each new class can be computed in time
2^{\poly(\textup{{tw}})}
,
the claim follows.
∎

From
Lemma

8.7
we know that
for each node of the tree decomposition,
the number of distinct classes is at most

2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{tw}}\log\textup{{tw}})}}\cdot\poly(n)
.
Hence, the final running time directly follows from

8.18

which concludes the proof by
8.17
.
∎

9
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-Completeness Results

In this section we show the completeness of the
Cycle
-HitPack
problem
and the
H
-HitPack
problem for
the second level of the polynomial hierarchy.
Formally, we prove

1.1
and
1.5
.

Towards proving these results, we first show in

Lemma

9.1

that the problems are contained in
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then, by the proof of

Theorem

9.10
,
which is given in
Section

9.2
,
we show the
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-hardness of
Triangle
-HitPack
which we extend to
Cycle
-HitPack
.
In
Section

9.3
we lift the hardness of

Triangle
-HitPack
to the general
H
-HitPack
problem by proving

Lemma

9.13
.

The
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-completeness results for
Cycle
-HitPack
and
H
-HitPack
from

1.1
and
1.5

then follow from
Lemma

9.1
together with the
hardness results
from
Sections

9.2
and
9.3
,
respectively.

Lemma 9.1
.

For any connected graph
H
,
the problems
H
-HitPack and
Cycle
-HitPack are in
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

We observe that for a given graph
G
, the problem of deciding if
there is a

H
-packing of size at least
\ell
is in
NP
, as the
H
-packing
itself constitutes a polynomial witness. We assume oracle access
to the
problem.

Next, we can solve
H
-HitPack
by guessing the deleted vertices
non-deterministically and
subsequently verifying that an
H
-packing of size at least

\ell
does not
exist in the remaining graph. This can be achieved with an

NP
-oracle by
querying it and negating the answer. Therefore,
H
-HitPack
can
be solved
in non-deterministic polynomial time using an
NP
oracle. Hence,

\textsc{$H$-HitPack}\in\textsf{NP}^{\textsf{NP}}=\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
. An analogous argument
shows that

\textsc{$\text{Cycle}$-HitPack}\in\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
.
∎

9.1
Satisfiability Problems Complete for
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}

In this section, we introduce some satisfiability problems
which are complete for the second level of the polynomial hierarchy.

We begin with the formal definition of the prototypical

\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-complete problem, which is based on Boolean formulas in
disjoint normal form (DNF).

Note that in this paper, for a variable

x
, we let
\overline{x}
denote the negation of
x
.
Moreover, we say
that
x
is a positive literal and
\overline{x}
is a negative
literal.
Finally, note that a maximal group of disjoint literals in a DNF is
commonly referred to as a

term
.
For example, the
3
-DNF formula

\psi=(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land\overline{x}_{3})\lor(x_{4}\land x_{5}\land\overline{x}_{1})

has 5 variables, 6 literals and 2 terms.
One of the terms of
\psi
is
(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land\overline{x}_{3})
.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
Q3DNF
2
denote the problem of deciding
whether a
given formula
\varphi
in disjunctive normal form with terms
of length at most three on the variable set
X\cup Y
, where

X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
and
Y=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
for some natural
numbers
m
and
n
, has an assignment to the variables in
X
such
that the remaining formula is a tautology.
That is,

\textsc{Q3DNF${}_{2}$}=\{\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in\textup{3DNF}\mid\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\,\forall y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\colon\varphi=1\}.

In his seminal work,
Stockmeyer
[
74
]
proved this problem to be

\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-complete.

Theorem 9.3

(
[
74
, Theorem 4.1.2]
)
.

The problem Q3DNF
2
is log-space
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-complete.

We define the related problem where the underlying SAT-formula is in CNF.

Definition 9.4
.

Denote by
Q3CNF
2
the
problem of deciding
whether a
given formula
\varphi
in conjunctive normal form with clauses of
length at most three on the variable set
X\cup Y
, where

X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
and
Y=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
for some natural
numbers
m
and
n
, has an assignment to the variables in
X
such
that remaining formula is unsatisfiable.
That is,

\textsc{Q3CNF${}_{2}$}=\{\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in\textup{3CNF}\mid\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\,\nexists y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\colon\varphi=1\}.

By using De Morgan’s laws and basic properties of quantification
and their negation,
we can extend the
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-completeness of
Q3DNF
2
from
Theorem

9.3
to
Q3CNF
2
.

Corollary 9.5
.

The problem
Q3CNF
2
is
log-space

\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-complete.

Proof.

For any given 3DNF-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
, let

\psi
be the 3CNF-formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every
literal by its negation and swapping every
\vee
for
\wedge
and
vice versa.

This yields a
log-space
reduction from
Q3DNF
2
to

Q3CNF
2
.
On the one hand, logarithmic space is clearly sufficient to
transform
\varphi
into
\psi
.
On the other hand,
\varphi
is equivalent to

\neg\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
,
by the laws of De Morgan and distributivity.
Therefore, the formula

\displaystyle\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\,\forall y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\colon\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=1

is equivalent to

\displaystyle\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\,\forall y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\colon\neg\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=1

,

which is in turn equivalent to

\displaystyle\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\,\nexists y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\colon\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=1

\displaystyle.\qed

As a next step we first formally define the size of a formula.

Definition 9.6

(Formula Size)
.

The
size

|\varphi|
of a CNF-formula
\varphi
is the number
of its
clauses. We say that
\varphi
is smaller than another formula
\varphi^{\prime}

if
|\varphi|<|\varphi^{\prime}|
.

The last two problems we introduce
consider subformulas of a given CNF-formula.
For this reason we first formally introduce this intuitive concept.

Definition 9.7

(Subformula)
.

Given a CNF-formula
\varphi=C_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge C_{m}
consisting of

m
clauses, we call any formula
\varphi^{\prime}
that results from deleting
an arbitrary subset of these clauses a
subformula
of
\varphi

and denote this by
\varphi^{\prime}\subseteq\varphi
.
If additionally
\varphi^{\prime}\neq\varphi
, that is, the deleted subset is
nonempty, the resulting subformula is called

proper
and we write
\varphi^{\prime}\subsetneq\varphi
.
If
\psi
is a (proper) subformula of
\psi^{\prime}
, then
\psi^{\prime}
is a
(proper) superformula of
\psi
.

With this definition, we define the problems
SUS
and its corresponding version
3CNF-SUS
for 3CNFs.

Definition 9.8

(Smallest Unsatisfiable Subformula)
.

SUS
is the problem of deciding, given a formula
\varphi
and
an integer
k
, whether
\varphi
has an unsatisfiable subformula of
size at most
k
.

3CNF-SUS
is the problem restricted to CNF formulas
with
at most three literals per clause.

Umans, Fortnow and Killian have reportedly proven
the
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-completeness of
SUS
(calling the problem MIN DNF
TAUTOLOGY)
[
73
, p. 35, L7, MIN DNF TAUTOLOGY]
, but
only Umans published a proof, and only for the version that does not
limit the number of literals in a clause
[
76
, Theorem 2]
.
Several years later and apparently without knowledge of Uman’s proof,
Liberatore
[
51
, Theorem 2]
reproved this statement by a
more direct reduction, but still without the restriction to
at most three literals per clause.
Although not strictly necessary for our purposes, we extend this
result by providing the missing proof of
the
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-hardness for
3CNF-SUS
.

Theorem 9.9
.

3CNF-SUS
is
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-complete.

Proof.

For membership in
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
, guess a subformula of size
k

and verify its
unsatisfiability with the
NP
-oracle.

We now
reduce
Q3CNF
2
to
3CNF-SUS
.
Given an instance

\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=C_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge C_{t}

with
t
clauses
for the problem
Q3CNF
2
,
that is normalized
to contain exactly three
literals per
clause by duplicating literals if necessary.
First, replace every
literal that is a positive occurrence of
x_{i}

by a negative
occurrence of the new variable
z_{i}
, and write
the
t

clauses of the resulting formula as

(\lambda_{1}^{1}\lor\lambda_{1}^{2}\lor\lambda_{1}^{3})\land\dots\land(\lambda_{t}^{1}\lor\lambda_{t}^{2}\lor\lambda_{t}^{3})
.
Note that any of these literals might be a negative or
positive occurrence of
y_{j}
for any
j\in[n]
since we
did not modify these variables at all.
The instance for
3CNF-SUS
is

(\psi,k)
, where, using the
definition
s=\lceil\log m\rceil
, we have

k=2^{s+1}-(m+1)+(2t+2)m+2t\\

and

\psi=W\wedge V\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}(X_{i}\wedge Z_{i})\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\Gamma_{j}
with

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{2^{s}-1}(\overline{w}_{2i}\vee\overline{w}_{2i+1}\vee w_{i}),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{i=2^{s}}^{2^{s+1}-(m+1)}(w_{i}),

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle=(x_{i}^{2t})\wedge(\overline{x}_{i}^{1}\vee x_{i})\wedge(\overline{x}_{i}\vee w_{2^{s+1}-i})\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{2t-1}(\overline{x}_{i}^{j+1}\vee x_{i}^{j}),

\displaystyle Z_{i}

\displaystyle=(z_{i}^{2t})\wedge(\overline{z}_{i}^{1}\vee z_{i})\wedge(\overline{z}_{i}\vee w_{2^{s+1}-i})\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{2t-1}(\overline{z}_{i}^{j+1}\vee z_{i}^{j}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=(\overline{w}_{1}\vee\lambda_{i}^{1}\vee u_{i})\wedge(\overline{u}_{i}\vee\lambda_{i}^{2}\vee\lambda_{i}^{3}).

\lambda_{1}^{1}\vee u_{1}
\lambda_{1}^{2}\vee\lambda_{1}^{3}
\lambda_{2}^{1}\vee u_{2}
\lambda_{2}^{2}\vee\lambda_{2}^{3}
\lambda_{3}^{1}\vee u_{3}
\lambda_{3}^{2}\vee\lambda_{3}^{3}
\lambda_{4}^{1}\vee u_{4}
\lambda_{4}^{2}\vee\lambda_{4}^{3}
\lambda_{t}^{1}\vee u_{t}
\lambda_{t}^{2}\vee\lambda_{t}^{3}
w_{1}
w_{2}\wedge w_{3}
w_{4}\wedge w_{5}
w_{6}\wedge w_{7}
w_{8}\wedge w_{9}
w_{10}\wedge w_{11}
w_{12}\wedge w_{13}
w_{14}\wedge w_{15}
\Gamma_{1}
\Gamma_{2}
\Gamma_{3}
\Gamma_{4}
\Gamma_{t}
W
V
X_{i}\cup Z_{i}
X_{2}\cup Z_{2}
X_{1}\cup Z_{1}
w_{2^{s}}\wedge w_{2^{s}+1}
w_{2^{s+1}-i-1}\wedge w_{2^{s+1}-i}
