w_{2^{s+1}-2}\wedge w_{2^{s+1}-1}
x_{i}
x_{i}^{1}
x_{i}^{2}
x_{i}^{3}
x_{i}^{2t-1}
x_{i}^{2t}
z_{i}
z_{i}^{1}
z_{i}^{2}
z_{i}^{3}
z_{i}^{2t-1}
z_{i}^{2t}
x_{2}
x_{2}^{1}
x_{2}^{2}
x_{3}^{2}
x_{2}^{2t-1}
x_{2}^{2t}
z_{2}
z_{2}^{1}
z_{2}^{2}
z_{2}^{3}
z_{2}^{2t-1}
z_{2}^{2t}
x_{1}
x_{1}^{1}
x_{1}^{2}
x_{1}^{3}
x_{1}^{2t-1}
x_{1}^{2t}
z_{1}
z_{1}^{1}
z_{1}^{2}
z_{1}^{3}
z_{1}^{2t-1}
z_{1}^{2t}

Figure 9.1
:
Illustration of the implications encoded by the different
sets of clauses used in the reduction of the proof of
Theorem
9.9
. Note that the clauses correspond to
the arrows in the illustration, not the depicted variables. For
example, the clause
(\overline{w}_{2}\vee\overline{w}_{3}\vee w_{1})
is equivalent
to the implication
(w_{2}\wedge w_{3})\rightarrow w_{1}
represented by
the bottom-most arrow of
W
in the illustration, the left-most arrow
right below it represent implication
w_{1}\rightarrow(\lambda_{1}^{1}\vee u_{1})
equivalent to the clause
(\overline{w}_{1}\vee\lambda_{1}^{1}\vee u_{1})
, and
the clause
(z_{1}^{2t})
corresponds to arrow at the top right
starting from nowhere, which is interpreted as an implication with a
true antecedent, equivalent to the clause
(z_{1}^{2t})
.

This construction is clearly possible in logarithmic space. Note
that
\psi
contains exactly

2^{s+1}-(m+1)+2m(3+2t-1)+2t=k+(2t+2)m

clauses. Each of these clauses can be interpreted as an
implication as illustrated in
Figure
9.1
.
We now prove the correctness of the reduction.
Intuitively, all clauses of
\psi
are selected
with the only exception that, for each
i\in[m]
,
either
X_{i}
or
Z_{i}
is not included entirely.

Observe that setting all variables to
1
satisfies all clauses
from
W\wedge V\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}(X_{i}\wedge Z_{i})
.
It follows that any unsatisfiable formula includes a nonempty
set of clauses from
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}
.
Moreover, since assigning
0
to both
w_{1}
and all
u_{i}

satisfies all of
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}
, and
w_{1}
is the
only variable shared with the remaining clauses
W\wedge V\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(X_{i}\wedge Z_{i})
, an unsatisfiable subformula
also includes
a subset of clauses from

W\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}(X_{i}\wedge Z_{i})
such that any assignment satisfying this subset is
forced to assign
1
to
w_{1}
.
It follows that an unsatisfiable subformula
includes all of
W
. This
is because
W
contains clauses that represent a
binary tree of implications of the form

w_{2i}\wedge w_{2i+1}\rightarrow w_{i}
that needs to be
included in full to force any assignment satisfying all selected
clauses from
W\wedge V\wedge\bigwedge_{i=0}^{m}(X_{i}\wedge Z_{i})
to
set
w_{1}
at the root of the implication tree to
1
.
Furthermore, any unsatisfiable subformula contains some clauses
that forces any assignment satisfying them to
set all the leaves of the mentioned binary tree, namely

w_{2^{s}},\dots,w_{2^{s+1}}
, to be set to
1
.
Including the set
V
of singleton clauses
does this for all but the last
m
leaves. For each remaining
leaf
w_{2^{s+1}-1}
, either
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
must be included.

We have seen so far that any unsatisfiable
subformula of
\psi

must contain a clause from
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}
,
all of
W\wedge V
, and, for each pair of implication
chains
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
, one chain in its entirety.
Including up to
2t
clauses of the other chain of a pair cannot
affect the satisfiability because only the last two of the
2t+2

clauses contain variables occurring outside of the chain, namely

x_{i}
and
w_{2^{s+1}-i}
, and including such a clause and
activating it via uninterrupted implication chain from the only
unconditional clause at the head of the chain, either

(x_{i}^{r})
or
(z_{i}^{r})
, requires at least
2t+1
clauses.

In addition to the
2^{s+1}-(m+1)+(2t+2)m
clauses
from
W\wedge V
and,
for each
i\in[m]
, either
X_{i}
or

Z_{i}
, we may
include exactly
2t
more clauses until the threshold
of
k
is reached,
which exactly suffices to include all of

\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\Gamma_{t}
but not any pertinent part of an
additional implication chain, which would require at least

2t+1
clauses.
Therefore,
\psi
has an unsatisfiable subformula
of size at most
k

if and only if its subformula containing all
clauses except, for
each
i\in[m]
, either
X_{i}
or

Z_{i}
, is unsatisfiable.
Any assignment that satisfies all clauses from
X_{i}
or
Z_{i}

must assign
1
to
x_{i}
or
z_{i}
, respectively, while the other
variable can
be set to
0
to satisfy as many clauses as possible from

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}
. This corresponds
to a consistent
assignment of either
0
or
1
to each
variable from

x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
, whereas assignments to

y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
are not
restricted in any way.

Any assignment satisfying all included clauses except possibly
those of
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}
assigns
1
to
w_{1}
. Such
an assignment satisfies the
first clause of
\Gamma_{i}
if
its sets
\lambda_{i}^{1}
or
u_{i}
to
1
. If
u_{i}
is set to
1
, then
satisfying the
second clause of
\Gamma_{i}
requires
\lambda_{i}^{2}
or
\lambda_{i}^{3}

to be set to
1
.
For any assignment setting
w_{1}
to
1
, two clauses of

\Gamma_{i}
are thus equivalent to
C_{i}
for each
i\in[m]

and

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}
is equivalent to

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}C_{i}=\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
.

This shows that
\varphi
is in

Q3CNF
2
if and only if
\psi
has an
unsatisfiable
subformula of size at most
k
.

Note that we do not know the exact minimum size
of an unsatisfiable
subformula since not all clauses from

\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\Gamma_{j}

might be needed. But the threshold
k
is sufficiently large
for all of them to be included in any case.
∎

9.2
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-Hardness for
Triangle
-HitPack
and
Cycle
-HitPack

We use the
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-hardness of
SUS
from
9.9

to prove the
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-hardness of
Triangle
-HitPack
.
The completeness for
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
then directly follows from
Lemma

9.1
.

Theorem 9.10
.

The problem
Triangle
-HitPack is

\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-hard on tripartite graphs.

Proof.

We reduce from
SUS
by mapping any given pair
(\varphi,k)
,
where

\varphi=C_{1}\land\dots\land C_{m}
is a formula with
m
clauses on the
variable set
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, to an instance
(G,U,k,\ell)
of
Triangle
-HitPack
as described
below. The entire construction is
shown in
Figure

9.2
for a simple example.

Construction.

The graph
G
contains, for every variable
x_{i}
, a
triangle
cycle
of
2m

triangles, each conjoined to the next at one vertex.
Formally, we have four sets of
m
vertices each,

V_{i}=\{v_{i}^{1},\dots,v_{i}^{m}\},\quad\overline{V}_{i}=\{\overline{v}_{i}^{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{i}^{m}\},\quad W_{i}=\{w_{i}^{1},\dots,w_{i}^{m}\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{W}_{i}=\{\overline{w}_{i}^{1},\dots,\overline{w}_{i}^{m}\},

and the edges of the triangles
\{w_{i}^{j},v_{i}^{j},\overline{w}_{i}^{j}\}
and

\{\overline{w}_{i}^{j},\overline{v}_{i}^{j},w_{i}^{j+1}\}

for every
j\in[m]
, identifying
w_{i}^{m+1}
with
w_{i}^{1}
for convenience.

For every clause
C_{j}
, we add a
clause triangle

\{a_{j},b_{j},c_{j}\}
.
Moreover,
for every literal in this clause, we add two
literal edges
from
a_{j}

and

b_{j}
to a
literal vertex
, namely
v_{i}^{j}
if the literal is

x_{i}
and
\overline{v}_{i}^{j}
if the
literal is
\overline{x}_{i}
for some
i\in[n]
.
We call the vertices of this clause gadget the
clause vertices
.

Finally, we choose

\ell=mn+m
as the number of triangles that should not be possible to pack
and

U=V(G)\setminus\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}
as the set of undeletable
vertices.
The number of vertices we are allowed to delete is
k
.
This finishes the construction of the instance
(G,U,k,\ell)
.

Correctness.

We now prove the correctness of the reduction.
A maximum triangle packing of
the graph
G
consists, without loss of
generality, of the
m
clause triangles and every second triangle of
each triangle cycle; these are
m+n\cdot m
triangles in total.
These triangles cover all vertices except, for each
i
, either
V_{i}

or
\overline{V}_{i}
. We interpret
V_{i}
being uncovered as assigning

1
to
x_{i}

and
\overline{V}_{i}
being uncovered as assigning
0
to
x_{i}
.

Deleting
c_{j}
means that we cannot keep the
j
th clause triangle in
a maximum packing. But we can keep the edge
\{a_{j},b_{j}\}
and replace

c_{j}
by any of its literal vertices unless they are already covered.
Since either
V_{i}
or
\overline{V}_{i}
is always covered, it follows
that we can still pack
mn+m
triangles into the graph from which a
set
D
of vertices has been deleted
if and only if the
corresponding subformula
consisting of the
clauses
\{C_{j}\mid c_{j}\in D\}
is satisfiable;
otherwise, it is impossible to pack this many triangles.
It follows immediately that from
G
we can obtain a graph without a
triangle packing of size
k=mn+m
by deleting
k
deletable
vertices
if and only if
\varphi
has an unsatisfiable subformula of size
at most
k
.
∎

w_{1}^{1}
v_{1}^{1}
\overline{w}_{1}^{1}
\overline{v}_{1}^{1}
w_{1}^{2}
v_{1}^{2}
\overline{w}_{1}^{2}
\overline{v}_{1}^{2}
w_{1}^{3}
x_{1}
w_{2}^{1}
v_{2}^{1}
\overline{w}_{2}^{1}
\overline{v}_{2}^{1}
w_{2}^{2}
v_{2}^{2}
\overline{w}_{2}^{2}
\overline{v}_{2}^{2}
w_{2}^{3}
x_{2}
w_{3}^{1}
v_{3}^{1}
\overline{w}_{3}^{1}
\overline{v}_{3}^{1}
w_{3}^{2}
v_{3}^{2}
\overline{w}_{3}^{2}
\overline{v}_{3}^{2}
w_{3}^{3}
x_{3}
a_{1}
b_{1}
c_{1}
C_{1}
a_{2}
b_{2}
c_{2}
C_{2}

Figure 9.2
:
Construction of
G(\varphi)
for the simple instance

(\varphi,k)
with
k=1
and

\varphi=(x_{1}\vee x_{2}\vee\overline{x}_{3})\wedge(\overline{x}_{1}\vee x_{2}\vee x_{3})
. Note that within the variable gadgets,
w_{1}^{3}
,
w_{2}^{3}
,
and

w_{3}^{3}
are identified
with

w_{1}^{1}

w_{2}^{1}
, and
w_{3}^{1}
, respectively, creating one

triangle cycle
for each variable.
Each clause has its own
gadget consisting of a triangle and additional
literal
edges
connecting it to the variable gadgets.

The dashed vertex
c_{2}
is deleted, which corresponds to
activating the second clause of the formula. The bold lines show
a maximum triangle packing of the remaining graph. The remaining
packed triangle correspond to the satisfying
assignment
x_{1}\mapsto 0,x_{2}\mapsto 1,x_{3}\mapsto 1
.
The maximum packing has a total size of
mn+m=8
, showing that
this is a no-instance, which is trivial in this case since all
subformulas of
\varphi
are satisfiable.

As a next step we extend the previous result to
Cycle
-HitPack
.
For this we make use of the problem
Triangle
-HitPart
which is defined as follows.

Definition 9.11

(
Triangle
-HitPart
)
.

The problem
Triangle
-HitPart asks,
given a graph
G
, a vertex set
U\subseteq V(G)
, and an integer
k
,
whether it is possible to delete
exactly

k

vertices from the
graph
G
such that the vertices of the remaining graph cannot be
partitioned into triangles.

Note that we require the deletion of
exactly

k
vertices for
this partition version of the problem since allowing for the deletion
of less than
k
vertices would render the problem trivial for any
positive
k
: If the number of vertices in the graph is divisible by

3
, deleting one vertex; otherwise, leave it as it is,
gives a graph with no triangle-partition.
We could also require the deletion of exactly
k
vertices for the
packing version of the problem. All of our results still hold because
deleting more vertices can never increase the packing number of the
remaining graph.

We will prove the following two theorems at once.

See
1.1

Theorem 9.12
.

The problem
Triangle
-HitPart is
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-hard on tripartite graphs.

Proof of

1.1
and
9.12
.

We extend the construction used in the proof of

Theorem

9.10
as follows.
We have already observed that for a no-instance all subgraphs of
G

resulting from
deleting up to
k
deletable vertices have a triangle packing of
size
mn+m

where all uncovered vertices are from
V\cup\overline{V}
.
For
k
deleted vertices, exactly

mn-k
among these vertices remain uncovered.
We extend
G
to a new graph
G^{\prime}

by adding, for each
r\in[mn-k]
, a vertex pair

\{y_{r},z_{r}\}

and, for each vertex
v\in V\cup\overline{V}
, the edges of the
triangle

\{v,y_{r},z_{r}\}
.

Each new vertex pair
\{y_{r},z_{r}\}
can be used for packing exactly one triangle
that covers
exactly one arbitrary vertex from
V\cup\overline{V}
. This enables
us to turn
any of the previously considered packings of size
mn+m
of
G
after
the deletion of
k
vertices into a triangle partition of size

mn+m+(mn-k)=2mn+m-k
of
G^{\prime}
after the deletion of the same vertices.
Conversely, for any triangle partition of
G^{\prime}
, which necessarily has
size
2mn+m-k
, we obtain a packing
of size exactly
mn+m
for
G
by removing the
mn-k
triangles
containing any of the new vertex
pairs.

This provides a direct reduction from
SUS
both to

Triangle
-HitPart
and, because a triangle partition is the
most efficient way of packing as many cycles as possible into a
graph, to

Cycle
-HitPack
.
∎

9.3
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
-Hardness for
H
-HitPack

Now, we focus on the proof of the following statement.

See
1.5

The containment in
\Sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{P}}
follows from
Lemma

9.1
.
The hardness result follows immediately from the hardness result
for
Triangle
-HitPack
,

Theorem

9.10
,
together with a polynomial-time reduction from
Triangle
-HitPack
to
H
-HitPack
,
formally stated in
Lemma

9.13
.
We actually prove a stronger result and additionally show
that this reduction is
pathwidth-preserving
,
by which we mean that for an input graph
G
of
Triangle
-HitPack
,
the
H
-HitPack
oracle is called by the algorithm only on input graphs whose pathwidth exceeds the pathwidth of
G
by at most some additive constant.
While we do not need this stronger version in the current section,
we use it in
Section

10
.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
H
be a fixed connected graph with at least three vertices.
There is a polynomial-time pathwidth-preserving reduction from
Triangle
-HitPack to
H
-HitPack.

Towards proving
Lemma

9.13
, let us first give some intuition and introduce some definitions.

Intuition.

The naive idea is to introduce, for each triangle
T
of the given instance,
a copy of the graph
H
, say
H_{T}
,
and to identify three (arbitrary) vertices of
H_{T}

with the vertices of the triangle
T
.
However, in this construction there is no guarantee that, in an
H
-packing of this modified instance,

H_{T}
is covered only by one copy of
H
.
Instead, it could be the case that one part of
H_{T}

is covered by one copy of
H
, while another part of
H_{T}
is covered by a different copy.
To avoid this situation, rather than identifying the vertices of the triangle
T
with corresponding vertices in
H_{T}
directly,
we introduce long chains (of gadgets)
between these vertices.
We create, for each vertex
v
of a triangle
T
,
a chain
C_{T,v}
consisting of modified copies of
H

(which we later formally introduce as diamond gadgets)
that allow us to propagate the information whether
T
was covered.
Again, as this propagation is not enforced,
there might be packings that do not properly propagate this information.
However, the construction of these diamond gadgets
and the length of the chain ensure that these undesired packings
have size far from the maximum. To make the construction with the chains work, we require that
H
is connected, and in order to have distinct vertices in
H_{T}
corresponding to the vertices of the triangle
T
, we need the requirement that
H
have size at least
3
.

Diamond Gadgets.

Inspired by Kirkpatrick and Hell
[
43
]
,
we define the
diamond gadget
as follows.
Let
H
be a connected graph with at least two vertices.
Let
u
and
u^{\prime}
be vertices with maximum distance in
H
. Since
H
is connected, this distance is finite, and since
H
has at least two vertices,
u
and
u^{\prime}
are distinct.
We introduce a new vertex
\overline{u}
that is a copy of
u
in the sense that it is incident precisely to the neighbors of
u
.
The graph
H
together with this new vertex
\overline{u}

and the corresponding new edges forms the
diamond gadget
(of
H
),
which we abbreviate by the symbol
\diamondsuit
.
We say that
u
and
\overline{u}
are the
connecting vertices
of
\diamondsuit
.

We point out two choices of packing
H
into
\diamondsuit
(there might be more):
(1) the packing that covers the original copy of
H
(with the corresponding vertices), in particular, it covers
u
.
We say that this is the
forward packing
for
\diamondsuit
,
or (2) the packing that is identical to the forward packing, except that it covers
\overline{u}
instead of
u
,
we say this is the
backward packing
for
\diamondsuit
.

\diamondsuit^{(1)}_{T,x}
\diamondsuit^{(2)}_{T,x}
\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,x}
\diamondsuit^{(1)}_{T,z}
\diamondsuit^{(2)}_{T,z}
\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,z}
\diamondsuit^{(1)}_{T,y}
\diamondsuit^{(2)}_{T,y}
\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,y}
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
x
y
z
H_{T}
T
\dots
\dots
\dots

Figure 9.3
:
An illustration of
H_{T}
for
T=xyz
.
The three rows of diamond gadgets correspond to
the chains
C_{T,x}
,
C_{T,y}
, and
C_{T,z}
, respectively.
The dashed edges of the triangle are deleted in the construction.

Proof of
Lemma

9.13
.

Let
H
be an arbitrary connected graph with at least three vertices.
Given an instance
I_{\triangle}=(G,U,k,\ell)
of
Triangle
-HitPack
,
we define an instance
I_{H}
of
H
-HitPack
as follows.
We set
n_{H}\coloneqq|V(H)|
,
and we denote by
\mathcal{T}
the set of all triangles in
G
.

For each triangle
T\in\mathcal{T}
and each vertex
v
of
T

we introduce a gadget
C_{T,v}
(cf.
Figure

9.3
) as follows:

•

For all
i\in[10n_{H}]
,
we create a copy of the gadget
\diamondsuit
,
denoted by
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
.

•

For all
i\in[10n_{H}-1]
,
we identify vertex
\overline{u}
of
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}

with vertex
u
of
\diamondsuit^{(i+1)}_{T,v}
.

•

The gadget
C_{T,v}
consists of the union of the diamond gadgets

\diamondsuit^{(1)}_{T,v},\dots,\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,v}
.

•

If, for all
i\in[10n_{H}]
,
we choose the forward packing for
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
,
then we say this is the
forward packing
for
C_{T,v}
.

•

If, for all
i\in[10n_{H}]
,
we choose the backward packing for
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
,
then we say this is the
backward packing
for
C_{T,v}
.

For each triangle
T\in\mathcal{T}
and for every vertex
v\in T
,
we identify
v
with the connecting vertex
u
of
\diamondsuit^{(1)}_{T,v}
.
For every triangle
T=xyz
in
G
,
we introduce a copy of
H
, denoted by
H_{T}
.
We identify the connecting vertices
\overline{u}
of

\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,x},\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,y},\diamondsuit^{(10n_{H})}_{T,z}
with three (arbitrary) distinct vertices

v_{p},v_{q},v_{r}
of
H_{T}
, respectively.
In order to avoid the situation that the edges in the triangles of
G
are used for packing
H
, we delete all edges which are originally in
G
. The construction for some triangle
T
is illustrated in
Figure

9.3
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the resulting graph.
We define
U^{\prime}\coloneqq U\cup V(G^{\prime})\setminus V(G)

as the set of undeletable vertices of
G^{\prime}
,
that is, the deletable vertices are precisely the deletable vertices from
G
.
We set
k^{\prime}\coloneqq k

and
\ell^{\prime}\coloneqq 30n_{H}\cdot\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert+\ell
.
Let
I_{H}=(G^{\prime},U^{\prime},k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
be the instance of
H
-HitPack
.
This finishes the construction.

Now we prove the correctness of this reduction.

Claim 9.14
.

If
I_{\triangle}
is a
Yes
-instance of
Triangle
-HitPack,
then
I_{H}
is a
Yes
-instance of
H
-HitPack.

Proof of Claim .

Let
S
be some solution of
I_{\triangle}=(G,U,k,\ell)
.
Since the deletable vertices of
G
are also deletable in
G^{\prime}

and by definition of
I_{H}
,
we get
S\subseteq V(G^{\prime})\setminus U^{\prime}
and
\lvert S\rvert\leq k=k^{\prime}
.
In the remainder we prove that all
H
-packings of
G^{\prime}\setminus S

have size less than
\ell^{\prime}
.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction that there is an
H
-packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for
G^{\prime}-S

containing at least
\ell^{\prime}
copies of
H
.
In the next steps we show that we can assume
that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is either the forward packing or the backward packing on all
gadgets
C_{T,v}
. A priori, this is not clear as, while we delete the edges
of the original graph
G
, each original vertex can be contained in multiple
triangles of
G
, and therefore might now be connected to multiple gadgets

C_{T,v}
,
C_{T^{\prime},v}
, and so on. So, a copy of
H
in some packing could
cover some part of
C_{T,v}
and some part of
C_{T^{\prime},v}
(other than just

v
). We argue that without loss of generality, we can assume that this is not the case.

1.

Consider some diamond gadget
\diamondsuit_{T,v}^{(i)}
.
Since
H
is connected and has at least two vertices, this gadget has precisely two vertices that are connected to the rest of
G"
: the connecting vertices
u
and
\overline{u}
.
Consequently, as
H
is connected and
\diamondsuit_{T,v}^{(i)}-\{u,\overline{u}\}
only has
|V(H)|-1
vertices, a copy of
H
in the packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
that covers some of the vertices of
\diamondsuit_{T,v}^{(i)}
has to cover at least one of
u
or
\overline{u}
. Thus, there are at most two copies of
H
that cover some vertices of
\diamondsuit_{T,v}^{(i)}
.

2.

Now suppose that in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
there is a copy of
H

that covers both connecting vertices of some diamond gadget. Then no other copy of
H
covers vertices of this diamond gadget.
Then we can replace this copy of
H

by the forward or backward packing for this diamond gadget
without changing the size (or feasibility) of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
Thus, we can assume that in the
H
-packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
there is no copy of
H

covering both connecting vertices of some diamond gadget.

3.

Consider some diamond gadget
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
and the corresponding distinguished vertices
u
,
\overline{u}
, and
u^{\prime}
.
Suppose some copy of
H
in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
covers some vertex
w
outside of
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
. We argue that this copy of
H
cannot cover
u^{\prime}
:
First, as
H
is connected, the vertices
u^{\prime}
and
w
have to be connected in
G
. Consider a shortest path between
u^{\prime}
and
w
in
G
. It has to go through one of the connecting vertices
u
or
\overline{u}
, say through
u
. This implies that the distance between
u^{\prime}
and
w
is strictly larger than the distance between
u^{\prime}
and
u
. Since the distance between
u^{\prime}
and
u
equals the diameter of
H
, the graph
H
cannot cover both
w
and
u^{\prime}
.
So we have shown that a copy of
H
that covers
u^{\prime}
covers no vertex outside of the corresponding gadget
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
. By the previous deductions, we know that such a copy covers precisely one of
u
or
\overline{u}
, and actually due to the fact that
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
has precisely
|V(H)|+1
vertices, such a copy of
H
then covers all vertices of
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
apart from one of
\overline{u}
or
u
. So, summarizing, if
u^{\prime}
is covered, then we can assume that
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
is covered according to the forward packing or backward packing, respectively.

4.

Suppose that
for some
T
and
v
,
there is (at least) one gadget
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}

for which the corresponding vertex
u^{\prime}
is covered by
\mathcal{P}
.
Let
i_{\min}\in[10n_{H}]
be the smallest such index.
From the previous point, we can assume that the copy of
H
that covers
u^{\prime}
covers
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min})}_{T,v}

according to either the forward or the backward packing.
Suppose it is the backward packing — the other case is analogous.
Then
\overline{u}
of
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}+1)}_{T,v}
is already covered by the backward packing of
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min})}_{T,v}
and consequently there is at most one other copy of
H
in the packing
\mathcal{P}
that covers vertices of
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}+1)}_{T,v}
. This copy then has to cover the vertex corresponding to
u
(in
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}+1)}_{T,v}
). Therefore, we can replace this copy of
H
by the backward packing of
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}+1)}_{T,v}
without violating the feasibility of the packing. Using the argument iteratively, we can assume that for all
i\geq i_{\min}
the corresponding gadget is covered according to the backward packing.
Now let us look at
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}-1)}_{T,v}
. By assumption, the corresponding vertex
u^{\prime}
is not covered. Let us consider a copy of
H
that covers some vertices from
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}-1)}_{T,v}
. We know that it covers precisely one of
u
or
\overline{u}
from
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}-1)}_{T,v}
. Since it does not covers both, but it also does not cover
u^{\prime}
, from the number of vertices of
H
, it follows that it has to cover some vertices outside of
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}-1)}_{T,v}
. Thus, it cannot cover
\overline{u}
since then it would have to cover other vertices in
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min})}_{T,v}
— however, all of these vertices are already covered. It follows that such a copy of
H
would have to cover
u
, and importantly this means that there is only one such copy in
\mathcal{P}
that covers vertices of
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}-1)}_{T,v}
. Hence, we can replace it by the backward packing for
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min}-1)}_{T,v}
without violating the feasibility of the packing. Again, we can use the argument iteratively for all
i<i_{\min}
.
So we can assume the backward packing on
C_{T,v}
.
Similarly we could assume the forward packing on
C_{T,v}
if
\diamondsuit^{(i_{\min})}_{T,v}
were covered according to the forward packing.

5.

Finally, suppose there are
T
and
v
such that, for all
i\in[10n_{H}]
,
the vertex
u^{\prime}
from
\diamondsuit^{(i)}_{T,v}
is not covered by the packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
In this case the packing covers at most

10(n_{H}-1)\cdot n_{H}
vertices of
C_{T,v}
. Note also, that since
H
is connected, each copy of
H
in the packing that covers vertices within
C_{T,v}
as well as outside, has to cover one of the three vertices of the triangle
T
. Consequently, there can be at most
3
such copies of
H
in the packing. Using this fact, together with the fact that at most
10(n_{H}-1)\cdot n_{H}
vertices of
C_{T,v}
are covered, we conclude that at most
10(n_{H}-1)+3

copies of
H
cover vertices of
C_{T,v}
.
Hence we can replace these copies of
H
with
the backward packing for
C_{T,v}
and obtain an overall packing that is
strictly larger
as we now pack
10n_{H}
copies of
H
to cover
C_{T,v}
.

So we have shown that in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, every copy of
H
either corresponds to a
backward or forward packing for a diamond gadget
or covers precisely the vertices of some
H_{T}
(if it were to cover some vertices of
H_{T}
and some vertices outside of
H_{T}
then these outside vertices are part of some diamond gadget and this would contradict the fact that this diamond gadget is covered either by the forward or backward packing).

Recall that we assumed for contradiction that
\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\rvert\geq\ell^{\prime}=30n_{H}\cdot\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert+\ell
.
By the construction of the graph,
the packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
contains already
30n_{H}\cdot\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert

copies of
H
from the chains
C_{T,v}
.
Then it contains at least an additional
\ell
copies of
H

that cover
H_{T}
for some triangle
T=xyz
.
However, then the corresponding gadgets
C_{T,x}
,
C_{T,x}
, and
C_{T,x}

have to be covered according to the
forward
packing
as otherwise the vertices at the intersection of
H_{T}

and these chains would be covered twice.
It follows that the vertices of the triangle
T
are covered.
So this would imply that there is a triangle-packing
of size at least
\ell
in
G\setminus S
,
contradicting that
S
is a solution for
I
.

We conclude that

I_{H}=(G^{\prime},U^{\prime},k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
is a
Yes
-instance of
H
-HitPack

which finishes the proof of the claim.
∎

Now we prove the reverse direction.

Claim 9.15
.

If
I_{H}=(G^{\prime},U^{\prime},k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
is a
Yes
-instance of
H
-HitPack,
then
I_{\triangle}=(G,U,k,\ell)
is a
Yes
-instance of
Triangle
-HitPack.

Proof of Claim .

Let
S^{\prime}
be some solution for
I_{H}
.
Since
I_{H}
and
I_{\triangle}
have the same set of deletable vertices,
we have
S^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
.
Moreover, since
k^{\prime}=k
,
it also holds that
\lvert S^{\prime}\rvert\leq k
.
To show that
S^{\prime}
is a solution for
I_{\triangle}
,
it remains to prove that
G-S^{\prime}
has no
\triangle
-packing of size at least
\ell
.

Suppose for contradiction’s sake that there is a
\triangle
-packing

\mathcal{P}
of size at least
\ell
in
G-S
.
We define a corresponding
H
-packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}
as follows.

•

For every triangle
T\in\mathcal{P}
and every vertex
v\in T
,
we add to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
the forward packing for
C_{T,v}

(containing
10n_{H}
copies of
H
),
and a copy of
H
that covers precisely
H_{T}
. Note that this is feasible as the forward packing does not cover any vertices from
H_{T}
.
This gives a total of
30n_{H}+1
copies of
H
in the packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
per triangle in
\mathcal{P}
.

•

For every triangle
T\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{P}
and every vertex
v\in T
,
we add the backward packing for
C_{T,v}

to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. This gives a total of
30n_{H}
copies of
H
per triangle outside of
\mathcal{P}
.

It is straightforward to see that the resulting packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is indeed feasible, and it has size at least
30n_{H}\lvert\mathcal{T}\rvert+\ell=\ell^{\prime}
.
This finishes the proof of the claim.
∎

It remains to show that the pathwidth of
G^{\prime}
exceeds that of
G
by at most some additive constant.

Claim 9.16
.

If
G
has pathwidth
pw
,
then
G^{\prime}
has pathwidth
\textup{{pw}}+\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Proof of Claim .

Suppose there is a path decomposition for
G
of width
pw
.
For every triangle
T=xyz
of
G
,
there is at least one bag that contains all vertices of
T
.
We replace it with a bag
X_{T}
that contains all vertices of
C_{T,x}
,
C_{T,y}
,
C_{T,z}
, and of
H_{T}
.
This gives a valid path decomposition of
G^{\prime}
.
Since the number of vertices in
C_{T,x}
,
C_{T,y}
,
C_{T,z}
, and
H_{T}

depends only on
H
(which is fixed),
the pathwidth of
G^{\prime}
is
\textup{{pw}}+\mathcal{O}(1)
.
∎

This finishes the proof of
Lemma

9.13
.
∎

10
Double-Exponential Lower Bounds Parameterized by Pathwidth

In this section, we prove
1.9
and
1.3
, which state double exponential lower bounds for
H
-HitPack
and
Cycle
-HitPack
in terms of the pathwidth of the input graph. Note that this directly implies corresponding lower bounds in terms of treewidth.
Let us first consider
1.9
and restate it for convenience.
We consider
1.3
later in
Section

10.3
.
See
1.9

Often such lower bounds are stated in their strengthened version,
in which it is assumed that a path decomposition of width
pw

is given as part of the input.
However, this is no strengthening in our case
since the runtime lower bound is double exponential in
pw

and it is well-known that a path decomposition of
G

can be computed in time exponential in
\textup{{pw}}^{2}
and linear in
n

[
45
]
.

Again, we first give a hardness result for
Triangle
-HitPack
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
\varphi
be a 3-SAT instance with
n
variables and
m
clauses.
In time
\mathcal{O}(n+m\log m)
,
we can construct an instance
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
of
Triangle
-HitPack such that

•

\varphi
is satisfiable if and only if
I
has a solution,

•

G
has pathwidth at most
\mathcal{O}(\log m)
,
and

•

G
is of size
\mathcal{O}(m\log m)
.

The proof of
Lemma

10.1
is given in
Section

10.1
.
As a second step, we then apply the pathwidth-preserving reduction from
Triangle
-HitPack
to
H
-HitPack
(for any fixed, connected graph
H
), as stated in
Lemma

9.13
.
Here are the details.

Proof of
1.9
.

Assume that
H
-HitPack
could be solved in time
2^{2^{o(\textup{{pw}})}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Let
\varphi
be an instance of 3-SAT with
n
variables and
m
clauses.
We use
Lemma

10.1
,
to obtain an instance
I_{\varphi}
of
Triangle
-HitPack
with pathwidth
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
that has a solution if and only if
\varphi
has a solution. Now we can execute our hypothetical algorithm for
H
-HitPack
instead of each oracle call of the reduction from
Lemma

9.13
on input

I_{\varphi}
. Note that this
procedure solves a given instance
\varphi
of 3-SAT.
Moreover, in total the procedure takes time

2^{2^{o(\textup{{pw}}(I_{\triangle})+\mathcal{O}(1))}}\cdot\lvert I_{\triangle}\rvert^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\leq 2^{2^{o(\log m)}}\cdot(n+m)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\leq 2^{o(n+m)}.

This gives a contradiction, as the ETH
(
[
36
]
combined with the Sparsification Lemma
[
37
]
)
implies that
3
-SAT cannot be solved in

2^{o(n+m)}
time
[
17
, Theorem 14.4]
.
∎

10.1
Lower Bound for
Triangle
-HitPack

Before proving
Lemma

10.1
,
i.e., the lower bound for
Triangle
-HitPack
,
we first provide some intuition for the construction.
Let
\varphi
be the 3-SAT formula.
The graph of the instance
I
of
Triangle
-HitPack
contains
a vertex set
Z
(of size
\mathcal{O}(\log m)
)
that separates the graph into two halves,
referred to as the left and right half.
We use the vertices in
Z
to represent (the
identifier
of) some clause.
The right half encodes the assignment to the variables of
\varphi

by deleting certain vertices,
while the left half is used to select a clause
for which we want to check if it is satisfied by the assignment.
Then each clause will correspond to some maximal triangle packing in the constructed graph.

In order to ensure that each clause is satisfied, we want to
rule out
the possibility that all literals of some clause are

unsatisfied
. This is done in the following way:
If the set of vertex deletions in the right half of the graph
specifies an assignment of variables that leaves all literals of some clause unsatisfied,
then the maximal triangle packing (after the deletions) that corresponds to that particular clause will have size at least
\ell
.
This way a variable assignment is not a solution if and only if
the corresponding deletion set is also not a solution
(as it allows too large of a packing).

To ensure that three unsatisfied literals of some clause yield a large triangle packing, we construct gadgets on the right half
that contribute a large number of triangles to the packing
whenever the literal is not satisfied by the clause.
Conversely, if the clause is satisfied by the literal,
the gadget contributes only a small number of triangles to the packing.
In terms of quantifiers and negations, we will check that
for all
clauses it holds that it is
not
the case that they contain three
unsatisfied
literals (or equivalently that each clause has at least one satisfied literal).

Proof of
Lemma

10.1
.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}
be the given 3-SAT formula with
n^{\prime}
variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime}}

and
m^{\prime}
clauses.

Preprocessing the Formula.

We assume in the following that no clause of
\varphi^{\prime}
contains the same literal multiple times.
Moreover, there is no clause that contains only one literal.
Otherwise we could greedily set the value of this literal
and simplify the formula accordingly.
Additionally, we replace clauses containing exactly two literals.
For two literals
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
,
let
C=(\lambda_{1}\lor\lambda_{2})
be such a clause of
\varphi^{\prime}

and let
y
be a fresh variable.
We replace this clause
C
by the two clauses

(\lambda_{1}\lor\lambda_{2}\lor y)\land(\lambda_{1}\lor\lambda_{2}\lor\overline{y}).

After doing this replacement for all clauses with only two literals,
let
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
be the resulting 3-SAT formula
with
n^{\prime\prime}\leq n^{\prime}+m^{\prime}
variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n^{\prime\prime}}
and
m^{\prime\prime}\leq 2m^{\prime}
clauses.
It is easy to verify that
\varphi^{\prime}
is satisfiable
if and only if
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
is satisfiable.

For a technical reason, which becomes clear later,
we modify the formula
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
further.
For each
i\in[n^{\prime\prime}]
,
we add the following four new clauses to the formula

(x_{i}\lor\overline{x}_{i}\lor x_{i+1})\land(x_{i}\lor\overline{x}_{i}\lor\overline{x}_{i+1})\land(x_{i}\lor\overline{x}_{i}\lor x_{i+2})\land(x_{i}\lor\overline{x}_{i}\lor\overline{x}_{i+2}),

where we set
x_{n^{\prime\prime}+1}=x_{1}
and
x_{n^{\prime\prime}+2}=x_{2}

to keep notation simple.

Let
\varphi
be the resulting formula.
Again, it can be easily verified that

\varphi
is satisfiable if and only if
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
is satisfiable.
By duplicating clauses, we can also achieve that
\varphi

contains exactly
2^{c}
clauses for some appropriate
c\geq 4
.
Hence, in the following we set
m=2^{c}\in\mathcal{O}(m^{\prime})

as the number of clauses of
\varphi

and
n=n^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(n^{\prime}+m^{\prime})
as the number of variables.

Gadgets.

For the construction of the gadgets we use an idea
which we refer to as a cycle of triangles.
To formalize this idea, we define the following gadget
which we later use to define the other gadgets.

Claim 10.2

(Auxiliary Gadget)
.

For all integers
r\geq 2
, there is a graph
\mathsf{TriCyc}_{r}
with
2r

distinguished vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
and
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}
such
that:

1.

This graph has exactly two maximum triangle packings

P
and
\overline{P}
of size
r
.

2.

Packing
P
covers
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}

and none of the vertices
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}
.

3.

Packing
\overline{P}
covers
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}

and none of the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
.

Proof.

Intuitively, the gadget
\mathsf{TriCyc}_{r}
consists of
2r
triangles arranged
in a cycle such that two triangles share one endpoint.

Formally, the graph
\mathsf{TriCyc}_{r}
consists of a cycle with
2r
vertices

u_{0},\dots,u_{2r-1}
where, for all
i\in[2r-1]
,

u_{i-1}
is adjacent to
u_{i}
,
u_{2r-1}
is connected to
u_{0}
,
and no other edges exist between these vertices.
Moreover, there are vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
and
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}

such that, for all
i\in[r]
,

v_{i}
is connected to
u_{2i-2}
and
u_{2i-1}
,
and
\overline{v}_{i}
is connected to
u_{2i-1}
and
u_{2i\bmod 2r}
.

We define the two packings
P
and
\overline{P}
as follows:

P\coloneqq\{(u_{2i-1},v_{i},u_{2i\bmod 2r})\mid i\in[r]\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{P}\coloneqq\{(u_{2i-2},\overline{v}_{i},u_{2i-1})\mid i\in[r]\}

It is easy to check that these two sets actually define a packing
of
r
triangles with the properties (2) and (3).
It remains to show that there is no other packing of
r
(or more) triangles.
But this follows directly from the fact that
the gadget
\mathsf{TriCyc}_{r}
contains only triangles with vertex set

\{v_{i},u_{2i-2},u_{2i-1}\}
or
\{\overline{v}_{i},u_{2i-1},u_{2i\bmod 2r}\}

for some
i\in[r]
,
since we assumed that
r\geq 2
.
Hence, whenever
r
(or more) triangles are contained in a packing
that is different from
\overline{P}
and
P
,
then all of these triangles cannot be pairwise vertex disjoint
as the neighboring triangles intersect.
∎

Based on this gadget
\mathsf{TriCyc}
,
we define the following gadget, which we use to “generate” the
clause number
(see
Figure

10.1
).
Although this gadget is just a special case of the gadget
\mathsf{TriCyc}
,
we provide it with a separate name to keep notation simple in the later proof.

Claim 10.3

(Selector Gadget)
.

For all integers
r\geq 2
,
there is a graph
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}

with
2r
distinguished vertices

v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
and
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}\in V

such that

1.

there are exactly two packings of
3r
triangles
denoted by
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
,

2.

P_{0}
covers
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}

and none of the vertices
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}
,

3.

P_{1}
covers
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}

and none of the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
,

4.

there is no packing of more than
3r
triangles,
and

Proof of Claim .

We use the graph
\mathsf{TriCyc}_{3r}
as the graph
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}

but only pick a third of the distinguished vertices of
\mathsf{TriCyc}{3r}

as the distinguished vertices of
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}

(see
10.2
).
Let
v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{3r}
and
\overline{v}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}^{\prime}_{3r}

be the distinguished vertices of
\mathsf{UniVar}_{3r}
.
For all
i\in[r]
, we choose
v_{i}\coloneqq v^{\prime}_{3i}

and
\overline{v}_{i}\coloneqq\overline{v}^{\prime}_{3i-2}

as the distinguished vertices of
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}
.
The properties of the gadget
follow immediately from
10.3
.
∎

\overline{v}_{1}
v_{1}
\overline{v}_{2}
v_{2}
\overline{v}_{3}
v_{3}

v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
v_{4}

Figure 10.1
:
Left figure presents a
\mathsf{Sel}_{4}
gadget.
The highlighted triangles depict the triangle packing
P_{\mathsf{true}}
.
Right figure presents
\mathsf{Lit}_{4}
along with the
unique maximum triangle packing
P_{\mathsf{false}}
.

With the construction of
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}
at hand,
we have everything ready to define the gadgets for encoding the literals.
For this gadget the size of the two largest solutions differs (see
Figure

10.1
).

Claim 10.4

(Literal Gadget)
.

For all integers
r\geq 4
,
there is a graph
\mathsf{Lit}_{r}

with
r
distinguished vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}\in V

such that

1.

there is a packing
P_{\mathsf{true}}
of
3r-1
triangles
that covers
none
of
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
,

2.

there is exactly one packing
P_{\mathsf{false}}
of
3r
triangles
that covers
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
, and

3.

there is no triangle packing with more than
3r
triangles.

Proof of Claim .

We define
\mathsf{Lit}_{r}
based on the gadget
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}
as follows.
The vertex and edge set of
\mathsf{Lit}_{r}

is the same as that of
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}

with the only modification that we identify the vertices

\overline{v}_{1}
and
\overline{v}_{3}
with each other.
We use the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}

as the distinguished vertices of
\mathsf{Lit}_{r}
.

First observe that identifying
\overline{v}_{1}
with
\overline{v}_{3}

does not create any new triangles. This directly implies the third property of the gadget.
By this choice it directly follows that
there is exactly one packing of
3r
triangles
which is precisely the packing
P_{1}
for
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}
.
Moreover, the only other packing of
3r
triangles for
\mathsf{Sel}_{r}
, i.e.,
P_{0}
,
contains
\overline{v}_{1}
and
\overline{v}_{3}
.
As we identified these two vertices,
this reduces the number of triangles by one. (There are now two choices for the packing
P_{\mathsf{true}}
.)
Hence, the first and second property of the gadget follow.
∎

Construction of the Instance.

Figure 10.2
:
An overview of the lower-bound construction. On
the left, we have the
selector gadget

that can cover either blue vertices (on the right) or
red vertices (on the left) in the green group.
The middle layer consists of
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))

groups of
6
vertices. Each group corresponds to the bit of the clause index. The right part represents the
literal gadget
,
ensuring satisfaction of the corresponding literal in the clause. Importantly,
the middle layer consists of only
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
vertices. Both the literal and
selector gadgets have a constant treewidth, resulting in a created instance
with a treewidth of
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
.

We present the overview of the construction in
Figure

10.2
.
Before defining the graph
G
,
we introduce some more notation.
For an integer
0<N\leq 2^{c}
,
we define
\textup{bin}(N)
as the binary encoding of
N-1
with
c
bits.
For all
j\in[c]
,
we denote by
\textup{bin}(N)[j]

the
j
th bit of the binary encoding of
N-1
.

With this notation,
we can now formally define the graph
G
of the instance
I
.
For all
\gamma\in[c]
,
b\in\{0,1\}
, and
p\in\{1,2,3\}
,
we create a vertex
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,b}

and define
Z\coloneqq\{z^{(\gamma)}_{p,b}\}_{\gamma,b,p}

as the set of these
6c
vertices.

The left side of the graph is defined as follows.
For all
\gamma\in[c]
,
we create a copy
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}
of the gadget
\mathsf{Sel}_{3}

and identify the copies of the vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
with the vertices

z^{(\gamma)}_{1,0},z^{(\gamma)}_{2,0},z^{(\gamma)}_{3,0}

and identify the copies of the vertices
\overline{v}_{1},\overline{v}_{2},\overline{v}_{3}

with the vertices

z^{(\gamma)}_{1,1},z^{(\gamma)}_{2,1},z^{(\gamma)}_{3,1}
.
There are no other vertices in the left side of the instance.

For the right side we let
\Lambda=\{x_{1},\overline{x}_{1},\dots,x_{n},\overline{x}_{n}\}
be the set of all literals
corresponding to the variables of
\varphi
.
Note that from the preprocessing it follows that
all of them appear in some clause.
For each
literal
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we define a set
C_{\lambda}\subseteq[m]\times\{1,2,3\}
such that
(j,p)\in C_{\lambda}
if and only if literal

\lambda
appears in the
j
th clause at position
p
.

For all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and all
(j,p)\in C_{\lambda}
,
we create a vertex
d_{\lambda}
and
introduce a copy of the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{c+1}

denoted by
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,j,p}
.
For each such gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,j,p}
and all
\gamma\in[c]
,
we identify the copy of the vertex
v_{\gamma}
from
\mathsf{Lit}_{c+1}

with the vertex
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,1-\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]}
.
The copy of the vertex
v_{c+1}
from
\mathsf{Lit}_{c+1}

is identified with
d_{\lambda}
. Intuitively, the vertex
d_{\lambda}
synchronizes the “behavior” of all gadget copies that belong to the same literal.
This completes the definition of the graph
G
.

Let
V
be the set of vertices of
G
, then the set of undeletable vertices
U\coloneqq V\setminus\{d_{\lambda}\mid\lambda\in\Lambda\}
, that is, only the
d_{\lambda}
’s can be deleted.
To conclude the construction,
we set
k\coloneqq n

as the bound on the number of vertices we are allowed to delete
and set
\ell\coloneqq 3m(3c+2)+9c+3

as the strict upper bound on the number of triangles that we are allowed to pack after the vertex deletions.
The resulting
Triangle
-HitPack
instance is
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
.

Correctness.

In the next two steps we show that the construction is correct.
We do this by handling both directions individually.

Claim 10.5
.

If
\varphi
is satisfiable,
then
I
has a solution.

Proof of Claim .

Let
\pi
be an assignment for the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}

such that
\varphi
is satisfied under
\pi
.
Based on
\pi
,
we define the solution
S
for
I
as

S\coloneqq\{d_{x_{i}}\mid i\in[n]\text{ and }\pi(x_{i})=\mathsf{true}\}\cup\{d_{\overline{x}_{i}}\mid i\in[n]\text{ and }\pi(x_{i})=\mathsf{false}\}.

By the definition of
S
, we get that
\lvert S\rvert=n=k
.
It remains to show that
S
is indeed a solution for
I
.

For the sake of a contradiction, assume that
S
is not a solution
and let
\mathcal{P}
be a maximum size packing of triangles of size at least
\ell
.
Let us analyze this packing
\mathcal{P}
.
Let
q
be the number of literal gadgets that contribute the maximum possible
3c+3
triangles to
\mathcal{P}
. (These are those gadgets that contribute a packing according to
P_{\mathsf{false}}
.)
From
10.4
it follows that this triangle packing
P_{\mathsf{false}}
covers precisely
c
vertices from
Z
(one for each
\gamma\in[c]
).
As
Z
has size
6c
it follows that
q\leq 6
, as otherwise some vertex from
Z
would be covered twice.

We strengthen this bound and argue that
q\leq 3
.
Assume otherwise and let
(\lambda_{i},j_{i},p_{i})
for
i\in[4]

be four gadgets
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{i},j_{i},p_{i}}
, each contributing
3c+3
triangles.
As
p_{i}\in[3]
,
there must be distinct
i_{1},i_{2}\in[4]
such that
p_{i_{1}}=p_{i_{2}}
.
For ease of notation assume that
i_{1}=1,i_{2}=2
and
p_{1}=1
.
By
10.4
,
the two gadgets cover
c
vertices in
\{z^{(\gamma)}_{1,b}\}_{\gamma,b}
.
As each vertex can only be covered by one triangle,
we get that all vertices in
\{z^{(\gamma)}_{1,b}\}_{\gamma,b}
are covered.
However, if that is the case, then from the properties of
10.3
,
it follows that, for all
\gamma\in[c]
, the gadget
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}

can contribute at most
8
triangles to the packing.
Now note that every literal gadget that does not contribute
3c+3
triangles to the packing
\mathcal{P}
contributes at most
3c+2
triangles, according to
10.4
.

Hence, the size of the packing
\mathcal{P}
is at most
3m(3c+2)+8c+q
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{P}
can have size at least
\ell=3m(3c+2)+9c+3

if and only if
q\geq c+3
.
Since we know that
q\leq 6
, we get
c\leq 3

which contradicts the assumption that
c\geq 4
.

Combining the two above results, we have that
q\leq 3
.
First suppose that
q\leq 2
. Then the size of
\mathcal{P}
is at most
3m(3c+2)+9c+2
, which contradicts the fact that its size should be at least
\ell
.
Thus, it remains to consider the case
q=3
. Let
\lambda_{i},j_{i}
for
i\in[3]
such that

\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{i},j_{i},p_{i}}
contributes the maximum possible
3c+3
triangles to
\mathcal{P}

for all
i\in[3]
, i.e., these literal gadgets contribute triangles according to the packing
P_{\mathsf{false}}
.
We claim that
j_{1}=j_{2}=j_{3}
.
For the sake of a contradiction, assume otherwise
and assume without loss of generality that
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
.
Thus, there is some
\gamma\in[c]
such that

\textup{bin}(j_{1})[\gamma]\neq\textup{bin}(j_{2})[\gamma]
. This means that exactly one of
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{1},j_{1},p_{1}}
or
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{2},j_{2},p_{2}}
covers a vertex in
z^{(\gamma)}_{1,0},z^{(\gamma)}_{2,0},z^{(\gamma)}_{3,0}
, whereas the other covers a vertex in
z^{(\gamma)}_{1,1},z^{(\gamma)}_{2,1},z^{(\gamma)}_{3,1}
.
Thus, by the properties from
10.3
,

\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}
contributes at most
8
triangles to the packing.
Hence, the size of the packing is bounded by
3m\cdot(3c+2)+(c-1)9+8+3

which contradicts the assumption about the size of
\mathcal{P}
,
i.e.,
\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert\geq\ell
.
Hence, we actually get that
j_{1}=j_{2}=j_{3}
.

So, for
j=j_{1}=j_{2}=j_{3}
and for all
i\in[3]
,
the gadgets
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{i},j,i}
contribute
3c+3
triangles to
\mathcal{P}
. Then

10.4
implies that
d_{\lambda_{i}}

is not deleted (since it is part of the packing
P_{\mathsf{false}}
),
i.e.,
d_{\lambda_{i}}\notin S
.
Hence, by our definition of
S
it must hold that
\pi(\lambda_{i})=\mathsf{false}

for all
i\in[3]

which contradicts the assumption that
\pi
satisfies
\varphi
.
∎

Now we prove the reverse direction.

Claim 10.6
.

If
I
has a solution,
then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

Proof of Claim .

Let
S\subseteq V\setminus U
be a solution for
I
.
The idea is to define a satisfying assignment
\pi
for
\varphi
based on
S
.
To show that this is possible, we first introduce some notation.
We define, for all
i\in[n]
,

S(i)\coloneqq\lvert\{d_{x_{i}},d_{\overline{x}_{i}}\}\cap S\rvert

as the number of vertices selected for each variable.
We first claim that, for all
i\in[n]
,
the solution
S
satisfies
S(i)<2
.

The Solution is Good.

We say that a solution
S
is
good
if for each
i\in[n]
we have
S(i)=1
. To simplify notation,
we write
S(i+n)\coloneqq S(i)

and similarly for the indices of the variables.
Now assume for contradiction that there is some
i\in[n]

such that
S(i)=2
.
Since we defined
k=n
,
this implies that there must be some
b\in[n]

such that
S(b)=0
.
Moreover, we claim that there must be some
b\in[n]

such that
S(b)=0
and, additionally,
S(b+1)+S(b+2)<4
.
Again for contradiction assume that, for all
b\in[n]

with
S(b)=0
, it holds that
S(b+1)+S(b+2)=4
.
We define the following sets

\displaystyle N_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{i\in[n]\mid S(i)=0\land S(i+1)+S(i+2)=4\}

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{i\in[n]\mid S(i)=1\}

\displaystyle N_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{i\in[n]\mid S(i)=2\land i-1,i-2\notin N_{0}\}.

Observe that each
i\in N_{0}
contributes four selected vertices to
S
,
each
i\in N_{1}
contributes one vertex,
and each
i\in N_{2}
contributes two vertices to
S
.
Moreover, this covers all vertices in
S
,
as there is no index
i\in[n]

with
S(i)=0
and
S(i+1)+S(i+2)<4
.
Hence, we get that

k=\sum_{i\in[n]}S(i)=4\lvert N_{0}\rvert+\lvert N_{1}\rvert+2\lvert N_{2}\rvert=n+\lvert N_{0}\rvert+\lvert N_{2}\rvert.

However, since we assumed that
N_{0}\neq\varnothing
,
we arrive at a contradiction as
k=n

and conclude that there is some
b\in[n]

such that
S(b)=0
and
S(b+1)+S(b+2)<4
.

In the next step we show that this also leads to a contradiction, thereby disproving
that there is some
i\in[n]
with
S(i)=2
.
We do this by constructing a large packing
which would imply that
S
is not a solution.
By our modification of
\varphi
,
for all
\lambda\in\{x_{b+1},\overline{x}_{b+1},x_{b+2},\overline{x}_{b+2}\}
,
there is a clause
(x_{b}\lor\overline{x}_{b}\lor\lambda)
in
\varphi
.
By the above reasoning we know
that there is at least one choice of
\lambda

such that
d_{\lambda}\notin S
.
Assume without loss of generality that
\lambda=x_{b+1}
,
the other cases follow analogously.
Let
q\in[m]
be the number
of the clause
(x_{b}\lor\overline{x}_{b}\lor x_{b+1})
.
We set
Q=\{(x_{b},q,1),(\overline{x}_{b},q,2),(x_{b+1},q,3)\}

to simplify notation.

For all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
(j,p)\in C_{\lambda}

where
(\lambda,j,p)\notin Q
,
we can use the packing
P_{\mathsf{true}}
from
10.4

to find a packing of
3c+2
triangles for the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,j,p}

such that no vertex from
Z\cup\{d_{\lambda}\}
is covered.
(Recall that
d_{\lambda}
corresponds to the vertex
v_{c+1}

in the respective gadget, and the vertices in
Z
are identified
with vertices of the form
v_{\gamma}
.)
This contributes
(3m-3)\cdot(3c+2)
triangles.

Also by
10.4
, for every
(\lambda,j,p)\in Q
,
we can find a packing of
3c+3
triangles for
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,j,p}

since
d_{\lambda}=v_{c+1}
is not deleted in these gadgets.
Observe that the vertex sets of these packings are disjoint
because the position
p
is different for each choice (and thus we consider different copies of the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{c+1}
).
This contributes
3(3c+3)
additional triangles to the packing.

Note that, by construction of
G
,
the triangle packings of the three gadgets

\mathsf{Lit}_{x_{b},j,1}
,
\mathsf{Lit}_{\overline{x}_{b},j,2}
, and
\mathsf{Lit}_{x_{b+1},j,3}

cover, for each
\gamma\in[c]
, exactly one of the sets

\{z_{1,0}^{\gamma},z_{2,0}^{\gamma},z_{3,0}^{\gamma}\}
or

\{z_{1,1}^{\gamma},z_{2,1}^{\gamma},z_{3,1}^{\gamma}\}
,
and they cover none of the vertices of the respective other set.
Thus, for every
\gamma\in[c]
,
we can find a packing of
9
triangles for the gadget
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}
.
This contributes
9c
additional triangles to the packing.

Hence, the final packing consists of

(3m-3)\cdot(3c+2)+3(3c+3)+9c=3m(3c+2)+9c+3

triangles which contradicts our assumption that

S
is a solution because we defined
\ell=3m\cdot(3c+2)+9c+3
.
We conclude that it cannot happen that
S(i)=2
for any
i\in[n]
.

Constructing and Verifying the Assignment.

Now we can define the assignment
\pi
for
\varphi
.
For all
i\in[n]
,
we set

\pi(x_{i})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathsf{true},&\text{if }d_{x_{i}}\in S,\\
\mathsf{false},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

By the fact that the solution
S
is good,
this assignment is well-defined
and each variable is assigned some truth-value.

Now we argue that
\pi
satisfies
\varphi
.
For the sake of a contradiction, assume that
\pi
does not satisfy
\varphi
.
Then there is at least one clause that is not satisfied by
\pi
.
Fix some arbitrary index
j\in[m]
of such an unsatisfied clause.
Next we construct a packing of at least
\ell
triangles in
G-S
.

For all
\gamma\in[c]
,
there is, by
10.3
,
some triangle packing
P^{(\gamma)}
for the gadget
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}

such that the vertices
z^{(\gamma)}_{1,\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]},z^{(\gamma)}_{2,\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]},z^{(\gamma)}_{3,\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]}

are covered by this packing (this is the packing
P_{\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]}
of the corresponding gadget).
By
10.3
,
we know that each packing
P^{(\gamma)}

contains exactly
9
triangles.

Let
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
, and
\lambda_{3}
be the literals
such that the
j
th clause is
(\lambda_{1}\lor\lambda_{2}\lor\lambda_{3})
.

•

For all literals

\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\}
, and for all
(j^{\prime},p)\in C_{\lambda}
,
we use
10.4

to obtain a triangle packing
P_{\lambda}
of size
3c+2

for the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,j,p}
.
As argued before, we can choose
P_{\lambda}=P_{\mathsf{true}}
such that
it does not cover any vertex from
Z\cup\{d_{\lambda}\}
.
Hence, we do not have to check whether the vertex
d_{\lambda}

is contained in
S
, that is, to check whether it is deleted.

•

For the literals
\lambda_{p}
with
p\in[3]
, and for all
(j^{\prime},p)\in C_{\lambda_{p}}
with
j^{\prime}\neq j
, we also select a triangle packing of size
3c+2

for the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{p},j^{\prime},p}
.
Again, this triangle packing does not use the vertex
d_{\lambda_{p}}
or any vertex from
Z
.

•

Finally, for the literals
\lambda_{p}
with
p\in[3]
, consider the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{p},j,p}
.
Recall that
\pi(\lambda_{p})=\mathsf{false}

as we assume that the clause is unsatisfied.
Hence,
d_{\lambda_{p}}\notin S
.
Thus, by
10.4
,
we can choose the triangle packing that contains
d_{\lambda_{p}}
for
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{p},j,p}
, that is, the packing of size
3c+3
that contains all of the vertices of the form
v_{\gamma}=z^{(\gamma)}_{p,1-\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]}
.

Let
P_{\lambda_{p}}
be the combined packing
for the gadgets
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{p},j^{\prime},p}
for all
(j^{\prime},p)\in C_{\lambda_{p}}
.
We first argue why the final packing is actually a packing,
that is, why the triangles from the right are disjoint
from the triangle packing for the left side of the graph.
For this fix some
\gamma\in[c]
.
We know that the gadget
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}
covers the vertices

z^{(\gamma)}_{p,\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]}
for all
p\in[3]
.
By our choice above,
for all
p\in[3]
,
the gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda_{p},j,p}

covers the vertices
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,1-\textup{bin}(j)[\gamma]}

for all
\gamma\in[c]
.
Hence, the two vertex sets are actually disjoint
which implies that the triangles from the packing are disjoint.

It remains to analyze the size of the resulting packing.
We know that
\lvert P^{(\gamma)}\rvert=9
,

\lvert P_{\lambda}\rvert=3c+2

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\}
,
and that

\lvert P_{\lambda_{p}}\rvert=(\lvert C_{\lambda_{p}}\rvert-1)\cdot(3c+2)+(3c+3)

for all
p\in[3]
.
Hence, the entire packing has size

\displaystyle\ c\cdot 9+\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\}}\lvert C_{\lambda}\rvert\cdot(3c+2)

\displaystyle+(3c+2)(\lvert C_{\lambda_{1}}\rvert-1)+(3c+2)(\lvert C_{\lambda_{2}}\rvert-1)+(3c+2)(\lvert C_{\lambda_{3}}\rvert-1)+3(3c+3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 9c+\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}(3c+2)\lvert C_{\lambda}\rvert+3

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 9c+3m\cdot(3c+2)+3

which is equal to
\ell
.
Since this is a contradiction to our assumption
that we are given a solution for the
Triangle
-HitPack
instance,
we conclude that
\pi
actually satisfies the formula
\varphi
.
∎

It remains to prove that the reduction
also satisfies the required properties about the size and the pathwidth of
G
.

Claim 10.7
.

Graph
G
has pathwidth at most
\mathcal{O}(\log m)

and the size of
G
if bounded by
\mathcal{O}(m\log m)
.

Proof of Claim .

The number of
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}
gadgets is precisely
c

and each gadget is of constant size.
Each gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,p,j}
is of size
\mathcal{O}(c)
(by
10.4
),
and there are
3m
such gadgets in total.
As we already accounted for the vertices in
Z
by the other gadgets,
the size of the graph can be bounded above by

\mathcal{O}(c)+3m\cdot\mathcal{O}(c)=\mathcal{O}(m\log m).

For the bound on the pathwidth we
first observe that if we delete
Z
,
then the graph consists of
c+3m
disjoint components.
Moreover, each such component either corresponds
to a gadget
\mathsf{Sel}^{(\gamma)}
, which has constant size,
or to a gadget
\mathsf{Lit}_{\lambda,p,j}
,
which has size
\mathcal{O}(c)=\mathcal{O}(\log m)
.
Thus, the pathwidth of the entire graph can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\log m)
.
∎

We finish the proof by recalling that
\varphi
is satisfiable
if and only if the original formula
\varphi^{\prime}
is satisfiable.
Moreover, based on our initial modifications to
\varphi
,
we get
m\in\mathcal{O}(m^{\prime})
which concludes the proof.
∎

10.2
Lower Bound for
Square
-HitPack

In this section we consider the problem
Square
-HitPack
and provide a lower bound matching the running time
of the general algorithm from
Section

7

as stated in
1.6
.
Formally, we prove
1.8
,
which we restate here for convenience.

See
1.8

Our reduction starts from 3-SAT and follows roughly the same outline
as the reduction for the general case.
However, to obtain this stronger bound with the logarithmic factor,
we have to construct an instance of
Square
-HitPack
with smaller pathwidth.
More precisely, if we can find a reduction such that the pathwidth
is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\log m/\log\log m)
,
then the claimed lower bound would follow immediately.

To understand how this can be achieved,
recall that the encoding of the clause number
was the limiting factor for the bound on the pathwidth in previous reduction.
By encoding the index of the clauses in binary,
the pathwidth of the graph became
\mathcal{O}(\log m)
.
When now considering
Square
-HitPack
,
we can actually find a better way of representing the clause number.
Recall that in the previous construction,
each gadget (independent of its type) covered vertices
from their respective half and one additional vertex from the middle only.
In the following we allow that a cycle can go from the left side
to the right half of the graph.
Then the cycle can actually be described by pair of vertices form the middle.

We use this idea as follows while again designing a graph with two halves.
The left half contains gadgets corresponding to selecting the clause
and the gadgets on the right side correspond to setting the variables
and checking whether the literals are satisfied.
For the vertices in the middle,
we now have two groups (high and low) of
\mathcal{O}(t)
vertices each
where
t
is chosen such that
t!=m

(this is for example possible by setting
t=\mathcal{O}(\log m/\log\log m)
).
The clause number is then encoded by a perfect matching
from the vertices of the high group to the vertices of the low group.
This gives us
t!
possible perfect matchings
which then clearly suffices to represent all clauses.

We define the gadgets on the left such that
they connect each vertex from the high group to a vertex from the low group
by a path of length two (i.e., a half of a
C_{4}
)
which then induces a perfect matching.
The gadgets on the right side then have to complete these cycles
by another path of length two to form a
C_{4}
.
We define these literal gadgets such that this is only possible
if the corresponding literal was not satisfied.
The interpretation is again that a large
C_{4}
-packign corresponds to an unsatisfied clause and thus, also formula
while a small packing indicates a satisfying assignment.

We note that for this construction to work
we crucially rely on the fact that we can transfer information
from one half to the other half
for which we exploit that we are packing cycles of length four.

In the following we give the formal details.

Proof of
1.8
.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}
be the given 3-SAT formula with

n^{\prime}
variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime}}
and
m^{\prime}
clauses. We preprocess the formula in
a similar way to that in
Section

10.1
, and we get a formula

\varphi
with
n
variables and
m
clauses. Let
t
be the minimum integer such
that
t!
is at least the number of clauses of the formula. Next, we duplicate
arbitrarily selected clause to guarantee that the final formula
has exactly
t!
clauses.

Gadgets.

As a next step we define two types of gadgets,
one for the literals and one for selecting the clause.

Claim 10.8

(Literal Gadget for
C_{4}
)
.

For all integers
r\geq 2
,
there is a graph
\mathsf{CLit}_{r}

with
2r
distinguished vertices

v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
and
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}\in V

such that

1.

there are exactly two packings of
C_{4}
’s of size
3r

denoted by
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
,

2.

P_{0}
covers
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}

and none of the vertices
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}
,

3.

P_{1}
covers
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{r}

and none of the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}
,

4.

there is no packing of
C_{4}
’s of size greater than
3r
.

Proof of Claim .

Intuitively, our literal gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{r}
is the graph obtained by replacing every triangle in
\mathsf{UniVar}_{r}
with a
C_{4}
.

First, we create
6r
copies of
C_{4}
, which we denote as

a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}d_{1},\dots,a_{6r}b_{6r}c_{6r}d_{6r}
. We identify
d_{i}
with

b_{i+1\bmod 6r}
for every
i\in[6r]
. For every
i\in[r]
, we name

a_{6i-5}
as
v_{i}
. Next, for every
i\in[r]
we name vertex

a_{6i-2}
as
\overline{v}_{i}
. This concludes the construction of graph

\mathsf{CLit}_{r}
.

Let
P_{0}=\{a_{2i+1}b_{2i+1}c_{2i+1}d_{2i+1}\mid i\in[3r]\}
and

P_{1}=\{a_{2i}b_{2i}c_{2i}d_{2i}\mid i\in[3r]\}

be the two distinguished packings.
We can easily verify that properties 2 and 3 of this claim are true.

Observe, that any
C_{4}
in the graph is of the form
a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}
for some

i\in[6r]
.
This means that an arbitrary
C_{4}
in any packing in the graph must cover
both vertices of
\{b_{i}d_{i}\}
for some
i\in[6r]
.
It follows that properties 1 and 4 are true as

\{b_{2i+1},d_{2i+1}\}\mid i\in[3r]\}=\{\{b_{2i},d_{2i}\}\mid i\in[3r]\}
,
every
C_{4}
in a packing must contain
b_{i},d_{i}
for some
i

and both sets have size
3r
only.
∎

We also introduce the selector gadget
\mathsf{CSel}
. Intuitively,
\mathsf{CSel}
is a
chain

of five
4
-cycles. Formally,
\mathsf{CSel}
is constructed from
5
copies of
C_{4}
, denoted as

a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}d_{1},\dots,a_{5}b_{5}c_{5}d_{5}
.
Then, we identify vertices
d_{i}
with

b_{i+1}
for every
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. This concludes the construction of

\mathsf{CSel}
.

Construction of the Instance.

Now we describe how to construct the instance of
C_{4}
-HitPack
from a processed instance of 3-SAT.
For all
\gamma\in[t]
,
g\in\{0,1\}
, and
p\in\{1,2,3\}
,
we create a vertex
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,g}

and define
Z\coloneqq\{z^{(\gamma)}_{p,g}\}_{\gamma,g,p}

as the set of vertices in the middle.

The left side of the graph is as follows.
For each pair
(i,j)\in[t]\times[t]
, we create a copy of the selector gadget, denoted by
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
.
For each gadget
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
, we connect
a_{1}
,
a_{3}
, and
a_{5}

(where
a_{i}
is the vertex in the
i
th
C_{4}
of
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
)
as follows to six vertices in
Z
:

•

We make
a_{1}
adjacent to the two vertices
z^{i}_{1,0}
and
z^{j}_{1,1}
.

•

We make
a_{3}
adjacent to the two vertices
z^{i}_{2,0}
and
z^{j}_{2,1}
.

•

We make
a_{5}
adjacent to the two vertices
z^{i}_{3,0}
and
z^{j}_{3,1}
.

The right side of the graph is as follows.
Let
\Lambda=\{x_{1},\overline{x}_{1},\dots,x_{n},\overline{x}_{n}\}
be the set of all literals
corresponding to the variables of
\varphi
.
Note that from the preprocessing it follows that
all of them appear in some clause.
For each
literal
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we define a set
\mathsf{ClauseID}_{\lambda}\subseteq[m]\times\{1,2,3\}
such that
(j,p)\in\mathsf{ClauseID}_{\lambda}
if and only if literal

\lambda
appears in the
j
th clause at position
p
.

We create two vertices
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})}
and
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{false})}
for each
i\in[n]
.
For all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and all
(j,p)\in\mathsf{ClauseID}_{\lambda}
,
we introduce a copy of the gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{t+1}

denoted by
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
.
Depending on the literal
\lambda
we consider two cases.

•

The literal
\lambda
is a positive literal,
that is,
\lambda=x_{i}
for some variable
x_{i}
:

Then, we identify
v_{t+1}
of the gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
with
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})}
.
Moreover,
we identify
\overline{v}_{t+1}
of the gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
with
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{false})}
.

•

The literal
\lambda
is a negative literal,
that is,
\lambda=\overline{x}_{i}
for some variable
x_{i}
:

Then we identify
v_{t+1}
of the gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
with
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{false})}
.
Moreover,
we identify
\overline{v}_{t+1}
of the gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
with
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})}
.

Let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
be
m
different permutation functions
of
t
elements, chosen in arbitrary order.
Note that there are
t!
different permutation functions of
t
elements and
{t!}=m
.
For each gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
and each
\gamma\in[t]
, we connect
\overline{v}_{\gamma}
and
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,0}
with an edge,
and connect
\overline{v}_{\gamma}
and
z^{(\sigma_{j}(\gamma))}_{p,1}
with an edge.
This concludes the construction of the graph
G
.

Let
V
be the set of vertices of
G
,
then the set of undeletable vertices is

U\coloneqq V\setminus\{d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})},d_{i}^{(\mathsf{false})}\mid i\in[n]\}
.
We set
k\coloneqq n
as the bound on the number of vertices we are allowed to
delete and set
\ell\coloneqq 3t^{2}+9m(t+1)+2t
.
The constructed
C_{4}
-HitPack
instance is
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
.
This concludes the description of the construction. Clearly, the runtime of the
reduction is polynomial. It remains to prove its correctness and analyze the
pathwidth of
G
.

Correctness.

Now, we show that the construction is correct.
We do this by handling both directions individually.

Claim 10.9
.

If
\varphi
is satisfiable, then
I
has a solution.

Proof of Claim .

Let
\pi
be an assignment for the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}

such that
\varphi
is satisfied under
\pi
.
Based on
\pi
,
we define the solution for
I
as

S\coloneqq\{d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})}\mid i\in[n]\text{ and }\pi(x_{i})=\mathsf{true}\}\cup\{d_{i}^{(\mathsf{false})}\mid i\in[n]\text{ and }\pi(x_{i})=\mathsf{false}\}.

It remains to show that
S
is indeed a solution for
I
, i.e., there is no
packing of
\ell
vertex-disjoint
4
-cycles in
G\setminus S
.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\mathcal{P}
is a maximum-size
packing of
4
-cycles of size at least
\ell
.
Let us analyze this packing
\mathcal{P}
.
First we check the right side of the graph
G
, which is the set of all literal gadgets.
Each of
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
can contribute at most
3(t+1)
many
4
-cycles to

\mathcal{P}
(if we ignore the
4
-cycles of
\mathcal{P}
in the middle that are incident with vertices of the literal gadgets).
There are
3m
literal gadgets and thus the literal gadgets can contribute
at most
3m(3t+3)
many
4
-cycles in total.

It remains to check the left part and the middle part of
G
. Since there
are
6t
vertices in the middle, the middle part can contribute at most
3t

many
4
-cycles to
\mathcal{P}
. We show that the middle part contributes
exactly
3t
many
4
-cycles to
\mathcal{P}
. Otherwise, the size of

\mathcal{P}
is strictly less than
3t+3(t^{2}-t)+2t+3m(3t+3)=\ell
, a
contradiction to our assumption.

Next, we argue that there are exactly
t
selector gadgets which are
incident with at least one
C_{4}
of
\mathcal{P}
contributed by the middle
part. Suppose that there are
q>t
selector gadgets which are incident with
at least one
C_{4}
of
\mathcal{P}
contributed by the middle part. Then
the size of
\mathcal{P}
is at most
3t+3(t^{2}-q)+2q+3m(3t+3)<\ell
, a
contradiction to our assumption. Thus it holds that there are exactly
t

selector gadgets which are incident with at least one
C_{4}
of
\mathcal{P}

contributed by the middle part.
Since there are
6t
vertices in the
middle part, it follows that there are exactly
t
selector gadgets each of
which is incident with three
4
-cycles of
\mathcal{P}
contributed by the
middle part. Thus the left part can contribute at most
2t+(t^{2}-t)\cdot 3=3t^{2}-t
many
4
-cycles to
\mathcal{P}
. By the construction, these
t

selector gadgets actually define a permutation of
t
elements, say

\sigma_{h}
(
h\in[m]
), according to the vertices of
Z
to which they
are adjacent.

Note that to pack
3t
many
4
-cycles in the middle part and
3m(3t+3)
many

4
-cycles on the right part (to make sure that
|\mathcal{P}|\geq\ell
),
the only possible situation is that each of the three literal gadgets
corresponding to the
h
th clause
C_{h}
are incident with
t
many
4
-cycles
of
\mathcal{P}
in the middle part. Moreover, each of the three literal
gadgets also contributes
3(t+1)
many
4
-cycles to
\mathcal{P}
. By the
definition of the solution
S
, all three literals of
C_{h}
are
set to
\mathsf{false}
by the assignment
\pi
.
Thus, the clause
C_{h}
is not
satisfied by
\pi
, contradicting that
\pi
is a satisfying assignment.

As a result, there is no packing of
\ell
vertex-disjoint
4
-cycles in
G\setminus S
and
S
is a solution to
I
.
∎

Now we prove the reverse direction.

Claim 10.10
.

If
I
has a solution, then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

Proof of Claim .

Let
S\subseteq V\setminus U
be a solution for
I
.
By a very similar analysis to that in

10.6
, we can show that the solution
S
is

good
,
that is, for no variable
x_{i}
,
both vertices
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})}
and
d_{i}^{(\mathsf{false})}
are deleted
(see
Section

10.1
for more details).
We define the assignment
\pi
for
\varphi
as follows.
For all
i\in[n]
,
we set

\pi(x_{i})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathsf{true},&\text{if }d_{i}^{(\mathsf{true})}\in S,\\
\mathsf{false},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

By the fact that the solution
S
is good,
this assignment is well-defined
and each variable is assigned some boolean value.

Now we argue that
\pi
satisfies
\varphi
. For the sake of a contradiction,
assume that
\pi
does not satisfy
\varphi
.
Hence, there is at least one
clause that is not satisfied by
\pi
and let
j\in[m]
be the index
of such an unsatisfied clause.
Next we construct a packing
\mathcal{P}
of at
least
\ell
many
4
-cycles in
G-S
.

Let
C_{j}=(\lambda_{1}\lor\lambda_{2}\lor\lambda_{3})

be this specific clause.
Since
\pi(\lambda_{p})=\mathsf{false}
for each
p\in[3]
,
vertex
v_{t+1}
is not deleted in each of
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda_{p},j,p}
by the construction.
By
10.8
,
we can add
t+1
many
4
-cycles (the packing
P_{0}
covering
v_{1},\ldots,v_{t+1}
) for the literal gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda_{p},j,p}
to
\mathcal{P}
for each
p\in[3]
.
Consider a vertex
\overline{v}_{\gamma}
(where
\gamma\in[t]
) of the literal
gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda_{p},j,p}
(where
p\in[3]
).
Recall that
\overline{v}_{\gamma}
is adjacent to
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,0}
and
z^{(\sigma_{j}(\gamma))}_{p,1}
.
Let
a_{2p-1}
be the vertex of
\mathsf{CSel}^{(\gamma,\sigma_{j}(\gamma))}
which is adjacent to both
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,0}
and
z^{(\sigma_{j}(\gamma))}_{p,1}
.
Then, we get the
C_{4}
that covers
\overline{v}_{\gamma}
,
a_{2p-1}
,
z^{(\gamma)}_{p,0}
and
z^{(\sigma_{j}(\gamma))}_{p,1}
.
Thus we get a packing
\mathcal{P}_{\text{mid}}
of
3t
many
4
-cycles of
this form and by our construction these cycles are vertex-disjoint.
We add all of the
4
-cycles in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{mid}}
to
\mathcal{P}
.

For any literal gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j^{\prime},p}
such that
j^{\prime}\neq j
,
by
10.8
, we can add
t+1
many
4
-cycles to
\mathcal{P}

as only one vertex of
v_{t+1}
and
\overline{v}_{t+1}
is deleted in the gadget.
For a selector gadget
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
, note that it is incident with either
three or none of the
4
-cycles of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{mid}}
by our construction.
If it is incident with three
4
-cycles of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{mid}}
,
then we add two
4
-cycles of
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
which does not cover any vertex
of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{mid}}
to
\mathcal{P}
.
Otherwise we add three
4
-cycles of
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
to
\mathcal{P}
.

We can verify that the
4
-cycles in
\mathcal{P}
are pairwise vertex-disjoint.
The size of
\mathcal{P}
is
3m(3t+3)+3t+2t+3(t^{2}-t)=\ell
.
This contradicts the fact that
S
is a solution for
I
.
Thus we can conclude that the constructed assignment
\pi
satisfies
\varphi
.
∎

It remains to prove that the reduction also satisfies the required properties about the size and the pathwidth of
G
.

Claim 10.11
.

Graph
G
has pathwidth at most
\mathcal{O}(t)
.

Proof of Claim .

Observe that if we delete
Z
,
then the graph consists of
t^{2}+3m
disjoint components.
Moreover, each such component either corresponds
to a gadget
\mathsf{CSel}^{(i,j)}
, which has constant size,
or to a gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{\lambda,j,p}
,
which has size
\mathcal{O}(t)=\mathcal{O}(t)
.
Thus, the pathwidth of the entire graph can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(t)
.
∎

Recall that
{t!}=m
, which means that
m=2^{\mathcal{O}(t\log{t})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(\textup{{pw}}\log{\textup{{pw}}})}
.
Observe that this concludes the proof of
1.8
because a
2^{2^{o(\textup{{pw}}\log{\textup{{pw}}})}}\cdot|V(G)|^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
algorithm for
C_{4}
-HitPack
would yield
a

2^{o(m)}
algorithm for 3-SAT with
m
clauses.
∎

10.3
Lower Bound for
Cycle
-HitPack

In this section we prove
1.3
, which we restate for convenience.

See
1.3

We note that this result does not follow from the statement of

1.8
although a
C_{4}
is obviously a cycle.
The main reason is
that there might be a large cycle packing despite the fact that there is no
large
C_{4}
packing (e.g., the graph itself is a long cycle).

However,
in the following we argue
that our reduction from
1.8

was already stated such that it works for
Cycle
-HitPack
.
The crucial piece is the following lemma.

Lemma 10.12
.

Let
I=(G,U,k,\ell)
be the
C_{4}
-HitPack instance
from
1.8
.
Let
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
be a set of vertices
and
\mathcal{P}
be an arbitrary cycle-packing for
G-S

containing at least one cycle that is not a
C_{4}
.
Then, there is a
C_{4}
-packing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for
G-S

such that
\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\rvert\geq\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert
.

Proof.

Recall that the constructed graph is based on two different types of gadgets.
The gadgets of the form
\mathsf{CSel}
were used to select some clause number and the
gadgets of the form
\mathsf{CLit}_{t+1}
encoded the literals appearing in the formula.

Consider some cycle
C
of length at least
5
in the packing
\mathcal{P}
.
This cycle
C
cannot be contained in the gadget
\mathsf{CSel}

since such a gadget does not contain any other cycles
besides the five
4
-cycles which are arranged as a path by construction.
Suppose
C

is entirely contained in some gadget
\mathsf{CLit}_{t+1}
. Let us denote
this gadget by
F
for ease of notation.
Then, by the structure of these gadgets,
C
intersects all

4
-cycles in
F
.
In particular, there is no other cycle in
\mathcal{P}
that is
entirely contained in
F
. Also, as a
non
-distinguished vertex of
F
is
not part of any edge outside of
F
, such a vertex cannot be contained in any
cycle of
\mathcal{P}
, other than possibly
C
.
Hence, removing the cycle
C
and then
adding some maximal packing of
C_{4}
in
F
that does
not
contain any
distinguished vertex gives another feasible packing of size at least that of

\mathcal{P}
.

Now consider some cycle
C
of length at least
5
in the packing
\mathcal{P}
that
is
not
entirely contained in some gadget. In this case we know that

C
enters some gadget
F
at some distinguished vertex and leaves
F
at
some other distinguished vertex. However, from the construction of the
gadgets it is straightforward to see that this path that is the intersection
of
C
with the gadget
F
goes through at least two
C_{4}
’s in
F
all of
whose vertices are non-distinguished vertices.
Therefore, removing
C
from the packing
\mathcal{P}
and adding one of these

C_{4}
instead gives another feasible packing of size at least that of

\mathcal{P}
(and fewer cycles of length at least
5
).
∎

Now we can directly prove
1.3
.
See
1.3

Proof of
1.3
.

It clearly suffices to prove that the construction
gives a reduction from 3-SAT to
Cycle
-HitPack
with the properties from
Lemma

10.1
.
Then, the result follows by the same arguments as
1.9
.

Since we did not modify our construction,
it suffices to prove the first property,
that is, the 3-SAT formula
\varphi
and the
Cycle
-HitPack
instance
I

are equivalent.
If
\varphi
is satisfiable,
we know that
I
has a solution for
Triangle
-HitPack
,
that is, after removing at most
k
vertices
there is no large packing of
C_{4}
.

Lemma

10.12
implies
that there is also no large packing of cycles
as otherwise there would also be a large packing of
C_{4}
.

For the converse direction assume that at most
k
vertices of
I

can be deleted such that there is no large cycle-packing.
Since every
C_{4}
-packing is also a cycle-packing,
this implies that there is no large
C_{4}
packing.
Hence, formula
\varphi
must be satisfiable.
∎
